  // Restore the saved color scheme preference, or
  // enact the browser preference if "automatic", 
  // without expecting DOM load to have completed.
  //
  // Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }
  // Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
  initializeReadingPreferences();

Irrationality exponents of logarithmic numbers

Values, quotients, and graded approximation


Gregory V. Chudnovsky
††thanks: Developed with OpenAI ChatGPT, including mathematical exposition and formalization assistance. The formalization scope and cited classical inputs are stated in the appendix.
October 16, 2026 — Lean statement review through Checkpoint136

Abstract
    
We establish degree-dependent irrationality-exponent bounds for logarithms
at algebraic arguments and sharper bounds when conjugate logarithms match.
A two-torus construction gives an upper bound of three for ratios of
logarithms of multiplicatively independent positive rational numbers.
We also treat normalized quadratic logarithms and circular logarithms.
The rational-logarithm and rational-power exponent-two cases were treated
earlier by Liu, Jiang and Zhang; we retain them within the broader
framework and its Lean verification. Starting from the interpolation and
determinant method in OpenAI’s September 24, 2026 preprint on the
irrationality exponent of π\pi, we track the arithmetic cost of algebraic
embeddings and the effects of matching conjugate logarithms.
A second, graded Padé construction
gives near-optimal measures for powers and bounded-degree polynomial
values at sufficiently small logarithmic arguments, with estimates uniform
in both coefficient height and rational denominator. We state the branch,
independence, parameter-order, and approximation-field hypotheses explicitly.
The Lean supplement has reviewed endpoint correspondences for all 62
numbered results: 53 direct checked endpoints or compositions, and nine
applications with named classical theorem inputs. Native proofs of those
inputs and separate formalizations of every unnumbered assertion are not
supplied. Appendix A gives the exact declarations,
hypotheses, and verification evidence.
  


Version note. The October 16, 2026 manuscript date
identifies the mathematical edition reviewed through Checkpoint136.
Attribution and literature comparisons were updated on October 20, 2026;
see Section 2.1 and the publication record in
Appendix A.


Contribution and source attribution.


The impetus for this investigation was OpenAI’s preprint
The irrationality exponent of π\pi is 22 [21].
It supplied the separated-weight determinant method and substantial
formal infrastructure. Section 2.1 compares the
present paper with Liu, Jiang and Zhang’s earlier logarithmic preprint
[23]. Our emphasis is on number-field norms, matching
embeddings, two-torus quotient bounds, graded applications, and precise
formal verification. The investigation, proof development, formalization,
and exposition were carried out by Gregory V. Chudnovsky with OpenAI
ChatGPT; the appendix distinguishes written proofs from checked endpoints.

1 Results and organization


This paper brings together the logarithmic investigations of
7–16 October 2026, including the expanded proofs and verification checkpoints through136.
Its purpose is to give one connected proof, with the
arithmetic and analytic estimates visible at the points where they are
used. The common question is how closely a fixed logarithmic number can
be approximated by rational numbers. We write μ⁡(x)\mu(x) for the ordinary
irrationality exponent of an irrational real number xx. The definition,
including the convention on reduced denominators, is given in
Section 3.


There are two main constructions. Moving-center interpolation gives
results at a fixed algebraic argument. Graded Padé approximation gives
uniform results for powers and polynomial targets when the argument is
sufficiently small. Their hypotheses and conclusions differ. In
particular, an exponent-two theorem for a logarithm does not imply the
same theorem for its quotient with another logarithm or for an
unrestricted higher power.







Target and hypotheses



Conclusion



Proof






Positive algebraic a≠1a\neq 1



2≤μ(loga)≤2minN≥1[ℚ(aN):ℚ]2\leq\mu(\log a)\leq 2\min_{N\geq 1}[\mathbb{Q}(a^{N}):\mathbb{Q}].
In particular the exponent is two if a positive power of aa is rational.



Theorem 4.1; Section 8.




Nonzero fixed logarithm λ\lambda of an algebraic a≠0a\neq 0



2≤μG(λ)≤2minN≥1[ℚ(i,aN):ℚ(i)]2\leq\mu_{G}(\lambda)\leq 2\min_{N\geq 1}[\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i},a^{N}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i})],
for approximation over ℚ⁡(i)\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) using denominator modulus.



Theorem 4.2.




Real x≠0x\neq 0, α,c∈E×\alpha,c\in E^{\times}; d=[E:ℚ]d=[E:\mathbb{Q}] and
eσ⁡(c)​x=σ⁡(α)e^{\sigma(c)x}=\sigma(\alpha) at ss embeddings



2≤μ⁡(x)≤2​d/s2\leq\mu(x)\leq 2d/s. Exact matching at every embedding gives
μ⁡(x)=2\mu(x)=2.



Theorem 10.1.

D>0D>0 rational nonsquare



μ⁡(log⁡α/D)=2\mu(\log\alpha/\sqrt{D})=2.



Corollary 10.2.




Nonzero rational rr; positive rational DD



μ⁡(arctan⁡r)=2\mu(\arctan r)=2 and μ⁡(π/D)=2\mu(\pi/\sqrt{D})=2.



Corollaries 4.3 and 10.3.




Multiplicatively independent positive rational a,ba,b



2≤μ⁡(log⁡a/log⁡b)≤32\leq\mu(\log a/\log b)\leq 3.

Ratios with compatible conjugate logarithms or algebraic slopes



Bounds 3​d/s3d/s under the exact branch and embedding conditions stated
in the theorems; these include normalized quadratic and mixed circular
examples.

Fixed integer n≥1n\geq 1 and ε>0\varepsilon>0



μ⁡(logn⁡(1+1/b))≤2+ε\mu(\log^{n}(1+1/b))\leq 2+\varepsilon and
μ⁡(arctann⁡(1/b))≤2+ε\mu(\arctan^{n}(1/b))\leq 2+\varepsilon for all sufficiently large
integers bb, depending on n,εn,\varepsilon.

hd​(z)=∑j≥0dj​z2​j+1/(2​j+1)h_{d}(z)=\sum_{j\geq 0}d^{j}z^{2j+1}/(2j+1)



For A>1+1/εA>1+1/\varepsilon, a threshold
b≥C⁡(n,ε,A)​|d|Ab\geq C(n,\varepsilon,A)|d|^{A} gives
μ⁡(P⁡(hd​(1/b)))≤2+ε\mu(P(h_{d}(1/b)))\leq 2+\varepsilon, uniformly in the choice of PP.



Theorem 17.1.




Varying integer polynomial coefficients and rational denominator



Joint lower bounds record both coefficient height and denominator;
their constants are uniform at a fixed evaluation point.



Theorem 17.3, Corollary 17.4, and
Theorem 18.1.





Some concrete comparisons help fix the scope. We obtain




μ⁡(log⁡2)=2,μ⁡(log⁡φ5)=2,2≤μ⁡(log⁡2log⁡3)≤3,φ=1+52.\mu(\log 2)=2,\qquad\mu\!\left(\frac{\log\varphi}{\sqrt{5}}\right)=2,\qquad 2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\log 2}{\log 3}\right)\leq 3,\quad\varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}.


The unnormalized log⁡φ\log\varphi is covered here only by
2≤μ⁡(log⁡φ)≤42\leq\mu(\log\varphi)\leq 4. The upper bound three for
log⁡2/log⁡3\log 2/\log 3 is not an equality assertion. For the small-argument
theorems, the threshold depends on the requested error in the exponent;
they do not prove exponent exactly two for every one of the fixed
higher-power targets.


The moving-center and two-torus geometric arguments are proved in the
body of the paper. The graded argument uses the explicitly stated
differential-system rank theorem of Fischler and Rivoal, together with
the classical transcendence theorems needed to check its hypotheses.
These are written mathematical proofs. Their formalization status is a
separate statement. The 11 October 2026 evening release supplies completed
Lean endpoints for the real and Gaussian single-logarithm bounds, the
single-logarithm 2​d/s2d/s matching bound, natural-parameter normalized
quadratic logarithms, restricted powers, and the rational-base quotient
bound 2≤μ≤32\leq\mu\leq 3. It also proves the moving-center interpolation
needed by these applications and uniform rational-slope two-torus
interpolation. Later steps supply ordinary arctangent and homogeneous
two-logarithm measures, real and Gaussian fractional-linear invariance,
the arbitrary rational-parameter quadratic norm and linear-closure
interfaces, and the analytic quadratic logarithmic family. They also
verify the invariant-field degree bound, reciprocal-trace formulas,
the explicit quartic example, and the signed character-product descent.
The later character increments complete the normalized character-sum
exponent theorem, including its actual field realization, norm and
logarithmic identity. Subsequent checkpoints complete the matched and
normalized ratio endpoints, the literal graded application recipes, the
sharp bivariate collision estimate, and bounded-fiber occupancy geometry.
The converse matching classification is checked relative to
Gel’fond–Schneider; the general short-jet rank and geometric multiplicity
applications retain the explicitly listed standard foundations.
Appendix A gives a reviewed correspondence for each
of the 62 numbered results, with the exact scope of these inputs. The
written proofs remain distinct from the kernel-checked conditional
applications and from native proofs of their classical foundations.


The paper is organized so that the fixed-value arguments can be read
before the graded construction. The preceding literature is discussed
next. After the conventions and elementary invariances, we prove the
moving-center theorem, construct its determinant, and compare the
arithmetic and analytic bounds. We then retain several embeddings in
that comparison and pass to the two-torus construction for ratios.
Detailed verification companions explain the quadratic analytic family
and the invariant-field, reciprocal, and character-product arguments.
The final mathematical sections develop the graded rank and coefficient
height estimates from their linear systems. This project is independent
of the separate lemniscate-period paper and the Math 2 project; it does
not resume the investigation of powers of π\pi.


1.1 The exponent-two and exponent-three conclusions


The results collected below are mathematical theorems with the hypotheses
and proofs given in the cited sections. A statement that its Lean
formalization is incomplete concerns the additional machine verification
of that proof. It does not designate the mathematical statement as a
conjecture. Conversely, a completed Lean result is identified by its exact
declaration and assumptions; compilation of related lemmas is not used as
a substitute for a formalized endpoint.


The following guide isolates the conclusions in the range
2≤μ≤32\leq\mu\leq 3. Degree-dependent theorems enter this range only under
the additional numerical condition displayed. All targets here are real,
and all exponents concern ordinary rational approximation.







Theorem family



Conclusion



Lean status

Theorem 4.1



μ⁡(log⁡a)=2\mu(\log a)=2 for a≠1a\neq 1

For the last row the argument must satisfy the stated threshold for the
chosen degree and ε\varepsilon. One cannot vary ε\varepsilon while
holding an arbitrary argument fixed and conclude exponent two.
The joint-measure theorem in Section 18 has its own
coefficient-height quantifier; its displayed quotient consequence
μ≤3+ρ\mu\leq 3+\rho is not an additional exponent-three endpoint.
The independent rational-base theorem already supplies exponent at most
three for those particular rational logarithm ratios.


The first six families are developed through interpolation, the
arithmetic norm of one selected minor, its analytic estimate, and the
parameter comparison. The small-argument family additionally uses the
precisely stated differential-system rank theorem in
Section 15. Rational fractional-linear transformations
preserve the resulting irrationality exponents when their determinant is
nonzero; their separate completed Lean statements are listed in
Appendix A.




2 Introduction and relation to earlier work


Logarithms give a natural testing ground for quantitative Diophantine
approximation. Their transcendence, differential equations and analytic
continuation are well understood, yet these facts do not by themselves
determine the quality of their rational approximations. For an irrational
real number xx, the assertion μ⁡(x)=2\mu(x)=2 says that, for every
ε>0\varepsilon>0, the inequality




0<|x−pq|<q−2−ε0<\left|x-\frac{p}{q}\right|<q^{-2-\varepsilon}


has only finitely many solutions in reduced rational numbers p/qp/q.
The dependence of the exceptional denominator threshold on
ε\varepsilon is part of the statement. This assertion does not require
a positive constant cc for which |x−p/q|≥c/q2|x-p/q|\geq c/q^{2} holds for all
rationals.


The present paper brings together several logarithmic approximation
problems that share constructions but require different arithmetic
arguments: individual logarithms, quotients of two logarithms,
coefficient-uniform linear forms, and powers at sufficiently small
rational arguments. We state their hypotheses separately. In particular,
a conclusion for log⁡α\log\alpha is not automatically a conclusion for
log⁡α/log⁡β\log\alpha/\log\beta, and a family of near-optimal bounds at arguments
tending to 11 is not an exponent-two theorem at a fixed argument.
The point of collecting the results is to explain the common machinery
once and then make each additional argument visible.


2.1 The earlier preprint of Liu, Jiang and Zhang


Liu, Jiang and Zhang’s Irrationality exponents of logarithms of
positive rational numbers [23] first appeared on
arXiv on October 7, 2026; the version compared here is v2, dated
October 8. Its Theorem 1 treats μ⁡(log⁡r)=2\mu(\log r)=2 for positive rational
r≠1r\neq 1; Corollaries 6–7 include nonzero rational logarithmic sums,
rational affine changes, and positive arguments with a rational power.
Its Theorems 2 and 9 treat distinct multiplicative centers and bounded
fiber occupancy. Both papers use separated logarithmic weights and
arithmetic–analytic determinant comparisons. No novelty is claimed here
for those overlapping conclusions or for bounded-fiber interpolation.


Their Section 8 explicitly leaves general algebraic conjugate-determinant
estimates untreated. Our number-field and matching-embedding results,
two-torus quotient bounds, and graded applications have separate proofs
and hypotheses below. This comparison identifies scope relative to that
preprint, rather than asserting priority against all preceding work.
The accompanying Lean supplement supplies a further verification
contribution with the exact limits stated in Appendix A.
This literature comparison was added on October 20, 2026; the retained
manuscript date is not a claim of earlier public appearance.



2.2 Classical logarithmic approximation


Mahler’s work on logarithms of algebraic numbers
[1] constructs algebraic-function approximations to the
logarithm and derives quantitative estimates after specialization at
algebraic arguments. Baker subsequently obtained explicit rational
approximation estimates for logarithms of certain rational numbers
[2]. These works illustrate a recurring principle: an
approximation construction must combine small analytic remainders with
arithmetic control of its coefficients. A high order of vanishing, on
its own, does not provide the required numerical estimate.


The use of Legendre polynomials and related Padé constructions was
developed by Alladi and Robinson [6].
Simultaneous logarithmic Padé approximants give access to several
logarithms at once; Rhin and Toffin [10] used this
approach for rational arguments close to 11. Rhin’s subsequent work
[11] shows how alternative polynomial constructions and
careful arithmetic estimates improve bounds obtained by direct
approximation. Rukhadze’s treatment of log⁡2\log 2 [12]
and Marcovecchio’s use of the Rhin–Viola method
[15] are further landmarks. For example,
Marcovecchio obtained the upper bound 3.574553913.57455391 for
μ⁡(log⁡2)\mu(\log 2). We cite this as a specific established comparison,
without claiming here to give a complete table of numerical records.
The work of Bondareva, Luchin and Salikhov on log⁡3\log 3
[18] supplies another example of the
importance of optimizing the polynomial and arithmetic parts together.


There is also an essential distinction between approximation by
rationals and approximation by elements of a fixed number field.
Marcovecchio and Viola [16] study
number-field irrationality and nonquadraticity measures for several
algebraic logarithms, using complex integrals and saddle-point
estimates. Hirata-Kohno, Muroi and Washio [19] treat shifted
logarithms over number fields through Padé approximants and effective
Poincaré–Perron arguments, with estimates at the different
archimedean places. These are especially relevant precedents when
conjugate embeddings enter an arithmetic norm calculation. Their
notions of height and approximation field must, however, be retained
when comparing the resulting exponents with the ordinary rational
exponent used here.


Kühne [17] gives a systematic construction of
logarithmic Padé approximants using Siegel’s lemma in a proof of
transcendence. This complements the explicit integral constructions:
one may obtain useful approximants either from a formula or from a
linear-algebraic existence argument. In either case the numerical
irrationality exponent requires a further comparison of coefficient
growth, denominators, nonvanishing and analytic decay.



2.3 Two logarithms and the role of qualitative independence


The theory of linear forms in logarithms provides both qualitative
independence statements and quantitative lower bounds. Baker’s series
[3, 4] is fundamental in this respect;
see also the systematic account in [5, Chapters 1–2].
For positive algebraic numbers that are multiplicatively independent,
their real logarithms are linearly independent over ℚ\mathbb{Q};
Baker’s theorem strengthens this to linear independence over
ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}. His transcendence theorem for nonzero
algebraic linear forms in logarithms also gives the corresponding
independence after adjoining 11. We use these statements only with
the specified algebraic arguments and fixed logarithms. They do not
assert algebraic independence of arbitrary products or powers of
several logarithms.


The distinction matters for a quotient. If α,β>0\alpha,\beta>0,
β≠1\beta\neq 1, and α,β\alpha,\beta are multiplicatively dependent,
then log⁡α/log⁡β\log\alpha/\log\beta is rational. In the independent case,
rational approximation to the quotient is equivalent to smallness of
a homogeneous linear form:




|log⁡αlog⁡β−pq|=|q​log⁡α−p​log⁡β|q​|log⁡β|.\left|\frac{\log\alpha}{\log\beta}-\frac{p}{q}\right|=\frac{|q\log\alpha-p\log\beta|}{q|\log\beta|}.


For approximations in a fixed bounded neighborhood of the quotient,
max⁡{|p|,q}\max\{|p|,q\} is comparable to qq. Thus a uniform lower bound
of order H−κH^{-\kappa} for the numerator yields an irrationality
bound of order q−κ−1q^{-\kappa-1}, with constants absorbed by an
arbitrarily small exponent margin. This elementary conversion explains
why the normalizations of linear-form measures and irrationality
exponents must not be interchanged.


A concrete benchmark is furnished by
[18, Theorem 1]. With
h1,h2,h∈ℤh_{1},h_{2},h\in\mathbb{Z} and H=max⁡{|h1|,|h2|}H=\max\{|h_{1}|,|h_{2}|\},
that theorem gives, for all sufficiently large HH,




|h1​log⁡2+h2​log⁡3+h|>H−4.116201.|h_{1}\log 2+h_{2}\log 3+h|>H^{-4.116201}.


Its stated Corollary 1 is μ⁡(log⁡3)≤5.116201\mu(\log 3)\leq 5.116201.
Taking h=0h=0 and applying the preceding conversion also gives
μ⁡(log⁡2/log⁡3)≤5.116201\mu(\log 2/\log 3)\leq 5.116201.
This last implication is the comparison made here; it does not
identify the inhomogeneous three-term estimate with the homogeneous
two-term problem or assert that the quoted bound is the latest record.


Interpolation determinants furnish an important approach to quantitative
two-logarithm estimates; Laurent’s work [13] is a
direct methodological predecessor. In the constructions below,
qualitative independence is used to exclude a rank obstruction when
the applicable rank theorem requires it. The eventual exponent still
comes from separately proved arithmetic and analytic estimates.
In particular, neither Baker’s qualitative theorem nor maximal rank
alone supplies an optimal irrationality exponent.



2.4 Graded Padé approximation and its precise scope


Two papers of 1983 are relevant and should be distinguished.
G. V. Chudnovsky’s paper on linear forms in exponentials and binomials
[7] discusses quantitative arithmetic approximation;
its final page includes the small-argument logarithmic-power
phenomenon revisited here. The paper of D. V. Chudnovsky and
G. V. Chudnovsky [8] treats functional
Padé approximation to solutions of linear differential equations,
using Wronskians and graded subrings of Picard–Vessiot extensions.
Functional approximation and numerical specialization are related,
but the passage between them has additional arithmetic requirements.


In 1984, G. V. Chudnovsky [9] announced optimal
irrationality and linear-independence statements for values of
EE-functions at rational points, based on graded Padé
approximation. Fischler and Rivoal [20] explain the
zero-estimate gap in the general argument and the subsequent
conditional results of Zudilin. They prove the exponent-two result
for irrational rational-point values of EE-functions with rational
Taylor coefficients, removing the additional independence assumptions
through a generalized Shidlovskii lemma.


The present logarithmic applications use their general
rational-differential-system rank results, with all nondegeneracy
hypotheses checked at the point of use. Their EE-function
irrationality theorem is not being applied to log⁡(1+z)\log(1+z), which
has a finite singularity and is not an EE-function. In particular,
the linear independence over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}} of successive
powers of one transcendental logarithm is a different input from the algebraic-linear
independence needed for a system of several logarithms. The rank
statements, coefficient estimates and specialization arguments are
therefore presented separately.


The small-argument conclusion has the quantifier order




∀n≥1∀ε>0∃b0​(n,ε)∀b≥b0​(n,ε).\forall n\geq 1\quad\forall\varepsilon>0\quad\exists b_{0}(n,\varepsilon)\quad\forall b\geq b_{0}(n,\varepsilon).


Here the evaluation argument changes with the allowed excess over
22. Holding bb fixed and then letting ε\varepsilon tend to zero
would be a different statement. Our treatment of this phenomenon is
an explicit reconstruction and development of the earlier method,
not a claim that the underlying small-argument phenomenon is new.



2.5 The impetus from OpenAI’s work


The immediate impetus for the present investigation was OpenAI’s
preprint The irrationality exponent of π\pi is 22, dated
September 24, 2026 [21]. Its proof combines weighted
interpolation along logarithmic curves with a nonzero determinant.
After denominators are cleared, arithmetic gives a lower bound for
that determinant. Analytic expansion gives an incompatible upper
bound: either repeated Taylor degrees force a collision saving, or
large transverse indices supply powers of small approximation errors.
The interpolation thresholds are independent of the subsequently
chosen centers, which is crucial to the order of parameter choices.
The paper proves an eventual bound for every exponent greater than
22, without asserting an effective denominator threshold.


We explicitly acknowledge this construction as a starting point,
including the weighted simplices, separated choices of scales, and
the arithmetic–analytic determinant comparison. The overlap with
[23] is identified above. The present number-field,
matching-embedding, two-torus and graded applications require the
additional arguments given below. Each inherited
input and each additional assertion is identified where it enters
the proof. A logarithmic consequence is not attributed to the OpenAI
paper merely because the construction was inspired by it.


For reproducibility, the accompanying dependency records distinguish
the manuscript [21] from the proof repository
[22], whose recorded upstream revision is
fd4aeeb2ee4fc729c18d98444fed42fd0529eeeb.
The public Lean scope statement for the π\pi result covers the
eventual approximation inequality and the supremum characterization
of its irrationality exponent. It does not include the manuscript’s
Flint–Hills-series consequence. Likewise, formal verification of a
supporting logarithmic lemma is distinguished here from formal
verification of a complete numerical theorem.



2.6 How to read the consolidated argument


The proofs are organized around three questions. First, why is there
a nonzero determinant with the prescribed rows and columns? This is
the interpolation or rank question. Second, how small can a nonzero
determinant with those coefficients be? This is the arithmetic
question, including every conjugate factor required by a norm.
Third, how small does the assumed approximation force the same
determinant to be? This is the analytic question. The comparison is
valid only when both estimates concern the same determinant and all
constants have the declared parameter dependence.


This organization also explains the different conclusions in the
several logarithmic settings. Rational arguments, quadratic
conjugation, reciprocal matching and several logarithms change the
arithmetic cost or the information available at the other embeddings.
The common determinant mechanism does not erase those differences.
We give each numerical conclusion with its actual hypotheses and
identify conditional consequences separately. The detailed proofs
and the formalization record provide complementary checks: the prose
explains the construction and estimates, while the record specifies
which statements have been represented and checked in Lean.




3 Approximation conventions and elementary transfers


3.1 Ordinary and Gaussian irrationality exponents


For an irrational real number xx, its ordinary irrationality exponent is




μ(x)=sup{ν>0:0<|x−pq|<q−ν for infinitely many reduced pq∈ℚ,q≥2}.\mu(x)=\sup\left\{\nu>0:0<\left|x-\frac{p}{q}\right|<q^{-\nu}\text{ for infinitely many reduced }\frac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q},\ q\geq 2\right\}.

(3.1)

A fixed multiplicative constant in the approximation inequality does not
change this supremum. Indeed, if C​q−νCq^{-\nu} is an upper bound and
ν′<ν\nu^{\prime}<\nu, then C​q−ν<q−ν′Cq^{-\nu}<q^{-\nu^{\prime}} for all sufficiently large qq.
We use this observation whenever a fixed scaling or a height comparison
introduces a constant.


For a complex number z∉ℚ⁡(i)z\notin\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}), define




μG(z)=sup{ν>0:0<|z−PQ|<|Q|−ν for infinitely many reduced PQ∈ℚ(i),|Q|>1}.\mu_{G}(z)=\sup\left\{\nu>0:0<\left|z-\frac{P}{Q}\right|<|Q|^{-\nu}\text{ for infinitely many reduced }\frac{P}{Q}\in\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}),\ |Q|>1\right\}.

(3.2)

Here P,Q∈ℤ⁡[i]P,Q\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathrm{i}] are relatively prime in that Euclidean ring;
multiplication by a unit does not alter |Q||Q|. The height is |Q||Q|, not
|Q|2|Q|^{2}. This convention is essential for the numerical exponent two.
Ordinary rational approximation is used throughout for real targets.
For a genuinely nonreal target, rational numbers on the real axis cannot
approximate arbitrarily closely, so (3.2) is a
separately specified approximation problem.


Lemma 3.1 (The Dirichlet lower exponent).

Every irrational real xx has μ⁡(x)≥2\mu(x)\geq 2. Every
z∉ℚ⁡(i)z\notin\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) has μG​(z)≥2\mu_{G}(z)\geq 2.



Proof.

For the real assertion, place the N+1N+1 points
0,x,…,N​x0,x,\ldots,Nx modulo ℤ\mathbb{Z} in NN intervals of length 1/N1/N.
Subtracting two points in the same interval gives integers p,qp,q with
1≤q≤N1\leq q\leq N and 0<|q​x−p|≤1/N0<|qx-p|\leq 1/N. Consequently
|x−p/q|≤1/(N​q)≤1/q2|x-p/q|\leq 1/(Nq)\leq 1/q^{2}.
The reduced denominators obtained as N→∞N\to\infty are unbounded:
otherwise the approximants, which converge to xx and stay in a fixed
bounded interval, would lie in a finite set. That would force xx to be
rational. Reducing a fraction only decreases its denominator, so the
upper bound in terms of the reduced denominator remains valid.
Every exponent below two therefore occurs infinitely often.


For the Gaussian assertion, take the (N+1)2(N+1)^{2} points
(a+b​i)​z(a+b\mathrm{i})z modulo ℤ⁡[i]\mathbb{Z}[\mathrm{i}], 0≤a,b≤N0\leq a,b\leq N, in N2N^{2} squares
of side 1/N1/N in a fundamental square. Two points in the same box give




0<|Q​z−P|≤2/N,0<|Q|≤2​N.0<|Qz-P|\leq\sqrt{2}/N,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq\sqrt{2}N.


Thus |z−P/Q|≤2/|Q|2|z-P/Q|\leq 2/|Q|^{2}. The errors tend to zero even after
reduction, and a bounded set contains only finitely many Gaussian
fractions of bounded reduced denominator. Since z∉ℚ⁡(i)z\notin\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}),
the denominators are unbounded. The constant two can be absorbed by
any smaller exponent.
∎



Lemma 3.2 (Rational fractional-linear invariance).

Let F=ℚF=\mathbb{Q} with the usual absolute value or F=ℚ⁡(i)F=\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) with the
complex modulus. Let x∉Fx\notin F, and, in the first case, assume xx is
real. If a,b,c,d∈Fa,b,c,d\in F and a​d−b​c≠0ad-bc\neq 0, then




T⁡(x)=a​x+bc​x+d∉F,μF​(T⁡(x))=μF​(x),T(x)=\frac{ax+b}{cx+d}\notin F,\qquad\mu_{F}(T(x))=\mu_{F}(x),


where μℚ=μ\mu_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mu and μℚ⁡(i)=μG\mu_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i})}=\mu_{G}.



Proof.

Clear the fixed denominators of a,b,c,da,b,c,d, so that all four lie in
ℤ\mathbb{Z} or ℤ⁡[i]\mathbb{Z}[\mathrm{i}]. Write Δ=a​d−b​c≠0\Delta=ad-bc\neq 0. The denominator
c​x+dcx+d cannot vanish, since otherwise x∈Fx\in F. Near xx,




|T⁡(x)−T⁡(y)|=|Δ|​|x−y||c​x+d|​|c​y+d|≍|x−y|,|T(x)-T(y)|=\frac{|\Delta|\,|x-y|}{|cx+d|\,|cy+d|}\asymp|x-y|,

(3.3)

with constants depending only on T,xT,x.
For a reduced approximant P/QP/Q, the transformed numerator and denominator
are a​P+b​QaP+bQ and c​P+d​QcP+dQ. Their common divisors divide both Δ​P\Delta P
and Δ​Q\Delta Q, and hence divide Δ\Delta, because P,QP,Q are relatively
prime. Thus reduction changes the denominator by a bounded factor.
Moreover |c​P+d​Q|=|Q|​|c​P/Q+d|≍|Q||cP+dQ|=|Q|\,|cP/Q+d|\asymp|Q| as P/Q→xP/Q\to x.
The transformed reduced denominator is therefore comparable to |Q||Q|.
Equation (3.3) transfers every admissible exponent,
with an arbitrarily small loss to absorb constants. Apply the same argument
to T−1T^{-1} for equality. The inverse also shows that T⁡(x)∉FT(x)\notin F.
∎



In particular, adding a rational number, multiplying by a nonzero rational
number, and taking a reciprocal preserve the ordinary exponent. The
corresponding operations over ℚ⁡(i)\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) preserve the Gaussian exponent.
An arbitrary algebraic multiplier is not covered by this lemma; the
normalized algebraic logarithms treated later require their own proof.


Lean scope.


The fractional-linear invariance is now formalized for the extended
real exponent, including infinity, in
QuadraticPi/RealMobiusInvariance.lean, and for the Gaussian
extended-real exponent in
QuadraticPi/GaussianMobiusInvariance.lean.
For the finite logarithmic endpoints the corresponding real-valued
supremum agrees with the extended definition; no finiteness assumption
is hidden in the general invariance statement.




3.2 Qualitative independence and logarithmic branches


We use three classical facts from transcendence theory; see
[5]. Hermite–Lindemann says that the exponential of a nonzero
algebraic number is transcendental. Thus every nonzero logarithm of a
nonzero algebraic number is transcendental. The same applies to a nonzero
algebraic multiple or quotient of such a logarithm. Gelfond–Schneider
says that, for algebraic β∉{0,1}\beta\notin\{0,1\} and algebraic irrational
rr, every value exp⁡(r​λβ)\exp(r\lambda_{\beta}) with
exp⁡(λβ)=β\exp(\lambda_{\beta})=\beta is transcendental. Thus a quotient
λα/λβ\lambda_{\alpha}/\lambda_{\beta} of chosen logarithms of algebraic
numbers, with this condition on β\beta, cannot be algebraic irrational.
Baker’s theorem implies that
logarithms of algebraic numbers which are linearly independent over ℚ\mathbb{Q}
are linearly independent over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}; its version including the
constant 11 gives the numerical independence used in the graded rank
argument. These facts provide qualitative independence, not the sharp
quantitative exponents asserted later.


For positive real aa, log⁡a\log a means the real logarithm. A complex
logarithm λ\lambda always means a fixed value satisfying eλ=ae^{\lambda}=a;
no single-valued global logarithm is being asserted. Different embeddings
of a number field act on algebraic coefficients, not on a chosen
transcendental value λ\lambda. At each embedding where an analytic
estimate is applied, the matching logarithm is specified by its own
exponential identity. This is the reason for the explicit matching
hypotheses in the conjugate-transfer theorems.


If positive rational a,ba,b are multiplicatively independent, then
a,b≠1a,b\neq 1 and log⁡a/log⁡b\log a/\log b is irrational: a rational value p/qp/q
would give aq=bpa^{q}=b^{p}. The resulting quotient is not assigned exponent
two merely because the two individual logarithms have exponent two.
Its approximation problem concerns the simultaneous linear form
q​log⁡a−p​log⁡bq\log a-p\log b, and is proved by a different determinant.



3.3 The order of limiting operations


All parameter choices below are finite. In the interpolation method the
number of transverse variables is chosen first, followed by the separated
weights and their associated rational approximants. Only then is the
polynomial cutoff sent to infinity through the required multiples.
Any constant depending on the fixed algebraic numbers, embeddings, or
chosen branch is fixed before that final limit. In the graded method the
grade and evaluation point are fixed before the auxiliary polynomial
degree tends to infinity.


The small-argument conclusions have the logical form




∀n≥1​∀ε>0​∃b0​(n,ε)​∀b≥b0​(n,ε):μ⁡(logn⁡(1+1/b))≤2+ε.\forall n\geq 1\ \forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists b_{0}(n,\varepsilon)\ \forall b\geq b_{0}(n,\varepsilon):\mu\bigl(\log^{n}(1+1/b)\bigr)\leq 2+\varepsilon.


They do not exchange the quantifiers in bb and ε\varepsilon and do not
establish exponent two for every fixed power at every fixed logarithmic
argument. Uniformity in polynomial coefficients is a separate conclusion,
proved below by using one auxiliary family for all those coefficients.




4 Single logarithms: statements and proof strategy


The argument in this part adapts the separated-weight determinant method
of OpenAI’s proof of μ⁡(π)=2\mu(\pi)=2 [21]. The change from
π\pi to a logarithm is geometrically substantial: the local
multiplicative coordinates are now aja^{j}, rather than all being 11.
We prove the required interpolation statement below, with all constants
chosen in the order needed by the Diophantine argument. We also give the
arithmetic and analytic estimates in full. In particular, the collision
estimate will not be used as an unexplained black box.


For a real irrational xx, write




μ⁡(x)=sup{ν>0:0<|x−p/q|<q−ν​ for infinitely many reduced ​p/q∈ℚ}.\mu(x)=\sup\{\nu>0:0<|x-p/q|<q^{-\nu}\text{ for infinitely many reduced }p/q\in\mathbb{Q}\}.


For z∉ℚ⁡(i)z\notin\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}), define its Gaussian exponent by




μG​(z)=sup{ν>0:0<|z−P/Q|<|Q|−ν​ for infinitely many reduced ​P/Q∈ℚ⁡(i)}.\mu_{G}(z)=\sup\{\nu>0:0<|z-P/Q|<|Q|^{-\nu}\text{ for infinitely many reduced }P/Q\in\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i})\}.


Gaussian numerators and denominators lie in ℤ⁡[i]\mathbb{Z}[\mathrm{i}], with |Q|>1|Q|>1.
Associates do not affect this definition.


The rational and rational-power cases of the following theorem also
appear in [23, Theorem 1 and Corollary 7].


Theorem 4.1.

Let a>0a>0 be algebraic, a≠1a\neq 1, and d=[ℚ(a):ℚ]d=[\mathbb{Q}(a):\mathbb{Q}]. Then log⁡a\log a
is irrational and




2≤μ⁡(log⁡a)≤2​d.2\leq\mu(\log a)\leq 2d.


In particular μ⁡(log⁡a)=2\mu(\log a)=2 if aa is rational. More generally, if
aN∈ℚa^{N}\in\mathbb{Q} for some integer N≥1N\geq 1, then μ⁡(log⁡a)=2\mu(\log a)=2.



Theorem 4.2.

Let a≠0a\neq 0 be algebraic, and let λ≠0\lambda\neq 0 be any fixed complex
number with eλ=ae^{\lambda}=a. Put d=[ℚ(i,a):ℚ(i)]d=[\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i},a):\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i})]. Then
λ∉ℚ⁡(i)\lambda\notin\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) and




2≤μG​(λ)≤2​d.2\leq\mu_{G}(\lambda)\leq 2d.


In particular, if a∈ℚ​(i)×a\in\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i})^{\times}, then μG​(λ)=2\mu_{G}(\lambda)=2.
The same conclusion holds whenever aN∈ℚ⁡(i)a^{N}\in\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) for some N≥1N\geq 1.



Theorem 4.2 includes nonzero branches at a=1a=1;
allowing them is harmless. The assumption λ≠0\lambda\neq 0 excludes the
rational logarithm zero.


Corollary 4.3.

For every nonzero rational rr, μ⁡(arctan⁡r)=2\mu(\arctan r)=2.



Proof.

The number a=(1+i​r)/(1−i​r)a=(1+\mathrm{i}r)/(1-\mathrm{i}r) belongs to ℚ⁡(i)\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) and has modulus
one. Choose λ=2​i​arctan⁡r\lambda=2\mathrm{i}\arctan r, so that eλ=ae^{\lambda}=a.
If rational approximants p/qp/q to arctan⁡r\arctan r had an exponent
ν>2\nu>2, choose 2<ν′<ν2<\nu^{\prime}<\nu. For large qq, multiplication by
2​i2\mathrm{i} would give Gaussian approximants with error at most
2​q−ν<q−ν′2q^{-\nu}<q^{-\nu^{\prime}}. Reducing the Gaussian fraction can only
decrease its denominator modulus, so the resulting approximation still
has error less than the reduced denominator to the power −ν′-\nu^{\prime}.
These reduced denominators are unbounded, since otherwise the
approximants in a bounded region would form a finite set and could not
converge to λ∉ℚ⁡(i)\lambda\notin\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}). This contradicts
Theorem 4.2. Thus μ⁡(arctan⁡r)≤2\mu(\arctan r)\leq 2.
Irrationality and the real Dirichlet bound give the reverse inequality.
∎



Complex scope.


The ordinary exponent defined using p/q∈ℚp/q\in\mathbb{Q} is a real-number
notion. If Im⁡λ≠0\operatorname{Im}\lambda\neq 0, then
|λ−p/q|≥|Im⁡λ||\lambda-p/q|\geq|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|, so it is not appropriate
to assert that this exponent is two. Theorem 4.2 specifies
one natural complex replacement. For positive real aa we use its real
logarithm in Theorem 4.1.


The proof has four stages. First, interpolation produces a nonzero
determinant whose entries are algebraic numbers. Second, clearing its
denominators and taking a norm gives a lower bound. Third, very accurate
rational approximations to λ\lambda make the same determinant small:
either many rows share a Taylor coefficient vector, or many rows carry
large powers of the approximation errors. Finally, weights are chosen
so that these two bounds are incompatible. The field degree occurs
in the arithmetic norm bound and is responsible for the factor dd
in the general upper bound.




5 Weighted interpolation at moving multiplicative centers


Theorem 5.1 follows the geometric method of
[21, Section 2]. We reproduce the argument because neither
the change in the multiplicative centers nor the order of quantifiers
can be omitted. The strengthened condition involving SS records the
largest number of centers in one multiplicative fiber.
Compare [23, Theorems 2 and 9] for the distinct-center
and bounded-fiber statements.


For W=(w0,…,wm)∈ℚ>0m+1W=(w_{0},\ldots,w_{m})\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}^{m+1}, let 𝒫W​(H)\mathcal{P}_{W}(H) be the
complex vector space with monomial basis




YhX1γ1⋯Xmγm,w0h+∑iwiγi≤H.Y^{h}X_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\cdots X_{m}^{\gamma_{m}},\qquad w_{0}h+\sum_{i}w_{i}\gamma_{i}\leq H.


For V=(v0,…,vm)>0V=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{m})>0, write




𝒥V​(H)=ℂ⁡[[t,u1,…,um]]/FV≥H,FV≥H={f:every monomial of ​f​ has weight at least ​H}.\mathcal{J}_{V}(H)=\mathbb{C}[[t,u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}]]/F_{V}^{\geq H},\qquad F_{V}^{\geq H}=\{f:\text{every monomial of }f\text{ has weight at least }H\}.


Thus the target coefficients have strict weight less than HH.
All monomial exponents are nonnegative integers. In particular, the
source includes its degree boundary whereas the target excludes its
weight boundary.


Theorem 5.1 (Varying centers with bounded fiber occupancy).

Fix integers m,K,S≥1m,K,S\geq 1, with S≤KS\leq K, and positive rationals
w0,v0,θw_{0},v_{0},\theta such that




0<θ<1,S​θm<1,K​w0v0​θm<1.0<\theta<1,\qquad S\theta^{m}<1,\qquad K\frac{w_{0}}{v_{0}}\theta^{m}<1.

(5.1)

There are successive lower thresholds for positive rational weights
w1,…,wmw_{1},\ldots,w_{m}, each depending only on these fixed data and earlier
weights, with the following property. Put vi=wi/θv_{i}=w_{i}/\theta for i>0i>0.
For each such choice of weights there is an integer R≥1R\geq 1, depending
only on the weights, such that the following holds for every collection




aj=(bj,cj​1,…,cj​m)∈ℂ××ℂm,0≤j<K.a_{j}=(b_{j},c_{j1},\ldots,c_{jm})\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{m},\qquad 0\leq j<K.


Assume that at most SS of the bjb_{j} have any one value, and that, within
each group having the same bjb_{j}, the cj​ic_{ji} are pairwise distinct for
every ii. There is an H0H_{0}, allowed to depend on the centers, for which




𝒫W​(H)⟶⨁j<K𝒥V​(H),P⟼[P⁡(bj​(1+t),(cj​i+ui+log⁡(1+t))i)]j<K\mathcal{P}_{W}(H)\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j<K}\mathcal{J}_{V}(H),\qquad P\longmapsto\left[P\bigl(b_{j}(1+t),(c_{ji}+u_{i}+\log(1+t))_{i}\bigr)\right]_{j<K}

(5.2)

is surjective whenever H≥H0H\geq H_{0} is an integer divisible by RR.



More formally, the assertion is that for fixed base data there exist
functions Ti​(w1,…,wi−1)T_{i}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{i-1}) such that




∀w1>T1∀w2>T2(w1)⋯∀wm>Tm(w1,…,wm−1),∃R>0∀ admissible centers {aj}∃H0∀H≥H0,R∣H⟹(5.2) is onto.\begin{gathered}\forall w_{1}>T_{1}\ \forall w_{2}>T_{2}(w_{1})\ \cdots\ \forall w_{m}>T_{m}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{m-1}),\\
\exists R>0\ \forall\text{ admissible centers }\{a_{j}\}\ \exists H_{0}\ \forall H\geq H_{0},\quad R\mid H\ \Longrightarrow\ \eqref{geom:eq:map}\text{ is onto}.\end{gathered}


All weights in these quantifiers are positive rationals, and R,H0,HR,H_{0},H
are integers. The positions of the centers do not occur in the
functions TiT_{i} or in the choice of RR.


The logarithm throughout is the formal series with constant term zero.
There is no choice of a global logarithm of bjb_{j} in this statement.


Corollary 5.2 (The required extension of PA).

Theorem 5.1 holds with the original conditions
K​θm<1K\theta^{m}<1 and K⁡(w0/v0)​θm<1K(w_{0}/v_{0})\theta^{m}<1, and with coordinatewise
distinctness across all centers.



Proof.

Take S=KS=K.∎



Corollary 5.3 (Distinct multiplicative centers).

If the bjb_{j} are pairwise distinct, the condition S​θm<1S\theta^{m}<1 is
automatic with S=1S=1. No distinctness condition on the cj​ic_{ji} is needed.
In particular this applies to bj=αjb_{j}=\alpha^{j} for a real α>1\alpha>1.



5.1 A local multiplicity estimate with uniform constants


Here a normal basis means a set of ambient vector fields whose images
form a basis of the quotient by the tangent space of a subvariety at a
general smooth point.


Generality convention for the multiplicity statement.


The analytic open set is understood to meet the component under
consideration. A coordinate or frame normal basis is a basis at general
smooth points of that same analytic component domain: it is valid on a
relatively open dense subset there. No Zariski openness of an analytic
frame’s normality locus is presumed. These conditions are made explicit
at the checked paper endpoint in Appendix A.


Lemma 5.4 (Weighted transverse multiplicity).

Fix ε>0\varepsilon>0. Give affine coordinates degree weights
ρa∈ℚ>0\rho_{a}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}. Let DbD_{b}
be a commuting analytic frame on an open subset, with positive costs
κb\kappa_{b}. Suppose polynomials fℓf_{\ell} of weighted degree at most N>0N>0
have an irreducible common-zero component ZZ of codimension k≥1k\geq 1,
and




Dγ​fℓ|Z=0if∑bκb​γb<ε​N.D^{\gamma}f_{\ell}|_{Z}=0\quad\hbox{if}\quad\sum_{b}\kappa_{b}\gamma_{b}<\varepsilon N.


For any coordinate normal basis AA and frame normal basis BB, each
of size kk,




∏a∈Aρa≤2​k!​ε−k​∏b∈Bκb.\prod_{a\in A}\rho_{a}\leq 2k!\,\varepsilon^{-k}\prod_{b\in B}\kappa_{b}.

(5.3)

There is no threshold depending on ZZ, the equations, or the frame.



Proof.

Choose a smooth point xx of ZZ where both normal-basis conditions hold
and where no other common-zero component passes. Commuting flows of the
fields in BB, starting from ZZ, give local analytic coordinates (z,b)(z,b),
with Z={b=0}Z=\{b=0\}. The assumed derivatives say that the expansions
fℓ​(z,b)f_{\ell}(z,b) have no monomial in bb of BB-weight less than
ε​N\varepsilon N; its coefficient is identically zero as a function of zz.


The affine slice obtained by fixing the coordinates outside AA is
transverse to ZZ at xx. On this slice bb is again a local coordinate
system and z=z⁡(b)z=z(b) is analytic. Substituting z⁡(b)z(b) cannot introduce a
monomial of smaller weight: each surviving term already contains a
factor bηb^{\eta} of weight at least ε​N\varepsilon N. The ideal of the
restricted equations is therefore contained in the ideal of series of
that weight or higher. The common zero is isolated on this slice,
because the support near xx is ZZ and the slice is transverse.
Consequently the ideal has finite colength. Inclusion of ideals gives
a surjection of its local quotient onto the quotient by the weighted
monomial ideal, so that its colength is at least




#⁡{η∈ℤ≥0k:∑b∈Bκb​ηb<ε​N}≥(ε​N)kk!​∏b∈Bκb.\#\{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}:\textstyle\sum_{b\in B}\kappa_{b}\eta_{b}<\varepsilon N\}\geq\frac{(\varepsilon N)^{k}}{k!\prod_{b\in B}\kappa_{b}}.


For the last inequality, the half-open unit cubes based at the admissible
integer points cover the positive simplex of weighted radius
ε​N\varepsilon N. This estimate is valid for every N>0N>0.


Take kk generic constant linear combinations of the restricted equations
whose common zero remains isolated at xx. Such combinations exist by
successively avoiding the finitely many positive-dimensional local
components. Their ideal is contained in the original ideal, so their
local intersection length is at least its colength. Each combination
still has degree at most NN in the weights indexed by AA.


That local intersection length is at most Nk/∏a∈AρaN^{k}/\prod_{a\in A}\rho_{a}.
Indeed choose an integer MM with M​ρa∈ℤ>0M\rho_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0} and make
the finite substitutions




xa=λa+zaM​ρa.x_{a}=\lambda_{a}+z_{a}^{M\rho_{a}}.


Choose λa≠xa​(x)\lambda_{a}\neq x_{a}(x), so all preimages of xx are unramified.
There are ∏a∈AM​ρa\prod_{a\in A}M\rho_{a} of them, each with the same local
length. The substituted equations have ordinary degree at most M​NMN.
The ordinary Bézout inequality for isolated zeros bounds the sum of
their local lengths by (M​N)k(MN)^{k}; other positive-dimensional components
do not invalidate the bound [24, Corollary VIII.3]. Dividing by
the number of preimages proves the claimed upper bound. Comparing the
two bounds proves even (5.3) without its harmless factor 22.
∎





5.2 Choosing the weights before choosing the centers


Choose a positive rational σ\sigma so small that




(1+3​σ)m+1​K​(w0/v0)​θm<1,(1+3​σ)m​S​θm<1,(1+σ)​θ<1.\begin{split}(1+3\sigma)^{m+1}K(w_{0}/v_{0})\theta^{m}&<1,\\
(1+3\sigma)^{m}S\theta^{m}&<1,\\
(1+\sigma)\theta&<1.\end{split}

(5.4)

Let L=m+2L=m+2 and




C∗=max1≤k≤m⁡2​k!​(L/σ)k.C_{*}=\max_{1\leq k\leq m}2k!(L/\sigma)^{k}.


Require, for index sets A,B⊆{0,…,m}A,B\subseteq\{0,\ldots,m\} of the same
size between 11 and mm,




∏a∈Awa>C∗​∏b∈Bvb\prod_{a\in A}w_{a}>C_{*}\prod_{b\in B}v_{b}

(5.5)

whenever the largest positive index in A△BA\mathbin{\triangle}B belongs
to AA. There is no condition of this kind when no positive index differs.


These requirements determine successive lower thresholds. If the
largest differing index is ii, cancel every index above ii occurring
in both sets; its contribution is wj/vj=θw_{j}/v_{j}=\theta. The remaining
ratio is wiw_{i} times a positive expression involving only lower weights,
w0,v0,θw_{0},v_{0},\theta and the two finite index sets. Thus all conditions for
index ii follow from one finite lower bound on wiw_{i}. Subsequent choices
do not alter them. In particular the procedure has not used any bjb_{j}
or cj​ic_{ji}.



5.3 The curve inequality


Let CC be an irreducible curve in affine space, and compute orders on
its smooth complete model. Define




degW⁡C=∑Pmax⁡(0,−ordP⁡Yw0,−ordP⁡X1w1,…,−ordP⁡Xmwm).\deg_{W}C=\sum_{P}\max\left(0,-\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}Y}{w_{0}},-\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}X_{1}}{w_{1}},\ldots,-\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}X_{m}}{w_{m}}\right).


An identically zero coordinate has order +∞+\infty and contributes no pole.
For a branch PP through aja_{j}, put




hP=min⁡(ordP⁡(Y/bj−1)v0,mini⁡ordP⁡(Xi−cj​i−log⁡(Y/bj))vi).h_{P}=\min\left(\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}(Y/b_{j}-1)}{v_{0}},\min_{i}\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}(X_{i}-c_{ji}-\log(Y/b_{j}))}{v_{i}}\right).

(5.6)

All these local orders are positive, possibly infinite; their minimum
is finite on a nonconstant branch, since these are formal coordinates.
Only branches through the specified affine centers enter the sums below.


Proposition 5.5.

For the weights just selected,




degW⁡C≥(1+σ)​∑P↦ajhP.\deg_{W}C\geq(1+\sigma)\sum_{P\mapsto a_{j}}h_{P}.

(5.7)




Proof.

Suppose a curve violates (5.7); it must meet a center.
For all sufficiently large integers NN, coefficient counting supplies
a nonzero polynomial FNF_{N} of WW-degree at most NN whose local expansions
at every center have VV-order at least (1+3​σ)​N(1+3\sigma)N. The ratio of
the numbers of imposed coefficients and available coefficients tends to




(1+3​σ)m+1​K​(w0/v0)​θm<1.(1+3\sigma)^{m+1}K(w_{0}/v_{0})\theta^{m}<1.


The conditions need not be independent for this existence assertion.
For clarity, for any fixed positive weights ρ1,…,ρn\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n},
the number of lattice points with ∑ρi​ei≤N\sum\rho_{i}e_{i}\leq N is




Nnn!​∏iρi+O⁡(Nn−1).\frac{N^{n}}{n!\prod_{i}\rho_{i}}+O(N^{n-1}).


Covering the corresponding simplex by unit cubes proves this estimate;
changing a weak cutoff to a strict cutoff changes only a boundary
contribution of order O⁡(Nn−1)O(N^{n-1}). Applying it to the source and to
the KK local packets gives the stated ratio. Since this ratio is
strictly less than one, the linear system imposing the vanishing has
a nonzero solution for every sufficiently large NN.


On Y≠0Y\neq 0 use the commuting frame




D0=Y∂Y+∑i∂Xi,Di=∂Xi.D_{0}=Y\partial_{Y}+\sum_{i}\partial_{X_{i}},\qquad D_{i}=\partial_{X_{i}}.


At the jjth center let tj=Y/bj−1t_{j}=Y/b_{j}-1 and
uj​i=Xi−cj​i−log⁡(Y/bj)u_{ji}=X_{i}-c_{ji}-\log(Y/b_{j}). Direct differentiation gives




D0=(1+tj)∂tj,Di=∂uj​i.D_{0}=(1+t_{j})\partial_{t_{j}},\qquad D_{i}=\partial_{u_{ji}}.

(5.8)

Every DaD_{a} preserves the bound on polynomial degree and reduces
weighted local order by at most vav_{a}. Hence Dγ​FND^{\gamma}F_{N} has order
at least (1+2​σ)​N(1+2\sigma)N at all centers if ∑ava​γa≤σ​N\sum_{a}v_{a}\gamma_{a}\leq\sigma N.
On a branch through a center, its order is at least
(1+2​σ)​N​hP(1+2\sigma)Nh_{P}. If its restriction to CC were nonzero, its total
pole order would be at most N​degW​CN\deg_{W}C, smaller than the displayed
total zero order. The divisor equality on the complete curve rules this
out. We obtain




Dγ​FN|C=0(∑ava​γa≤σ​N).D^{\gamma}F_{N}|_{C}=0\qquad(\textstyle\sum_{a}v_{a}\gamma_{a}\leq\sigma N).

(5.9)



Set δ=σ​N/L\delta=\sigma N/L. Let VrV_{r} be the common zero set in Y≠0Y\neq 0
of derivatives of cost at most r​δr\delta, for 0≤r≤L0\leq r\leq L.
The part C∘=C∩{Y≠0}C^{\circ}=C\cap\{Y\neq 0\} belongs to all these zero sets.
Starting with a component of VLV_{L} containing C∘C^{\circ}, choose
containing components backwards. Their dimensions lie between 11 and
mm, since FN≠0F_{N}\neq 0. Some two adjacent components coincide, say in ZZ.
It is a component of the earlier derivative zero set, and every further
derivative of its defining equations of cost less than δ\delta vanishes
on ZZ. Apply Lemma 5.4 with ε=σ/L\varepsilon=\sigma/L.
For all coordinate and frame normal bases A,BA,B of ZZ we have




∏a∈Awa≤C∗​∏b∈Bvb.\prod_{a\in A}w_{a}\leq C_{*}\prod_{b\in B}v_{b}.

(5.10)

Here the phrase “defining equations” means the finite list
Dγ​FND^{\gamma}F_{N} used for the earlier zero set; it does not assert that
this list generates the reduced ideal of ZZ. That assertion is not
needed in Lemma 5.4. If the equal adjacent components
occur at stages r,r+1r,r+1, differentiating an equation of cost at most
r​δr\delta by cost less than δ\delta produces an equation from stage
r+1r+1. This explains precisely why the lemma applies. The existence of
equal adjacent components follows from strict dimension decrease for
proper inclusions of irreducible varieties.


Check the finitely many generic normal-basis rank conditions on one
common smooth open subset of ZZ.
We now show that YY is constant on ZZ. If some positive coordinate
direction is nontangent, let ii be its largest index. All larger
positive indices occur in neither kind of normal basis. A coordinate
normal basis containing ii exists. Every frame normal basis must also
contain ii, by (5.5) and (5.10). The images of
the other frame vectors therefore fail to span the normal quotient.
Their ambient span is the hyperplane




Q=ker⁡(d​Xi−d​Y/Y).Q=\ker(dX_{i}-dY/Y).


For a hyperplane QQ and tangent subspace TT, failure of the image of
QQ to fill the quotient by TT is equivalent to T⊆QT\subseteq Q.
Thus d​Xi=d​Y/YdX_{i}=dY/Y on the smooth locus of ZZ.


If YY were nonconstant on ZZ, take a smooth point with d​Y≠0dY\neq 0
and cut by general hyperplanes to obtain an algebraic curve through it
on which YY remains nonconstant. On its smooth complete model the
identity of differentials persists. The nonconstant meromorphic
function YY has a zero or pole; there d​Y/YdY/Y has nonzero residue,
whereas the exact meromorphic differential d​XidX_{i} has residue zero.
This contradiction proves that YY is constant. If instead every
positive coordinate direction was tangent to ZZ, those mm independent
directions already fill its tangent space, since ZZ is proper; then
d​Y=0dY=0 directly, with the same conclusion.


It follows that the violating curve lies in some fiber Y=b≠0Y=b\neq 0.
Only the centers with bj=bb_{j}=b can lie on it. Let their number be KbK_{b},
so 1≤Kb≤S1\leq K_{b}\leq S. Inside this fiber the contact becomes




hP=mini⁡ordP⁡(Xi−cj​i)/vi.h_{P}=\min_{i}\operatorname{ord}_{P}(X_{i}-c_{ji})/v_{i}.


The ratio of condition counts to coefficient counts for an auxiliary
polynomial GN​(X1,…,Xm)G_{N}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}) is now




Kb​(1+3​σ)m​θm<1.K_{b}(1+3\sigma)^{m}\theta^{m}<1.


Choose nonzero GNG_{N} of weighted degree at most NN with local order at
least (1+3​σ)​N(1+3\sigma)N at these centers. The same divisor argument gives
∂XγGN|C=0\partial_{X}^{\gamma}G_{N}|_{C}=0 for ∑ivi​γi≤σ​N\sum_{i}v_{i}\gamma_{i}\leq\sigma N.
For m=1m=1 even GN|C=0G_{N}|_{C}=0 is impossible: a curve in the affine line is
the whole line, on which a nonzero polynomial cannot vanish identically.


For m≥2m\geq 2, repeat the nested-component argument in this fiber.
It produces a component Z′Z^{\prime} containing the affine curve, of codimension
between 11 and m−1m-1, and satisfies (5.10) for index sets in
{1,…,m}\{1,\ldots,m\}. Here coordinate and frame directions coincide.
There can be only one normal-basis index set: two different sets, ordered
so their largest differing index belongs to the first, would contradict
(5.5). Select an index ii in this nonempty unique basis.
All other coordinate directions fail to span the normal quotient,
so d​Xi=0dX_{i}=0 on Z′Z^{\prime} and XiX_{i} is constant on Z′Z^{\prime}.
Distinctness within the fiber implies that Z′Z^{\prime}, and hence CC, meets
at most one prescribed center.


Let that center be aja_{j}. Choose a coordinate XlX_{l} nonconstant on CC.
The meromorphic function Xl−cj​lX_{l}-c_{jl} has total pole order at most
wl​degW​Cw_{l}\deg_{W}C, and at least vl​hPv_{l}h_{P} zeros at each branch through aja_{j}.
Consequently




degW⁡C≥θ−1​∑P↦ajhP>(1+σ)​∑P↦ajhP,\deg_{W}C\geq\theta^{-1}\sum_{P\mapsto a_{j}}h_{P}>(1+\sigma)\sum_{P\mapsto a_{j}}h_{P},


contradicting the supposed violation. This proves (5.7).
∎



Remark 5.6.

If the bjb_{j} are distinct, once YY is constant the curve can meet only
one center. The entire fiberwise auxiliary-polynomial and persistent-
component argument can then be omitted; the last divisor comparison
applies immediately.




5.4 From curves to all coefficient packets


We give the global step because it is responsible for both the strict
cutoff and the order of quantifiers in Theorem 5.1.


Choose an integer R>0R>0, depending only on W,VW,V, such that
R/waR/w_{a} and R/vaR/v_{a} are positive integers for all aa. Increasing RR
if necessary, all coordinates have degree at most RR. Embed affine
space by every monomial of WW-degree at most RR, including 11, and
let XX be its projective closure. Its constant-coordinate chart is
exactly the original affine space: the image there is the closed graph
of the monomials, with the original coordinates among them. Write
ℒ=𝒪X​(1)\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}_{X}(1), which is very ample.


For a curve meeting this chart,




deg⁡(ℒ|C)=R​degW​C.\deg(\mathcal{L}|_{C})=R\deg_{W}C.

(5.11)

Indeed, on its smooth complete model the largest pole of the homogeneous
coordinate functions at each point is exactly the right-hand local
maximum: the corresponding pure power with exponent R/waR/w_{a} attains
it. The constant section handles maximum zero. Clearing these poles
gives sections without a common zero, proving (5.11).


Choose Taylor polynomials Gi​(t)G_{i}(t) of log⁡(1+t)\log(1+t) whose omitted terms
have tt-weight strictly greater than viv_{i}; for example retain terms of
degrees below an integer TiT_{i} with v0​Ti>viv_{0}T_{i}>v_{i}. At aja_{j} use the
algebraic regular coordinates




tj=Y/bj−1,uj​i′=Xi−cj​i−Gi​(tj).t_{j}=Y/b_{j}-1,\qquad u^{\prime}_{ji}=X_{i}-c_{ji}-G_{i}(t_{j}).


Changing between uj​i′u^{\prime}_{ji} and uj​iu_{ji} adds a series of weight greater
than viv_{i}. Both this substitution and its inverse preserve every
weighted filtration ideal, and hence give inverse automorphisms on
every finite packet. They also preserve the minimum (5.6):
if tjt_{j} is nonzero on a branch, the normalized order of its tail is
strictly larger than ordP⁡(tj)/v0\operatorname{ord}_{P}(t_{j})/v_{0}; if it is identically zero, the
tail vanishes. Thus it cannot change the minimum.


At aja_{j} form the ideal




Ij=(tjR/v0,(uj​1′)R/v1,…,(uj​m′)R/vm).I_{j}=\bigl(t_{j}^{R/v_{0}},(u^{\prime}_{j1})^{R/v_{1}},\ldots,(u^{\prime}_{jm})^{R/v_{m}}\bigr).


It has finite colength. The centers are distinct by the hypotheses, so
these ideals extend to a coherent ideal sheaf II on XX, equal to the
unit ideal away from the centers. For example one may take the kernel
of the map to the direct sum of their finite local quotient sheaves.
Its valuation on each curve branch through a center is




ordP⁡I=R​hP.\operatorname{ord}_{P}I=Rh_{P}.

(5.12)



Take the ordinary blow-up p:X′→Xp:X^{\prime}\to X of II. With its exceptional
effective Cartier divisor EE,




I​𝒪X′=𝒪X′​(−E),A=p∗​ℒ.I\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}(-E),\qquad A=p^{*}\mathcal{L}.


The bundle −E-E is relatively ample; these are ordinary Rees-algebra
blow-up facts, valid here without smoothness of XX or X′X^{\prime}
[26, Tags 02OS and 0AG6].


We claim A−(1+σ)​EA-(1+\sigma)E is nef. A noncontracted integral curve upstairs
mapping to a curve meeting the affine chart maps birationally, since
the blow-up is an isomorphism away from finitely many points.
Equations (5.11)–(5.12) and
Proposition 5.5 give nonnegative degree on that curve.
A curve whose image misses the affine chart misses EE altogether.
A contracted curve has AA-degree zero and strictly positive −E-E-degree
by relative ampleness. These cases cover all integral curves.
On a noncontracted curve the intersection with EE is computed by
pulling back to its smooth complete model. There the ideal II
becomes a principal ideal and the degree of its zero divisor is the
sum of the valuations R​hPRh_{P}. This is the link between the branchwise
curve inequality and the intersection calculation on the blow-up.


For some integer a>1a>1, a​A−EaA-E is ample: choose bb making
I⊗ℒbI\otimes\mathcal{L}^{b} globally generated, embed the blow-up in
X×ℙrX\times\mathbb{P}^{r} by those generators, and restrict the product
ample bundle to obtain (b+1)​A−E(b+1)A-E. The exact identity




A−E=a−1a⁡(1+σ)−1​(A−(1+σ)​E)+σa⁡(1+σ)−1​(a​A−E)A-E=\frac{a-1}{a(1+\sigma)-1}\bigl(A-(1+\sigma)E\bigr)+\frac{\sigma}{a(1+\sigma)-1}(aA-E)


shows that A−EA-E is ample, since a positive rational multiple of an
ample class plus a nef class is ample [25, Corollary 1.4.10].


For all sufficiently large nn, the ordinary Rees algebra gives




p∗𝒪X′(−nE)=In,Rqp∗𝒪X′(−nE)=0(q>0).p_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}(-nE)=I^{n},\qquad R^{q}p_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}(-nE)=0\quad(q>0).

(5.13)

To justify the word “ordinary”, work over each member of a finite
affine cover of XX. The Rees algebra is a Noetherian graded algebra
generated in degree one. Its degree-nn piece is precisely InI^{n}.
The graded-piece comparison and higher-cohomology vanishing on Proj
apply for all sufficiently large nn, and the finite cover gives one
threshold [26, Tags 0AG6 and 0AG7]. No equality with an integral
closure of InI^{n}, and no assertion for small nn, is used.


The projection formula, Leray, and Serre vanishing for A−EA-E yield




H1​(X,In⊗ℒn)=H1​(X′,𝒪X′​(n⁡(A−E)))=0H^{1}(X,I^{n}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{n})=H^{1}(X^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}(n(A-E)))=0


for all sufficiently large nn. Therefore the exact sequence for InI^{n}
makes H0​(X,ℒn)H^{0}(X,\mathcal{L}^{n}) surject onto its local coefficient classes
modulo InI^{n}. Completing these finite-length local quotients does not
alter them. Each generator of IjI_{j} has weight exactly RR, so




Ijn⊆FV≥n​R.I_{j}^{n}\subseteq F_{V}^{\geq nR}.


There is thus a surjection from the quotient by IjnI_{j}^{n} onto the desired
packet of weight strictly less than n​RnR. Equality of these ideals is
neither asserted nor needed.
The Serre-vanishing assertion used here is the positive-degree
cohomology vanishing for every coherent sheaf after a sufficiently high
power of an ample line bundle [26, Tag 0B5U].


Finally, for large nn, all sections of ℒn\mathcal{L}^{n} come from
homogeneous degree-nn polynomials in the embedding coordinates.
Apply Serre vanishing to the homogeneous ideal sheaf of XX in the
ambient projective space. Each such polynomial restricts on the affine
chart to a polynomial of WW-degree at most n​RnR. Put H=n​RH=nR and undo
the filtered coordinate changes above. This proves surjectivity of
(5.2).


All geometric size thresholds can depend on the chosen centers, and
their finite maximum determines H0H_{0}. The integer RR was fixed before
any center. This proves every quantifier of Theorem 5.1.




6 The determinant and its arithmetic lower bound


We treat two base fields simultaneously. Let F=ℚF=\mathbb{Q}, with real
λ=log⁡a\lambda=\log a, or let F=ℚ⁡(i)F=\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}), with arbitrary fixed nonzero
λ\lambda satisfying eλ=ae^{\lambda}=a. Put E=F⁡(a)E=F(a) and d=[E:F]d=[E:F].
Suppose, toward a contradiction, that approximations




ri=Pi/Qi,|λ−ri|≤|Qi|−ν,wi=⌈log⁡|Qi|⌉r_{i}=P_{i}/Q_{i},\qquad|\lambda-r_{i}|\leq|Q_{i}|^{-\nu},\qquad w_{i}=\lceil\log|Q_{i}|\rceil

(6.1)

can be chosen with unbounded denominators. Here Pi,QiP_{i},Q_{i} are in
ℤ\mathbb{Z} or ℤ⁡[i]\mathbb{Z}[\mathrm{i}], according to FF, and ν>2​d\nu>2d.
Choose the weights successively, and then fix them for the degree
limit. Large denominators ensure ri≠0r_{i}\neq 0 and
|ri|≤|λ|+1|r_{i}|\leq|\lambda|+1.


Take bj=ajb_{j}=a^{j}, cj​i=j​ric_{ji}=jr_{i}, 0≤j<K0\leq j<K. The distinctness
condition in Theorem 5.1 holds since ri≠0r_{i}\neq 0.
Set




Ti=⌈wi/(θ​v0)⌉,Gi​(t)=∑1≤k<Ti(−1)k+1​tkk,Li=lcm⁡(1,…,Ti−1).T_{i}=\left\lceil w_{i}/(\theta v_{0})\right\rceil,\quad G_{i}(t)=\sum_{1\leq k<T_{i}}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}t^{k}}{k},\quad L_{i}=\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,T_{i}-1).


The usual empty lcm equals one. The following identity permits this
short arithmetic truncation while keeping the exact logarithm in the
analytic estimate.


Lemma 6.1 (Exact invariance of a full-row minor).

Fix a finite coefficient packet consisting of all
(j,s,β)(j,s,\beta) with v0​s+∑ivi​βi<Hv_{0}s+\sum_{i}v_{i}\beta_{i}<H, and fix any
selection of as many source columns as there are rows. Let AGA_{G}
be the square coefficient matrix obtained from




P⁡(bj​(1+t),(cj​i+Gi​(t)+ui)i).P\bigl(b_{j}(1+t),(c_{ji}+G_{i}(t)+u_{i})_{i}\bigr).


Suppose every nonzero term tat^{a} of Ti​(t)T_{i}(t) satisfies
v0​a≥viv_{0}a\geq v_{i}. Then, with the same row and column orders,




detAG+T=detAG.\det A_{G+T}=\det A_{G}.


This includes equality at the weight boundary.



Proof.

Substitution ui↦ui+Ti​(t)u_{i}\mapsto u_{i}+T_{i}(t) preserves the ideal of
monomials of weight at least HH. Its inverse is the substitution
with −Ti-T_{i}, so it induces an automorphism of the finite quotient.
More explicitly, a coefficient of tk​uγt^{k}u^{\gamma} can contribute to
the coefficient of ts​uβt^{s}u^{\beta} only if γ≥β\gamma\geq\beta and




v0​(s−k)≥∑ivi​(γi−βi).v_{0}(s-k)\geq\sum_{i}v_{i}(\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}).


The source coefficient therefore has weight at most the target weight,
and so already belongs to the packet. Every term other than the
unchanged coefficient has k<sk<s, since all nonzero TiT_{i} have positive
order. Ordering rows by increasing Taylor degree, and arbitrarily
inside each degree, makes this coefficient transformation triangular
with diagonal entries 11. This holds independently in each center
block. Hence AG+T=U​AGA_{G+T}=UA_{G} with detU=1\det U=1, which proves the claim.
∎



For our choice of TiT_{i}, the logarithmic tail starts at weight
v0​Ti≥wi/θ=viv_{0}T_{i}\geq w_{i}/\theta=v_{i}. Lemma 6.1 therefore
identifies every full-row minor formed with GiG_{i} with the minor formed
using the exact logarithm and the same selected columns. This is
determinant equality under a target coordinate change; individual
entries need not agree.


The columns are Yh​XγY^{h}X^{\gamma} with w0​h+w⋅γ≤Hw_{0}h+w\cdot\gamma\leq H;
the rows are ρ=(j,s,β)\rho=(j,s,\beta) with
v0​s+w⋅β/θ<Hv_{0}s+w\cdot\beta/\theta<H. The entry is




[ts​uβ]​P​(aj​(1+t),(j​ri+Gi​(t)+ui)i).[t^{s}u^{\beta}]\,P\bigl(a^{j}(1+t),(jr_{i}+G_{i}(t)+u_{i})_{i}\bigr).

(6.2)

Theorem 5.1 supplies a nonzero square minor
ΔH∈E\Delta_{H}\in E using all M=MHM=M_{H} rows. Define




b¯=1M​H​∑ρw⋅β,M∼K​θm​Hm+1(m+1)!​v0​∏iwi,0≤b¯≤θ.\bar{b}=\frac{1}{MH}\sum_{\rho}w\cdot\beta,\qquad M\sim\frac{K\theta^{m}H^{m+1}}{(m+1)!v_{0}\prod_{i}w_{i}},\qquad 0\leq\bar{b}\leq\theta.




Choose an integer b≥1b\geq 1 such that b​aba is an algebraic integer.
Put




DH=∏iLi⌊H/wi⌋,CH=DHM​b(K−1)​∑colh​∏iQi∑colγi−∑rowβi.D_{H}=\prod_{i}L_{i}^{\lfloor H/w_{i}\rfloor},\qquad C_{H}=D_{H}^{M}b^{(K-1)\sum_{\rm col}h}\prod_{i}Q_{i}^{\sum_{\rm col}\gamma_{i}-\sum_{\rm row}\beta_{i}}.


Then CH​ΔHC_{H}\Delta_{H} is a nonzero algebraic integer in EE.
Indeed, scaling columns by ∏iQiγi\prod_{i}Q_{i}^{\gamma_{i}} and rows by
∏iQi−βi\prod_{i}Q_{i}^{-\beta_{i}} leaves denominators only from the GiG_{i}
and from aj​ha^{jh}. The indicated DHD_{H} and bb factors clear these.
The exponents of QiQ_{i} are nonnegative: any nonzero determinant
term matches each row with a column satisfying γ≥β\gamma\geq\beta.
Such a determinant term exists because ΔH≠0\Delta_{H}\neq 0. To check the
clearing entry by entry, after removing uβu^{\beta} the remaining
iith factor has exponent γi−βi\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}. Multiplication by
Qiγi−βiQ_{i}^{\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}} replaces it by
(j​Pi+Qi​Gi​(t))γi−βi(jP_{i}+Q_{i}G_{i}(t))^{\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}}, whose denominators divide
Liγi−βiL_{i}^{\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}}. This exponent is at most
⌊H/wi⌋\lfloor H/w_{i}\rfloor. In column hh, multiplication by
b(K−1)​hb^{(K-1)h} clears aj​ha^{jh} because




b(K−1)​h​aj​h=(b​a)j​h​b(K−1−j)​hb^{(K-1)h}a^{jh}=(ba)^{jh}b^{(K-1-j)h}


is an algebraic integer for every 0≤j<K0\leq j<K. The determinant of
the resulting integral matrix is exactly CH​ΔHC_{H}\Delta_{H}.


Set Λ=4​log⁡2\Lambda=4\log 2 and w∗=mini⁡wiw_{*}=\min_{i}w_{i}. We use the elementary
bound log⁡lcm⁡(1,…,n)≤Λ​n\log\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,n)\leq\Lambda n.
Here is a convenient proof with deliberately generous constants.
If ℒn=lcm⁡(1,…,n)\mathcal{L}_{n}=\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,n), then




ℒ2​n/ℒn|(2​nn)≤22​n.\mathcal{L}_{2n}/\mathcal{L}_{n}\ \mid\ \binom{2n}{n}\leq 2^{2n}.


Indeed, each prime contributes at most one additional power to the
quotient, and the corresponding prime power in (n,2​n](n,2n] contributes
a carry in the valuation of the binomial coefficient. Iterating over
powers of two gives log⁡ℒ2k<2k+1​log⁡2\log\mathcal{L}_{2^{k}}<2^{k+1}\log 2.
For n>1n>1, choose 2k−1<n≤2k2^{k-1}<n\leq 2^{k} and use monotonicity; the case
n=1n=1 is immediate. In particular log⁡Li≤Λ​Ti\log L_{i}\leq\Lambda T_{i}.
It follows that




log⁡|CH|M​H≤1−b¯+E0,E0=Λ​mθ​v0+Λ​∑i1wi+θw∗+(K−1)​log⁡bw0.\frac{\log|C_{H}|}{MH}\leq 1-\bar{b}+E_{0},\quad E_{0}=\frac{\Lambda m}{\theta v_{0}}+\Lambda\sum_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}}+\frac{\theta}{w_{*}}+\frac{(K-1)\log b}{w_{0}}.

(6.3)

To see the term 1−b¯1-\bar{b} explicitly, put
ℓi=log⁡|Qi|\ell_{i}=\log|Q_{i}|, so that wi−1<ℓi≤wiw_{i}-1<\ell_{i}\leq w_{i}.
For the numerator-denominator contribution to CHC_{H},




∑i(∑colγi−∑rowβi)​ℓi≤∑colw⋅γ−∑roww⋅β+∑row|β|≤M​H​(1−b¯)+M​H​θ/w∗.\begin{split}\sum_{i}\Bigl(\sum_{\rm col}\gamma_{i}-\sum_{\rm row}\beta_{i}\Bigr)\ell_{i}&\leq\sum_{\rm col}w\cdot\gamma-\sum_{\rm row}w\cdot\beta+\sum_{\rm row}|\beta|\\
&\leq MH(1-\bar{b})+MH\theta/w_{*}.\end{split}


Also Ti≤wi/(θ​v0)+1T_{i}\leq w_{i}/(\theta v_{0})+1, which bounds log⁡DH/H\log D_{H}/H by the
first two terms in E0E_{0}. Finally, ∑colh≤M​H/w0\sum_{\rm col}h\leq MH/w_{0}.


For the real case, let 𝒮\mathcal{S} be all embeddings
τ:E↪ℂ\tau:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C} except the distinguished real embedding.
For the Gaussian case there are dd embeddings fixing i\mathrm{i};
complex conjugation pairs these with the other dd absolute
embeddings, which send i\mathrm{i} to −i-\mathrm{i}. Let 𝒮\mathcal{S} be the
embeddings fixing i\mathrm{i} except the distinguished one.
Thus |𝒮|=d−1|\mathcal{S}|=d-1 in both cases.
At an embedding in 𝒮\mathcal{S}, Cauchy’s estimate
on |t|=1/2|t|=1/2, |ui|=1|u_{i}|=1 and a determinant expansion give




log⁡|τ​ΔH|M​H≤Eτ+log⁡MH,\frac{\log|\tau\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\leq E_{\tau}+\frac{\log M}{H},

(6.4)

where we may take




Eτ=(K−1)​log+​|τ​a|+log⁡(3/2)w0+log⁡Bλw∗+log⁡2v0,Bλ=K⁡(|λ|+1)+2.E_{\tau}=\frac{(K-1)\log^{+}|\tau a|+\log(3/2)}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log B_{\lambda}}{w_{*}}+\frac{\log 2}{v_{0}},\qquad B_{\lambda}=K(|\lambda|+1)+2.


Here |Gi​(t)|≤log⁡2|G_{i}(t)|\leq\log 2, independently of TiT_{i}.
In the real case τ​ri=ri\tau r_{i}=r_{i}; for the selected Gaussian
embeddings the same equality holds because they fix FF.
For completeness, each entry at such an embedding is at most




2s​|τ​a|j​h​(3/2)h​Bλ|γ|≤exp⁡(H​Eτ).2^{s}|\tau a|^{jh}(3/2)^{h}B_{\lambda}^{|\gamma|}\leq\exp(HE_{\tau}).


The determinant expansion has M!≤MMM!\leq M^{M} summands, which explains
the final term in (6.4). Here
log+⁡x=max⁡(0,log⁡x)\log^{+}x=\max(0,\log x).


The integer absolute norm of CH​ΔHC_{H}\Delta_{H} has modulus at least
one. In the Gaussian case pair conjugate factors and divide its
logarithm by two. Both cases yield




log⁡|ΔH|M​H≥−d⁡(1−b¯)−Ear(d)−o⁡(1),Ear(d)=d​E0+∑τ∈𝒮Eτ.\frac{\log|\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\geq-d(1-\bar{b})-E_{\rm ar}^{(d)}-o(1),\qquad E_{\rm ar}^{(d)}=dE_{0}+\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{S}}E_{\tau}.

(6.5)

This displays the crucial degree factor. When d=1d=1, the embedding
sum is empty and it disappears. Over ℚ⁡(i)\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}) this is precisely
the elementary fact that a nonzero Gaussian integer has modulus
at least one.
More explicitly, in the real case




1≤|NE/ℚ​(CH​ΔH)|=|CH|d​|ΔH|​∏τ∈𝒮|τ​ΔH|.1\leq|N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(C_{H}\Delta_{H})|=|C_{H}|^{d}|\Delta_{H}|\prod_{\tau\in\mathcal{S}}|\tau\Delta_{H}|.


In the Gaussian case the same product, with dd embeddings fixing
i\mathrm{i}, is the square root of the absolute norm over ℚ\mathbb{Q}. Pairing
an embedding τ\tau with τ¯\overline{\tau} is valid even when EE
is not preserved as a subset of ℂ\mathbb{C} by complex conjugation:
x↦τ⁡(x)¯x\mapsto\overline{\tau(x)} is still an embedding of the abstract
field EE into ℂ\mathbb{C}. Thus the norm argument introduces precisely
dd copies of log⁡|CH|\log|C_{H}| in both cases. One can take the
o⁡(1)o(1) in (6.5) to be
(d−1)​log⁡M/H(d-1)\log M/H.



7 Analytic translation and collision saving


For a column PP and multi-index uu-degree δ\delta, define




fδ,P​(z)=[uδ]​P​(ez,z+u1,…,z+um).f_{\delta,P}(z)=[u^{\delta}]P(e^{z},z+u_{1},\ldots,z+u_{m}).


For P=Yh​XγP=Y^{h}X^{\gamma} this is
(γδ)​eh​z​z|γ|−|δ|\binom{\gamma}{\delta}e^{hz}z^{|\gamma|-|\delta|} if
δ≤γ\delta\leq\gamma, and zero otherwise. By Lemma 6.1, the same determinant ΔH\Delta_{H}
can be computed with full logarithms. Put




z=j​λ+log⁡(1+t),ϵj​i=j⁡(ri−λ).z=j\lambda+\log(1+t),\qquad\epsilon_{ji}=j(r_{i}-\lambda).


Then ez=aj​(1+t)e^{z}=a^{j}(1+t) and
j​ri+log⁡(1+t)+ui=z+ui+ϵj​ijr_{i}+\log(1+t)+u_{i}=z+u_{i}+\epsilon_{ji}. Expanding the common
entire column functions in the uu variables gives the exact row identity




rowj,s,βfull=∑δ≥βw⋅δ≤H(δβ)​ϵjδ−β​([ts]​fδ,P​(j​λ+log⁡(1+t)))P.\operatorname{row}_{j,s,\beta}^{\rm full}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\geq\beta\\
w\cdot\delta\leq H\end{subarray}}\binom{\delta}{\beta}\epsilon_{j}^{\delta-\beta}\bigl([t^{s}]f_{\delta,P}(j\lambda+\log(1+t))\bigr)_{P}.

(7.1)

This is a finite sum: fδ,P=0f_{\delta,P}=0 whenever
δ≰γ\delta\not\leq\gamma for a monomial column P=Yh​XγP=Y^{h}X^{\gamma},
and all columns satisfy w⋅γ≤Hw\cdot\gamma\leq H.
The scalar coefficients are independent of the selected column,
which is necessary when applying determinant multilinearity.


Since log⁡|Qi|>wi−1\log|Q_{i}|>w_{i}-1, the approximation hypothesis gives




|ϵj​i|≤K​eν​e−ν​wi.|\epsilon_{ji}|\leq Ke^{\nu}e^{-\nu w_{i}}.


Using (δβ)≤2|δ|\binom{\delta}{\beta}\leq 2^{|\delta|} and
|δ|≤H/w∗|\delta|\leq H/w_{*}, we obtain




|(δβ)ϵjδ−β|≤exp{−νw⋅(δ−β)+HEtr},Etr=log⁡2+log⁡K+νw∗.\left|\binom{\delta}{\beta}\epsilon_{j}^{\delta-\beta}\right|\leq\exp\left\{-\nu w\cdot(\delta-\beta)+HE_{\rm tr}\right\},\qquad E_{\rm tr}=\frac{\log 2+\log K+\nu}{w_{*}}.

(7.2)

This remains valid when some errors are zero, with a zeroth power
interpreted as one. Each row expansion has at most C​(1+H)mC(1+H)^{m} terms,
where CC depends only on the fixed weights. All choices in the
multilinear determinant expansion therefore contribute at most
exp⁡{O⁡(M​log⁡H)}\exp\{O(M\log H)\} summands. Notice that no logarithmic tail
appears in this estimate: the exact shear has already identified the
short arithmetic determinant with its full-logarithm counterpart.


Set Ran=4​K​(|λ|+1)R_{\rm an}=4K(|\lambda|+1). On |t|=1/2|t|=1/2, the test points
j​λ+log⁡(1+t)j\lambda+\log(1+t) have modulus less than Ran/2R_{\rm an}/2.
On |z|≤Ran|z|\leq R_{\rm an},




|fδ,P​(z)|≤exp⁡{H⁡(Ran/w0+log⁡(2​Ran)/w∗)}.|f_{\delta,P}(z)|\leq\exp\{H(R_{\rm an}/w_{0}+\log(2R_{\rm an})/w_{*})\}.


This follows directly from h≤H/w0h\leq H/w_{0}, |γ|≤H/w∗|\gamma|\leq H/w_{*},
and (γδ)≤2|γ|\binom{\gamma}{\delta}\leq 2^{|\gamma|}.
The following estimate makes the collision argument explicit.


Lemma 7.1 (Taylor coefficient collisions).

Choose one analytic row vector from each row expansion
(7.1); its entries have the form




Tr,P=[tℓr]​fδr,P​(jr​λ+log⁡(1+t)),ℓr=sr<H/v0.T_{r,P}=[t^{\ell_{r}}]f_{\delta_{r},P}(j_{r}\lambda+\log(1+t)),\qquad\ell_{r}=s_{r}<H/v_{0}.


Let nδ=#⁡{r:δr=δ}n_{\delta}=\#\{r:\delta_{r}=\delta\}. Put




c=log⁡24,Chol=Ranw0+log⁡(2​Ran)w∗,C1=c−log(1−2−1/2).c=\frac{\log 2}{4},\quad C_{\rm hol}=\frac{R_{\rm an}}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log(2R_{\rm an})}{w_{*}},\quad C_{1}=c-\log(1-2^{-1/2}).


Then




|detT|≤M!exp(MHChol)2∑rℓrexp(−c∑δnδ2+C1M).|\det T|\leq M!\exp(MHC_{\rm hol})2^{\sum_{r}\ell_{r}}\exp\left(-c\sum_{\delta}n_{\delta}^{2}+C_{1}M\right).

(7.3)




Proof.

Expand on the common disk




fδ,P​(z)=∑n≥0cδ,n,P​(z/Ran)n.f_{\delta,P}(z)=\sum_{n\geq 0}c_{\delta,n,P}(z/R_{\rm an})^{n}.


Cauchy’s estimate gives |cδ,n,P|≤exp⁡(H​Chol)|c_{\delta,n,P}|\leq\exp(HC_{\rm hol}).
If




ar,n=[tℓr]​(jr​λ+log⁡(1+t)Ran)n,a_{r,n}=[t^{\ell_{r}}]\left(\frac{j_{r}\lambda+\log(1+t)}{R_{\rm an}}\right)^{n},


then |ar,n|≤2ℓr​2−n|a_{r,n}|\leq 2^{\ell_{r}}2^{-n}. Consequently the Taylor
expansions of all rows converge absolutely, and multilinearity gives
an absolutely convergent sum of determinants with row vectors
(cδr,nr,P)P(c_{\delta_{r},n_{r},P})_{P}.


If two rows have equal pairs (δr,nr)(\delta_{r},n_{r}), those coefficient
vectors are equal and that determinant vanishes. In each surviving
summand, the nδn_{\delta} Taylor indices in the δ\delta group are
distinct nonnegative integers. Their sum is at least
nδ​(nδ−1)/2n_{\delta}(n_{\delta}-1)/2. Writing half the geometric decay separately,
