we therefore have




∑surviving ​(nr)2−∑rnr≤2−14∑δnδ(nδ−1)∑n1,…,nM≥02−12∑rnr=exp(−c∑δnδ2+C1M).\begin{split}\sum_{\text{surviving }(n_{r})}2^{-\sum_{r}n_{r}}&\leq 2^{-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\delta}n_{\delta}(n_{\delta}-1)}\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{M}\geq 0}2^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r}n_{r}}\\
&=\exp\left(-c\sum_{\delta}n_{\delta}^{2}+C_{1}M\right).\end{split}


Every coefficient determinant has absolute value at most
M!​exp⁡(M​H​Chol)M!\exp(MHC_{\rm hol}), by its permutation expansion.
Multiplying these bounds proves (7.3).
∎



Since ∑rℓr<M​H/v0\sum_{r}\ell_{r}<MH/v_{0} and M!=exp⁡(O⁡(M​log⁡M))M!=\exp(O(M\log M)),
Lemma 7.1 implies




|detT|≤exp{−c∑δnδ2+MH(Ehol+o(1))},|\det T|\leq\exp\left\{-c\sum_{\delta}n_{\delta}^{2}+MH(E_{\rm hol}+o(1))\right\},


where




Ehol=Ranw0+log⁡2v0+log⁡(2​Ran)w∗.E_{\rm hol}=\frac{R_{\rm an}}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log 2}{v_{0}}+\frac{\log(2R_{\rm an})}{w_{*}}.


One may take this o⁡(1)o(1) to be (log⁡M+C1)/H(\log M+C_{1})/H.
It is therefore uniform in every row choice and every choice of the
selected source columns. This is the collision mechanism of
[21, Lemma 3.3], with all bounds needed here displayed.


Choose 0<A<10<A<1 and 0<η<10<\eta<1, and put




L=η2​K​θm(m+1)​v0​Am,Ean=Etr+Ehol.L=\frac{\eta^{2}K\theta^{m}}{(m+1)v_{0}A^{m}},\qquad E_{\rm an}=E_{\rm tr}+E_{\rm hol}.


There are two possibilities for each determinant in the row expansion.
If at least η​M\eta M rows have w⋅δ≤A​Hw\cdot\delta\leq AH, put




JH=#⁡{δ∈ℤ≥0m:w⋅δ≤A​H}∼Am​Hmm!​∏iwi.J_{H}=\#\{\delta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m}:w\cdot\delta\leq AH\}\sim\frac{A^{m}H^{m}}{m!\prod_{i}w_{i}}.


Cauchy–Schwarz gives




∑δnδ2≥η2​M2JH,1M​H​∑δnδ2≥L+o⁡(1).\sum_{\delta}n_{\delta}^{2}\geq\frac{\eta^{2}M^{2}}{J_{H}},\qquad\frac{1}{MH}\sum_{\delta}n_{\delta}^{2}\geq L+o(1).


All approximation factors can then be discarded from above, since
δ≥β\delta\geq\beta makes their main exponential contribution at most
one. The normalized saving is at least c​L+o⁡(1)cL+o(1).
Otherwise more than (1−η)​M(1-\eta)M rows have w⋅δ>A​Hw\cdot\delta>AH,
and




∑roww⋅(δ−β)≥M​H​(A⁡(1−η)−b¯).\sum_{\rm row}w\cdot(\delta-\beta)\geq MH\bigl(A(1-\eta)-\bar{b}\bigr).


Discarding the collision saving now yields the approximation saving
ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯)\nu(A(1-\eta)-\bar{b}). The number of row choices contributes only
O⁡(log⁡H/H)O(\log H/H) after division by M​HMH. Consequently




log⁡|ΔH|M​H≤Ean+o⁡(1)+max⁡{−c​L+o⁡(1),−ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯)}.\frac{\log|\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm an}+o(1)+\max\{-cL+o(1),-\nu(A(1-\eta)-\bar{b})\}.

(7.4)

Comparison with (6.5) contradicts existence of the minor
provided




gd:=ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−θ)−d⁡(1−θ)>0,Ear(d)+Ean<gd,c​L>d+Ear(d)+Ean.g_{d}:=\nu(A(1-\eta)-\theta)-d(1-\theta)>0,\quad E_{\rm ar}^{(d)}+E_{\rm an}<g_{d},\quad cL>d+E_{\rm ar}^{(d)}+E_{\rm an}.

(7.5)

For the second alternative the difference between its required saving
and the arithmetic main term is




ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯)−d⁡(1−b¯)=ν​A​(1−η)−d−(ν−d)​b¯≥gd,\nu\bigl(A(1-\eta)-\bar{b}\bigr)-d(1-\bar{b})=\nu A(1-\eta)-d-(\nu-d)\bar{b}\geq g_{d},


because ν>d\nu>d and b¯≤θ\bar{b}\leq\theta. For the first alternative,
d⁡(1−b¯)≤dd(1-\bar{b})\leq d. The inequalities in (7.5) are
strict; hence all the displayed o⁡(1)o(1) terms can be absorbed by taking
HH sufficiently large through the allowed multiples.



8 Parameters and proof of the stated bounds


Fix ν>2​d\nu>2d. Choose a rational number b0b_{0} with
1/2<b0<1−d/ν1/2<b_{0}<1-d/\nu. For sufficiently small rational δ0>0\delta_{0}>0,
put θ=1−δ0\theta=1-\delta_{0} and A=1−b0​δ0A=1-b_{0}\delta_{0}. Then




ν⁡(A−θ)>d⁡(1−θ),A2<θ.\nu(A-\theta)>d(1-\theta),\qquad A^{2}<\theta.


Choose positive rational B,CB,C satisfying




θ<A<B<1,C>1,C​B<1,B<C​θ<1.\theta<A<B<1,\qquad C>1,\qquad CB<1,\qquad B<C\theta<1.


Such choices exist by choosing A/θ<C<1/AA/\theta<C<1/A first and then
A<B<min⁡(1/C,C​θ)A<B<\min(1/C,C\theta). Take η>0\eta>0 small so that gd>0g_{d}>0.
For each dimension mm, set




K=⌊Cm⌋,w0=B−m,v0=2​K​θm​w0.K=\lfloor C^{m}\rfloor,\quad w_{0}=B^{-m},\quad v_{0}=2K\theta^{m}w_{0}.


The volume conditions (5.1) with S=KS=K hold, and




K/w0→0,v0→∞,m/v0→0,L=η2​(B/A)m2​(m+1)→∞.K/w_{0}\to 0,\qquad v_{0}\to\infty,\qquad m/v_{0}\to 0,\qquad L=\frac{\eta^{2}(B/A)^{m}}{2(m+1)}\to\infty.


All constants depending on the fixed algebraic number aa, its
conjugates, its degree, and the chosen branch λ\lambda are fixed
before this limit. Thus the height terms in (6.3)
and (6.4), and Ran/w0R_{\rm an}/w_{0}, tend to zero as mm grows.
Choose one finite dimension giving sufficient margin in (7.5).
Concretely, first reserve a positive error budget ε<gd/4\varepsilon<g_{d}/4.
Choose mm so large that the sum of every term independent of the
transverse weights is below ε\varepsilon and c​L>d+2​εcL>d+2\varepsilon.
Increasing mm further if necessary makes both conditions simultaneous:
C​B<1CB<1 controls the height and growth terms, whereas
C​θ/B>1C\theta/B>1 controls v0v_{0} and B/A>1B/A>1 controls LL.


Next choose the approximations successively with denominators large
enough for Theorem 5.1 and for all terms involving
1/wi1/w_{i} and 1/w∗1/w_{*} to fit within the remaining error margin.
This is possible under the unbounded-approximations hypothesis;
the weight thresholds do not depend on the centers. Finally let
HH tend to infinity through the required multiples. Equations
(6.5) and (7.4) contradict (7.5).
This excludes unbounded-denominator approximations for every
ν>2​d\nu>2d.
The quantifier order is therefore




(a,λ,ν);(θ,A,B,C,η);m,K,w0,v0;(r1,w1),…,(rm,wm);R,H0;H→∞.(a,\lambda,\nu)\ ;\ (\theta,A,B,C,\eta)\ ;\ m,K,w_{0},v_{0}\ ;\ (r_{1},w_{1}),\ldots,(r_{m},w_{m})\ ;\ R,H_{0}\ ;\ H\to\infty.


The degree modulus may be chosen as soon as the weights are fixed,
before evaluating the centers; the notation above groups it with
H0H_{0} only for the final degree limit. No denominator or transverse
weight varies during that limit. This distinction is essential:
choosing new approximants after each value of HH would not justify
the use of the center-dependent threshold H0H_{0}.


The argument also excludes exact representations of λ\lambda in
the base field: such representations could be given arbitrarily
large denominators, and the proof never required reduced fractions
or a nonzero approximation error. Hence λ∉F\lambda\notin F.
Real Dirichlet approximation gives the lower bound two when F=ℚF=\mathbb{Q}.
For F=ℚ⁡(i)F=\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}), place the (N+1)2(N+1)^{2} points
q​λmodℤ⁡[i]q\lambda\bmod\mathbb{Z}[\mathrm{i}], q=u+i​vq=u+\mathrm{i}v, 0≤u,v≤N0\leq u,v\leq N, in
N2N^{2} equal square boxes of a fundamental square. Two points give




0<|Q​λ−P|≤2/N,0<|Q|≤2​N,|λ−P/Q|≤2/|Q|2.0<|Q\lambda-P|\leq\sqrt{2}/N,\qquad 0<|Q|\leq\sqrt{2}N,\qquad|\lambda-P/Q|\leq 2/|Q|^{2}.


The strict positivity in the first inequality follows from
λ∉ℚ⁡(i)\lambda\notin\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}). The absolute approximation errors are at
most 2/N\sqrt{2}/N, since a nonzero Gaussian integer QQ has |Q|≥1|Q|\geq 1.
They therefore tend to zero. After reducing the fractions, the
denominator moduli cannot stay bounded: otherwise the approximants in
a bounded region would belong to a finite set, none equal to λ\lambda.
Reduction also preserves the upper bound 2/|Q|22/|Q|^{2}, with QQ now the
reduced denominator, because reduction only decreases its modulus.
Every exponent less than two therefore occurs
infinitely often. This proves both main theorems except their
rational-power clauses.


Multiplication by a fixed nonzero rational number preserves both
exponents. Indeed, write it as u/vu/v with fixed nonzero integers.
An approximant P/QP/Q becomes u​P/(v​Q)uP/(vQ), whose reduced denominator has
modulus at most |v​Q||vQ| and whose error is multiplied by |u/v||u/v|.
For any exponent ν′<ν\nu^{\prime}<\nu, the fixed factors are absorbed by
|Q|ν−ν′|Q|^{\nu-\nu^{\prime}} for large QQ. Distinct converging approximants
still have unbounded reduced denominators. Applying the same argument
to the inverse multiplier proves equality of exponents.
For real a>0a>0, log⁡(aN)=N​log⁡a\log(a^{N})=N\log a. For any complex branch,
eN​λ=aNe^{N\lambda}=a^{N}, so N​λN\lambda is an admissible fixed logarithm
in Theorem 4.2. These identities prove the clauses.
They also improve the upper bounds to




μ(loga)≤2minN≥1[ℚ(aN):ℚ],μG(λ)≤2minN≥1[ℚ(i,aN):ℚ(i)].\mu(\log a)\leq 2\min_{N\geq 1}[\mathbb{Q}(a^{N}):\mathbb{Q}],\qquad\mu_{G}(\lambda)\leq 2\min_{N\geq 1}[\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i},a^{N}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i})].





9 Why the full algebraic exponent-two claim remains open here


The analytic translation and the varying-center interpolation do
extend. The unresolved step is a sufficiently strong arithmetic
replacement for (6.5). Its leading term is
−d⁡(1−b¯)-d(1-\bar{b}), rather than −(1−b¯)-(1-\bar{b}). A fixed field degree
does not make this difference an error term: all parameters are
fixed before H→∞H\to\infty.


With the displayed parameter scheme, A2<θA^{2}<\theta implies




A−θ1−θ<θ−θ1−θ=θ1+θ<12.\frac{A-\theta}{1-\theta}<\frac{\sqrt{\theta}-\theta}{1-\theta}=\frac{\sqrt{\theta}}{1+\sqrt{\theta}}<\frac{1}{2}.


Thus even with η=0\eta=0 and zero errors, the gap
ν⁡(A−θ)>d⁡(1−θ)\nu(A-\theta)>d(1-\theta) is impossible when ν≤2​d\nu\leq 2d.
This is a limitation of this adaptation, not a disproof of the
conjecture μ⁡(log⁡a)=2\mu(\log a)=2 for all positive algebraic a≠1a\neq 1.


Two potential shortcuts require additional arguments:

1. 

A rational trace of the determinant need not be small merely
because its distinguished embedding is small. The other conjugate
terms have only the weak bounds (6.4).

2. 

Analytic smallness cannot simply be assigned to every conjugate.
For a conjugate τ​a\tau a, choose κ=Log⁡(τ​a)/λ\kappa=\operatorname{Log}(\tau a)/\lambda.
At z=j​λ+xz=j\lambda+x, x=log⁡(1+t)x=\log(1+t), the desired value is




(τ​a)j​(1+t)=eκ​z​e(1−κ)​x.(\tau a)^{j}(1+t)=e^{\kappa z}e^{(1-\kappa)x}.


For a column of YY-degree hh, the additional factor expands as
∑k≥0(1−κ)k​hk​xk/k!\sum_{k\geq 0}(1-\kappa)^{k}h^{k}x^{k}/k!.
It introduces test vectors indexed by both the transverse index
and kk, with kk as large as H/v0H/v_{0}.
The elementary collision count then has O⁡(Hm+1)O(H^{m+1}) possible groups,
instead of O⁡(Hm)O(H^{m}); its previous lower bound no longer forces a
saving of order M​H≍Hm+2MH\asymp H^{m+2}.
This explains why the source’s existing collision lemma does not
by itself remove the norm loss. It does not rule out a stronger lemma.




A proof of the full conjecture must therefore remove the leading
degree loss, compensate for it by an improved analytic estimate,
or reorganize the construction. For example, simply adjoining all
conjugate jet blocks rationalizes the arithmetic, but only the
distinguished block has shifts of size |Qi|−ν|Q_{i}|^{-\nu}; the other
shifts are generally of constant size. That construction also
needs a new estimate.


These observations describe the limitation of the general norm
argument. They do not preclude improvements for fields in which several
embeddings share a compatible logarithmic flow. Those refinements are
developed later in the paper. They also show why a degree-dd argument
cannot be advertised as exponent two merely because dd is fixed.


For example, for φ=(1+5)/2\varphi=(1+\sqrt{5})/2 the bound established in this
part is 2≤μ⁡(log⁡φ)≤42\leq\mu(\log\varphi)\leq 4. No positive power of φ\varphi
is rational: its other conjugate is −φ−1-\varphi^{-1}, and equality
of conjugate positive powers would contradict their different moduli.
The later quadratic normalization theorem does prove
μ⁡(log⁡φ/5)=2\mu(\log\varphi/\sqrt{5})=2. These are different assertions.
Only rational scaling invariance has been proved here; replacing that
scaling by the irrational algebraic factor 5\sqrt{5} is not justified.


Exact formalization scope for this part


The 11 October 2026 core release proves the real and Gaussian
single-logarithm bounds of Theorems 4.1
and 4.2, including the minimum over positive powers
of the argument. It includes exact exponent two for rational and
Gaussian-rational exponential arguments and the corresponding power
cases. The actual varying-center interpolation proposition is supplied
by QuadraticPi.MovingLogUniformInterpolation.movingLogInterpolation;
it is no longer an unproved input to these public endpoints.


The full bounded-fiber occupancy theorem is now supplied by
QuadraticPi.MovingLogOccupancyInterpolation.bounded_fiber_interpolation.
Its distinct-multiplicative-center specialization imposes no additive
separation, and chooses the divisibility modulus before the centers.
The ordinary-real arctangent adapter is supplied by
QuadraticPi/RationalArctangentTheorem.lean.
The Gaussian Dirichlet proof uses the sufficient error constant 44
instead of the sharper constant 22 displayed in the exposition; the
numbered exponent statement is unchanged. Exact source names, hypotheses,
and audit records are given in Appendix A.




10 Normalized logarithms and matching field embeddings


A field norm gives an arithmetic lower bound by multiplying the absolute
values of all conjugates. Its degree cost can be reduced only when the
same determinant has an analytic upper bound at several embeddings.
We formulate the exact matching condition first, including its logarithm
branches. The interpolation ingredients are Theorem 5.1 and
Proposition 5.5, originating in [21]; the new step is the
comparison at several embeddings.


Theorem 10.1 (Partial matching for one normalized logarithm).

Let E⊂ℂE\subset\mathbb{C} be a number field of degree dd, let
α,c∈E×\alpha,c\in E^{\times}, and let x∈ℝ∖{0}x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.
Suppose a set SS of s≥1s\geq 1 embeddings σ:E↪ℂ\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
satisfies




exp⁡(σ⁡(c)​x)=σ⁡(α)(σ∈S).\exp(\sigma(c)x)=\sigma(\alpha)\qquad(\sigma\in S).

(10.1)

Then




2≤μ⁡(x)≤2​d/s.2\leq\mu(x)\leq 2d/s.


In particular, if every embedding satisfies (10.1),
then μ⁡(x)=2\mu(x)=2.



The nonzero logarithm of σ⁡(α)\sigma(\alpha) specified by this condition is
λσ=σ⁡(c)​x\lambda_{\sigma}=\sigma(c)x. It need not be its principal logarithm.
All embeddings are counted, including both members of each complex pair.


Proof.

The Hermite–Lindemann theorem recalled in
Section 3.2 first shows that xx is transcendental: if xx were
nonzero algebraic, then σ⁡(c)​x\sigma(c)x would be nonzero algebraic, whereas
its exponential is algebraic. Consequently the ordinary irrationality
exponent applies and Lemma 3.1 gives the lower bound two.


Put D=d/sD=d/s. Fix ν>2​D\nu>2D and choose 2​D<ρ<ν2D<\rho<\nu. Suppose there
are infinitely many reduced fractions pi/qip_{i}/q_{i}, qi>0q_{i}>0, with




|x−pi/qi|≤qi−ν.|x-p_{i}/q_{i}|\leq q_{i}^{-\nu}.


Their denominators are unbounded. By discarding finitely many, we may
assume pi≠0p_{i}\neq 0 and, simultaneously for the finitely many good embeddings,




ri=c​pi/qi,wi=⌈log⁡qi⌉,|σ⁡(ri)−λσ|≤qi−ρ.r_{i}=cp_{i}/q_{i},\qquad w_{i}=\lceil\log q_{i}\rceil,\qquad|\sigma(r_{i})-\lambda_{\sigma}|\leq q_{i}^{-\rho}.


The dimension and the number of chosen fractions will be fixed before
the final polynomial degree tends to infinity.


Choose the positive rational parameters m,K,w0,v0,θm,K,w_{0},v_{0},\theta of the
one-torus interpolation construction, where




0<θ<1,K​θm<1,K⁡(w0/v0)​θm<1.0<\theta<1,\qquad K\theta^{m}<1,\qquad K(w_{0}/v_{0})\theta^{m}<1.


Once these parameters have been fixed, the fractions are chosen
successively so that their wiw_{i} exceed the separated-weight thresholds.
Write w⋅γ=∑iwi​γiw\cdot\gamma=\sum_{i}w_{i}\gamma_{i} and w∗=mini⁡wiw_{*}=\min_{i}w_{i}.
For a fixed F0>2/θF_{0}>2/\theta, set




Ti=⌈F0​wi/v0⌉,Gi​(t)=∑1≤k<Ti(−1)k+1​tkk,Li=lcm⁡(1,…,Ti−1).T_{i}=\lceil F_{0}w_{i}/v_{0}\rceil,\quad G_{i}(t)=\sum_{1\leq k<T_{i}}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}t^{k}}{k},\quad L_{i}=\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,T_{i}-1).


An empty least common multiple is one. The columns of the coefficient
matrix are P=Yh​XγP=Y^{h}X^{\gamma} with w0​h+w⋅γ≤Hw_{0}h+w\cdot\gamma\leq H; its rows
are all (j,k,β)(j,k,\beta) satisfying




0≤j<K,v0​k+w⋅β/θ<H.0\leq j<K,\qquad v_{0}k+w\cdot\beta/\theta<H.


Its entry in that row and column is




[tk​uβ]​αj​h​(1+t)h​∏i(j​ri+Gi​(t)+ui)γi.[t^{k}u^{\beta}]\,\alpha^{jh}(1+t)^{h}\prod_{i}(jr_{i}+G_{i}(t)+u_{i})^{\gamma_{i}}.

(10.2)

The centers are (αj,j​r1,…,j​rm)(\alpha^{j},jr_{1},\ldots,jr_{m}). Their torus coordinate
is nonzero and each additive coordinate separates the centers, since
ri≠0r_{i}\neq 0. Repeated values of αj\alpha^{j} cause no difficulty in the
moving-center interpolation theorem. Taylor replacement acts by a
unitriangular filtered row change, as in Lemma 6.1;
the selected full-row minor equals the corresponding full-logarithm minor. Thus, for all sufficiently large
divisible HH, the matrix has a nonzero full-row minor ΔH∈E\Delta_{H}\in E.
Keep its selected columns fixed when applying embeddings. Denote its
size by MM and put




b¯H=1M​H​∑rowsw⋅β,0≤b¯H≤θ.\overline{b}_{H}=\frac{1}{MH}\sum_{\text{rows}}w\cdot\beta,\qquad 0\leq\overline{b}_{H}\leq\theta.




The arithmetic estimate.
Choose integers u0,v1≥1u_{0},v_{1}\geq 1 such that u0​cu_{0}c and v1​αv_{1}\alpha are
algebraic integers. Let




Ji=∑selected columnsγi−∑rowsβi.J_{i}=\sum_{\text{selected columns}}\gamma_{i}-\sum_{\text{rows}}\beta_{i}.


One nonzero determinant term matches each row to a column with
β≤γ\beta\leq\gamma componentwise, so every Ji≥0J_{i}\geq 0. Scale a column
(h,γ)(h,\gamma) by v1(K−1)​h​∏i(u0​qi​Li)γiv_{1}^{(K-1)h}\prod_{i}(u_{0}q_{i}L_{i})^{\gamma_{i}} and a
row β\beta by ∏i(u0​qi​Li)−βi\prod_{i}(u_{0}q_{i}L_{i})^{-\beta_{i}}. The scaled entries
are algebraic integers: the additive factors have coefficients in
𝒪E\mathcal{O}_{E} because




u0​qi​Li​(j​ri+Gi​(t))=j​pi​(u0​c)​Li+u0​qi​Li​Gi​(t),u_{0}q_{i}L_{i}(jr_{i}+G_{i}(t))=jp_{i}(u_{0}c)L_{i}+u_{0}q_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(t),


and the torus factor is
v1(K−1−j)​h​(v1​α)j​hv_{1}^{(K-1-j)h}(v_{1}\alpha)^{jh}. It follows that




CH​ΔH∈𝒪E∖{0},CH=v1(K−1)​∑colh​∏i(u0​qi​Li)Ji∈ℤ>0.C_{H}\Delta_{H}\in\mathcal{O}_{E}\setminus\{0\},\qquad C_{H}=v_{1}^{(K-1)\sum_{\rm col}h}\prod_{i}(u_{0}q_{i}L_{i})^{J_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.


Let Λ=4​log⁡2\Lambda=4\log 2. The elementary lcm estimate and the weighted
degree bounds give




log⁡Li\displaystyle\log L_{i}
≤Λ​F0​wi/v0,\displaystyle\leq\Lambda F_{0}w_{i}/v_{0},



∑iwi​Ji\displaystyle\sum_{i}w_{i}J_{i}
≤M​H​(1−b¯H),∑colh≤M​H/w0,\displaystyle\leq MH(1-\overline{b}_{H}),\qquad\sum_{\rm col}h\leq MH/w_{0},



log⁡CHM​H\displaystyle\frac{\log C_{H}}{MH}
≤1−b¯H+Ear,Ear=Λ​F0v0+log⁡u0w∗+(K−1)​log⁡v1w0.\displaystyle\leq 1-\overline{b}_{H}+E_{\rm ar},\quad E_{\rm ar}=\frac{\Lambda F_{0}}{v_{0}}+\frac{\log u_{0}}{w_{*}}+\frac{(K-1)\log v_{1}}{w_{0}}.

(10.3)

Taking the nonzero integer norm yields the exact lower bound




∑σ:E↪ℂlog⁡|σ⁡(ΔH)|M​H≥−d(1−b¯H)−dEar.\sum_{\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\frac{\log|\sigma(\Delta_{H})|}{MH}\geq-d(1-\overline{b}_{H})-dE_{\rm ar}.

(10.4)



Good embeddings.
For a selected column PP use the entire row-vector families




fa,P​(z)=[ua]​P​(ez,z+u1,…,z+um).f_{a,P}(z)=[u^{a}]P(e^{z},z+u_{1},\ldots,z+u_{m}).


At a good embedding substitute
z=j​λσ+log⁡(1+t)z=j\lambda_{\sigma}+\log(1+t). The exponential identity and the
additive identity are exactly




ez=σ​(α)j​(1+t),j​σ​(ri)+log⁡(1+t)+ui=z+ui+j⁡(σ⁡(ri)−λσ).e^{z}=\sigma(\alpha)^{j}(1+t),\qquad j\sigma(r_{i})+\log(1+t)+u_{i}=z+u_{i}+j(\sigma(r_{i})-\lambda_{\sigma}).


We use the full-logarithm minor here, which equals (10.2)
by the preceding unitriangular change. There is therefore one common coefficient family for all rows at a
given embedding, with precisely the same transverse grades as in the
one-logarithm collision lemma. Let




R=4​K​(1+maxσ∈S⁡|λσ|),κ=log⁡24,L=η2​K​θm(m+1)​v0​Am.R=4K\bigl(1+\max_{\sigma\in S}|\lambda_{\sigma}|\bigr),\quad\kappa=\frac{\log 2}{4},\quad L=\frac{\eta^{2}K\theta^{m}}{(m+1)v_{0}A^{m}}.


Lemma 7.1 and the translation estimate give, uniformly for σ∈S\sigma\in S,




log⁡|σ⁡(ΔH)|M​H≤Ean+o⁡(1)+max⁡{−κ​L+o⁡(1),−ρ⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯H)},\frac{\log|\sigma(\Delta_{H})|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm an}+o(1)+\max\{-\kappa L+o(1),-\rho(A(1-\eta)-\overline{b}_{H})\},

(10.5)

where a sufficient error bound is




Ean=2​log⁡2v0+Rw0+log⁡4+log⁡K+ρ+log⁡(2​R)w∗.E_{\rm an}=\frac{2\log 2}{v_{0}}+\frac{R}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log 4+\log K+\rho+\log(2R)}{w_{*}}.


All o⁡(1)o(1) terms here are for H→∞H\to\infty after every other datum is fixed.


Other embeddings.
The values σ⁡(ri)=σ⁡(c)​pi/qi\sigma(r_{i})=\sigma(c)p_{i}/q_{i} stay bounded, because
pi/qi→xp_{i}/q_{i}\to x. On |t|=1/2|t|=1/2 the GiG_{i} are uniformly bounded.
Cauchy’s coefficient bound applied directly to (10.2),
followed by Hadamard’s inequality, gives




log⁡|σ⁡(ΔH)|M​H≤Ebad+o⁡(1),Ebad=O⁡(Kw0+1v0+1+log⁡Kw∗).\frac{\log|\sigma(\Delta_{H})|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm bad}+o(1),\qquad E_{\rm bad}=O\left(\frac{K}{w_{0}}+\frac{1}{v_{0}}+\frac{1+\log K}{w_{*}}\right).

(10.6)

The fixed implied constant depends on the field and endpoint data.
Indeed h≤H/w0h\leq H/w_{0}, k≤H/v0k\leq H/v_{0}, ∑iγi≤H/w∗\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}\leq H/w_{*},
and the determinant factor contributes only log⁡M/H=o⁡(1)\log M/H=o(1).
This estimate does not claim analytic smallness at a bad embedding.


Combining (10.4)–(10.6) and dividing by ss,
the arithmetic leading term is −D⁡(1−b¯H)-D(1-\overline{b}_{H}). It suffices to have




g\displaystyle g
=ρ⁡(A⁡(1−η)−θ)−D⁡(1−θ)>0,\displaystyle=\rho(A(1-\eta)-\theta)-D(1-\theta)>0,



E\displaystyle E
=D​Ear+Ean+(D−1)​Ebad<g,κ​L>D+E.\displaystyle=DE_{\rm ar}+E_{\rm an}+(D-1)E_{\rm bad}<g,\qquad\kappa L>D+E.

(10.7)

For the approximation alternative the worst value of
b¯H\overline{b}_{H} is θ\theta, because ρ>D\rho>D. For the collision
alternative the inequality 1−b¯H≤11-\overline{b}_{H}\leq 1 suffices.


Here is the order of choices verifying (10.7). Choose rational
b0b_{0} with 1/2<b0<1−D/ρ1/2<b_{0}<1-D/\rho and small rational δ>0\delta>0; put
θ=1−δ\theta=1-\delta, A=1−b0​δA=1-b_{0}\delta. Then
A2<θA^{2}<\theta and ρ⁡(A−θ)>D⁡(1−θ)\rho(A-\theta)>D(1-\theta). Choose η>0\eta>0
small enough to keep a strict gap, and fix any rational F0>2/θF_{0}>2/\theta. Choose rational B,CB,C so that




A<B<θ,B/θ<C<1/B.A<B<\sqrt{\theta},\qquad B/\theta<C<1/B.


In particular C>1C>1, C​B<1CB<1, and B<C​θ<1B<C\theta<1. Set




K=⌊Cm⌋,w0=B−m,v0=2​K​θm​w0.K=\lfloor C^{m}\rfloor,\quad w_{0}=B^{-m},\quad v_{0}=2K\theta^{m}w_{0}.


As m→∞m\to\infty, K/w0→0K/w_{0}\to 0, v0→∞v_{0}\to\infty, and




L=η2​(B/A)m2​(m+1)→∞.L=\frac{\eta^{2}(B/A)^{m}}{2(m+1)}\to\infty.


First fix a sufficiently large finite mm. Next choose the mm good
fractions successively so that all geometric thresholds and all w∗−1w_{*}^{-1}
errors are satisfied. Finally let HH increase through the required
multiples. The strict inequalities contradict (10.4).
Thus no ν>2​d/s\nu>2d/s can occur infinitely often.
∎



Completed Lean theorem.


The exact bound of Theorem 10.1, with an actual nonempty
finite set of matching embeddings, is
QuadraticPi.partialScalarMatchingLogarithm_irrationalityExponent_bounds.
Its all-embedding endpoint is
QuadraticPi.scalarMatchingLogarithm_irrationalityExponent_eq_two.
Both full types and transitive axiom lists were rechecked for this revision.
In particular, when 2​d≤3​s2d\leq 3s this already formalized theorem gives a
bound in the range 2≤μ⁡(x)≤32\leq\mu(x)\leq 3.



10.1 Quadratic logarithms, circular values, and rational linear closure


Corollary 10.2.

Let D>0D>0 be rational and nonsquare, and let α∈ℚ⁡(D)\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})
be positive, different from 11, and have norm 11 or −1-1. Then




μ⁡(log⁡αD)=2.\mu\left(\frac{\log\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)=2.





Proof.

For norm one take c=Dc=\sqrt{D} and x=log⁡α/Dx=\log\alpha/\sqrt{D}.
The two logarithms are log⁡α\log\alpha and −log⁡α-\log\alpha, because
the conjugate of α\alpha is α−1\alpha^{-1}. Thus both embeddings
are good. For norm minus one replace α\alpha by α2\alpha^{2} and
cc by 2​D2\sqrt{D}, keeping xx unchanged.
∎



In particular, with φ=(1+5)/2\varphi=(1+\sqrt{5})/2,




μ⁡(log⁡(1+2)2)=2,μ⁡(log⁡φ5)=2.\mu\left(\frac{\log(1+\sqrt{2})}{\sqrt{2}}\right)=2,\qquad\mu\left(\frac{\log\varphi}{\sqrt{5}}\right)=2.


The algebraic normalizations are part of the conclusions. Invariance
under nonzero rational scaling does not license removing a
factor D\sqrt{D}. In the determinant proof the normalization has
an explicit function: conjugation changes both D\sqrt{D} and the chosen
logarithm to their negatives, so the approximation error is small at
both embeddings. Already in the norm-one case, for unnormalized
rational approximants to log⁡α\log\alpha, conjugation fixes the approximants while negating the
logarithm. The second error would then tend to 2​log⁡α2\log\alpha, which
is nonzero. The averaged small-error argument would no longer apply.


For D∈ℚ×D\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}, choosing D=i​−D\sqrt{D}=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{-D} when D<0D<0, let




fD​(z)=1D​log⁡1+D​z1−D​z=2​∑n≥0Dn​z2​n+12​n+1f_{D}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}\log\frac{1+\sqrt{D}\,z}{1-\sqrt{D}\,z}=2\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{D^{n}z^{2n+1}}{2n+1}

(10.8)

denote its branch at zero. For D>0D>0 and real zz, use
|D​z|<1|\sqrt{D}\,z|<1; for D<0D<0 use its continuous real branch
fD​(z)=2​arctan⁡(−D​z)/−Df_{D}(z)=2\arctan(\sqrt{-D}\,z)/\sqrt{-D} for all real zz.
In either case (1−D​z2)​fD′​(z)=2(1-Dz^{2})f_{D}^{\prime}(z)=2.


Corollary 10.3.

For fixed D∈ℚ×D\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}, every nonzero rational linear combination
of finitely many admissible rational values fD​(r)f_{D}(r) has irrationality
exponent two. If D<0D<0, the combination may also include the value
π/−D\pi/\sqrt{-D}. In particular




μ⁡(fD​(r))=2(r≠0),μ⁡(π/D)=2(D∈ℚ>0).\mu(f_{D}(r))=2\quad(r\neq 0),\qquad\mu(\pi/\sqrt{D})=2\quad(D\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}).





Proof.

For a nonzero combination xx, choose an integer n≥1n\geq 1 clearing
its rational coefficients. Then exp⁡(n​D​x)=α\exp(n\sqrt{D}\,x)=\alpha is a
product of integer powers of the algebraic torus coordinates in
(10.8); the circular value contributes an integer power of −1-1.
If ℚ⁡(D)\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}) is quadratic, its other embedding sends D\sqrt{D}
to −D-\sqrt{D} and α\alpha to α−1\alpha^{-1}, so both exponential
identities hold with c=n​Dc=n\sqrt{D}. If DD is a positive rational
square the field is ℚ\mathbb{Q} and only its identity embedding is needed.
Apply Theorem 10.1 with all embeddings good.
For the last circular assertion use E=ℚ⁡(−D)E=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-D}), c=i​Dc=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{D},
α=−1\alpha=-1 and x=π/Dx=\pi/\sqrt{D} directly.
∎



For orientation, the exact identities




f1​(1/3)=log⁡2,12​f2​(1/2)=log⁡(1+2)2,12​f5​(1/5)=log⁡φ5f_{1}(1/3)=\log 2,\quad\tfrac{1}{2}f_{2}(1/2)=\frac{\log(1+\sqrt{2})}{\sqrt{2}},\quad\tfrac{1}{2}f_{5}(1/5)=\frac{\log\varphi}{\sqrt{5}}


put familiar constants in one differential family. The fixed-DD
restriction and the exclusion of zero are essential. The torus identity
behind the addition formula is rational, but logarithmic addition must
retain its chosen branches; arbitrary principal-branch replacements
after successive additions would lose periods.



10.2 The invariant field and the limit of exact scalar matching


Theorem 10.4.

Let α,c\alpha,c be nonzero algebraic numbers and let x∈ℝ∖{0}x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
satisfy ec​x=αe^{cx}=\alpha. Set




E=ℚ⁡(α,c),K=ℚ⁡(c2,α+α−1,c⁡(α−α−1)).E=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,c),\qquad K=\mathbb{Q}\bigl(c^{2},\alpha+\alpha^{-1},c(\alpha-\alpha^{-1})\bigr).


Then




μ(x)≤2[K:ℚ].\mu(x)\leq 2[K:\mathbb{Q}].


Moreover the good embeddings of this minimal field EE are exactly
its embeddings fixing KK, and their number is [E:K]∈{1,2}[E:K]\in\{1,2\}.



Proof.

Write S=α+α−1S=\alpha+\alpha^{-1} and T=c⁡(α−α−1)T=c(\alpha-\alpha^{-1}).
Since α=(S+T/c)/2\alpha=(S+T/c)/2, one has E=K⁡(c)E=K(c) and [E:K]≤2[E:K]\leq 2.
If the index is two, the nontrivial KK-automorphism sends
(c,α)(c,\alpha) to (−c,α−1)(-c,\alpha^{-1}); both it and the identity satisfy
the exponential matching condition. If the index is one, the identity
alone suffices. Theorem 10.1 gives the displayed bound.


To classify all good embeddings, suppose σ\sigma is good. If
α≠1\alpha\neq 1, then




σ⁡(α)=exp⁡((σ⁡(c)/c)​c​x),c​x≠0.\sigma(\alpha)=\exp\bigl((\sigma(c)/c)\,cx\bigr),\qquad cx\neq 0.


The Gel’fond–Schneider theorem [5, Chapters 1–2], used with this
specific logarithm c​xcx of α\alpha, forces σ⁡(c)/c∈ℚ\sigma(c)/c\in\mathbb{Q}.
If α=1\alpha=1, the two logarithms c​xcx and σ⁡(c)​x\sigma(c)x are nonzero
integer multiples of 2​π​i2\pi\mathrm{i}, and their ratio is again rational.
Extend σ\sigma to an automorphism τ\tau of a normal closure. If
τ⁡(c)=r​c\tau(c)=rc with rational rr, the finite order of τ\tau gives
rn=1r^{n}=1 for some nn, hence r=1r=1 or −1-1. Thus a good embedding
has exactly one of the two actions




(c,α)⟼(c,α),(c,α)⟼(−c,α−1).(c,\alpha)\longmapsto(c,\alpha),\qquad(c,\alpha)\longmapsto(-c,\alpha^{-1}).


These elements generate EE. Both actions fix KK, and every
KK-embedding has one of these actions and is good. This proves the claim.
∎



Lean scope.


The degree bound, the index-one-or-two statement under minimal generation,
and both directions of the classification are now checked. The converse
uses the explicit standard Gel’fond–Schneider interface in
QuadraticPi.ScalarMatchingInvariantConverse.invariantField_theorem.
The direct degree bound does not require that converse. The all-matching
rigidity consequence has its own checked endpoint.


An immediate rigidity statement is useful: if all embeddings of
some number field containing α,c\alpha,c are good, then
c2∈ℚc^{2}\in\mathbb{Q} and α∈ℚ⁡(c)\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}(c). Indeed every automorphism of a
normal closure sends cc to ±c\pm c, so fixes c2c^{2}; every automorphism
fixing cc also fixes α=ec​x\alpha=e^{cx}. Exact all-embedding matching
therefore descends to degree at most two. It is stronger than matching
absolute-value logarithms, and it is not a mechanism for arbitrarily
high-degree examples. The statement about at most two good embeddings
uses the minimal field E=ℚ⁡(α,c)E=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,c); in a larger ambient
field the restrictions can have multiple extensions.


Corollary 10.5 (Reciprocal trace degree).

For every positive algebraic α≠1\alpha\neq 1,




μ(log⁡αα−α−1)≤2[ℚ(α+α−1):ℚ].\mu\left(\frac{\log\alpha}{\alpha-\alpha^{-1}}\right)\leq 2[\mathbb{Q}(\alpha+\alpha^{-1}):\mathbb{Q}].


Equivalently, for real algebraic t>2t>2 of degree mm,




μ⁡(log⁡((t+t2−4)/2)t2−4)≤2​m.\mu\left(\frac{\log((t+\sqrt{t^{2}-4})/2)}{\sqrt{t^{2}-4}}\right)\leq 2m.





Proof.

Take c=α−α−1c=\alpha-\alpha^{-1} in Theorem 10.4.
Then c2=(α+α−1)2−4c^{2}=(\alpha+\alpha^{-1})^{2}-4 and
c⁡(α−α−1)=c2c(\alpha-\alpha^{-1})=c^{2}, so K=ℚ⁡(α+α−1)K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha+\alpha^{-1}).
Replacing α\alpha by α−1\alpha^{-1} leaves the target unchanged;
we can therefore choose the root above one in the equivalent formulation.
∎



For a reciprocal irreducible minimal polynomial of degree 2​m2m,
the map α↦α−1\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{-1} is a nontrivial involution,
whose fixed field is ℚ⁡(α+α−1)\mathbb{Q}(\alpha+\alpha^{-1}); the trace degree is
mm. For example,




α=2+2+2+4​22\alpha=\frac{2+\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{2+4\sqrt{2}}}{2}


has irreducible polynomial X4−4​X3+4​X2−4​X+1X^{4}-4X^{3}+4X^{2}-4X+1. To check
irreducibility, the only possible rational roots are ±1\pm 1, neither
a root. A factorization into monic integral quadratics has constants
both 11 or both −1-1. Constants 11 would require integer roots of
u2+4​u+2u^{2}+4u+2, and constants −1-1 force opposite cubic and linear
coefficients. Both are impossible. Its trace is 2+22+\sqrt{2}, giving




μ⁡(log⁡((2+2+2+4​2)/2)2+4​2)≤4.\mu\left(\frac{\log((2+\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{2+4\sqrt{2}})/2)}{\sqrt{2+4\sqrt{2}}}\right)\leq 4.




Corollary 10.6 (Quadratic character combinations).

Let E/ℚE/\mathbb{Q} be a finite totally real Galois extension with group GG,
fix a real embedding ϕ:E↪ℝ\phi:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}, and let
χ:G→{1,−1}\chi:G\to\{1,-1\} be a nontrivial character. There is a positive
nonsquare rational DχD_{\chi}, chosen independently of the input aa,
such that




ϕ⁡(Eker⁡χ)=ℚ⁡(Dχ).\phi(E^{\ker\chi})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D_{\chi}}).


For every a∈Ea\in E with ϕ⁡(g⁡(a))>0\phi(g(a))>0 for all g∈Gg\in G, put




βχ​(a)=∏g∈Gg​(a)χ⁡(g).\beta_{\chi}(a)=\prod_{g\in G}g(a)^{\chi(g)}.


If βχ​(a)≠1\beta_{\chi}(a)\neq 1, then




μ⁡(1Dχ​∑g∈Gχ⁡(g)​log⁡ϕ⁡(g⁡(a)))=2.\mu\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{D_{\chi}}}\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)\log\phi(g(a))\right)=2.


The same conclusion holds for a specified positive nonsquare rational
DχD_{\chi} satisfying the displayed equality of real subfields.



Proof.

The kernel has index two, so its fixed field is quadratic. A real
quadratic field is the field generated by the positive square root of
some positive nonsquare rational; this choice is made before aa.
Reindexing the signed product gives
h⁡(βχ)=βχχ⁡(h)h(\beta_{\chi})=\beta_{\chi}^{\chi(h)}. The product thus lies in
the quadratic fixed field and its nontrivial conjugate is its inverse,
so its field norm is one. Positivity of all factors gives




log⁡ϕ⁡(βχ)=∑g∈Gχ⁡(g)​log⁡ϕ⁡(g⁡(a)).\log\phi(\beta_{\chi})=\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)\log\phi(g(a)).


The product-not-one condition makes this logarithm nonzero. Apply
Corollary 10.2 in the compatible real quadratic
realization. The full proof, including the realization and norm
transport, is given in Theorem 14.7.
∎



This is a result about the displayed normalized character sum.
It does not assert exponent two for arbitrary unnormalized logarithmic
combinations. If a negative-character automorphism fixes aa, the
positive and negative product factors pair and cancel, giving
βχ​(a)=1\beta_{\chi}(a)=1. More generally, a nontrivial product requires
Eker⁡χ⊆ℚ⁡(a)E^{\ker\chi}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}(a), and consequently [ℚ(a):ℚ][\mathbb{Q}(a):\mathbb{Q}]
must be even. In particular, positive inputs of odd degree have zero
character logarithmic sum; see Proposition 14.8.



Lean scope.


This corollary is now a completed Lean endpoint, including the actual
product, quadratic degree, norm one, positive logarithmic identity and
compatible square-root realization. The declaration
QuadraticPi.QuadraticCharacterExponent.exists_logSum_irrationalityExponent_eq_two
chooses DχD_{\chi} and the compatible field isomorphism before all
admissible aa. It assumes neither an unproved quadratic identification
nor an extra analytic interpolation statement. The specified-field
variant is logSum_irrationalityExponent_eq_two_of_fieldRange
in the same namespace. No canonical integral or squarefree choice of
DχD_{\chi} is claimed. The later stabilizer and input-degree cancellation
statements are also compiled. Exact source declarations and recorded
build/axiom evidence are listed in Section 14.8
and Appendix A.





11 Ratios of two logarithms: two torus coordinates


Theorem 11.1.

Let a,b>0a,b>0 be rational and multiplicatively independent. Then




2≤μ⁡(log⁡alog⁡b)≤3.2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\log a}{\log b}\right)\leq 3.


In particular μ⁡(log⁡2/log⁡3)≤3\mu(\log 2/\log 3)\leq 3.



Formal verification.


The theorem is exported as
QuadraticPi.rationalLogRatio_irrationalityExponent_bounds
in QuadraticPi/RationalLogRatioTheorem.lean.
The actual two-torus interpolation and determinant estimates are proved;
no interpolation premise remains in this public endpoint.
The verified conclusion is the bound 2≤μ≤32\leq\mu\leq 3.
See Appendix A for dependencies and compiler evidence.
The completion claim in this paragraph is specific to the rational
ratio theorem and its rational homogeneous two-logarithm consequence.
Its source chain consists of the TwoLog* developments and
these public rational endpoints; it does not identify the more general
number-field comparisons below as completed Lean theorems.


We prove the case (a,b)=(2,3)(a,b)=(2,3) in detail, and explain the rational
denominator changes at the end. Put
ξ=log⁡2/log⁡3\xi=\log 2/\log 3. Unique factorization proves ξ∉ℚ\xi\notin\mathbb{Q}.
To exclude exponent ν>3\nu>3, suppose there are arbitrarily good
approximations




ri=pi/qi,|ξ−ri|≤qi−ν,wi=⌈log⁡qi⌉.r_{i}=p_{i}/q_{i},\qquad|\xi-r_{i}|\leq q_{i}^{-\nu},\qquad w_{i}=\lceil\log q_{i}\rceil.

(11.1)

The fractions can be chosen distinct, nonzero, reduced, and with
successively large denominators. All parameters and fractions will
be fixed before a final polynomial degree HH tends to infinity.
Here multiplicative independence means that
au​bv=1a^{u}b^{v}=1, for (u,v)∈ℤ2(u,v)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}, implies u=v=0u=v=0.
In particular a≠1a\neq 1 and b≠1b\neq 1, so both real logarithms are
nonzero. If log⁡a/log⁡b=p/q\log a/\log b=p/q, with q≥1q\geq 1, then
q​log⁡a−p​log⁡b=0q\log a-p\log b=0 and hence aq​b−p=1a^{q}b^{-p}=1, a contradiction.
Thus the theorem concerns an irrational real number, and its lower
bound is the ordinary real Dirichlet bound.


To justify the contradiction formulation, if μ⁡(ξ)>3\mu(\xi)>3, choose
3<ν<μ⁡(ξ)3<\nu<\mu(\xi) and obtain infinitely many rational approximations
satisfying (11.1). Their reduced denominators are
unbounded: a bounded interval contains only finitely many reduced
fractions of bounded denominator. Reduction cannot spoil the inequality,
since replacing a denominator by a smaller one increases its negative
power. Discarding finitely many terms gives nonzero, pairwise distinct
slopes and permits each next denominator to exceed any fixed threshold.
The weights wi=⌈log⁡qi⌉w_{i}=\lceil\log q_{i}\rceil are positive integers. We use
both sides of the elementary relation
wi−1<log⁡qi≤wiw_{i}-1<\log q_{i}\leq w_{i}; its lower side accounts for the ceiling loss
in the analytic estimate.



11.1 The new interpolation statement


Throughout this section write w⋅a=∑iwi​aiw\cdot a=\sum_{i}w_{i}a_{i} and
w∗=mini⁡wiw_{*}=\min_{i}w_{i}. The ambient coordinates are Y,Z,X1,…,XmY,Z,X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}, m≥2m\geq 2.
Give them degree weights
W=(w0,w0,w1,…,wm)W=(w_{0},w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}).
At centers




(Y,Z,X)=(3j,2j,0),0≤j<K,(Y,Z,X)=(3^{j},2^{j},0),\qquad 0\leq j<K,

(11.2)

use local coordinates




t=Y/3j−1,s=Z/2j−1,ui=Xi−log⁡(1+s)+ri​log⁡(1+t),t=Y/3^{j}-1,\quad s=Z/2^{j}-1,\quad u_{i}=X_{i}-\log(1+s)+r_{i}\log(1+t),


with weights V=(v,v,w1/θ,…,wm/θ)V=(v,v,w_{1}/\theta,\ldots,w_{m}/\theta).


Lemma 11.2.

Let K≥1K\geq 1, m≥2m\geq 2, 0<θ<10<\theta<1, and w0,v>0w_{0},v>0 be rational.
Assume there is a rational σ>0\sigma>0 with





(1+3​σ)m+2​K​(w0/v)2​θm<1,\displaystyle(1+3\sigma)^{m+2}K(w_{0}/v)^{2}\theta^{m}<1,




(1+3​σ)m+1​(w0/v)​θm−1<1,\displaystyle(1+3\sigma)^{m+1}(w_{0}/v)\theta^{m-1}<1,




(1+3​σ)m−1​θm−1<1,(1+σ)​θ<1,\displaystyle(1+3\sigma)^{m-1}\theta^{m-1}<1,\qquad(1+\sigma)\theta<1,




w0/v>4​(m+3)/σ.\displaystyle w_{0}/v>4(m+3)/\sigma.

(11.3)

There are successive thresholds for w1,…,wmw_{1},\ldots,w_{m}, independent
of the distinct nonzero rational slopes rir_{i}, such that the map




P⟼[P⁡(3j​(1+t),2j​(1+s),(log⁡(1+s)−ri​log⁡(1+t)+ui)i)]j<KP\longmapsto\left[P\bigl(3^{j}(1+t),2^{j}(1+s),(\log(1+s)-r_{i}\log(1+t)+u_{i})_{i}\bigr)\right]_{j<K}

(11.4)

from polynomials of WW-degree at most HH onto all coefficient
packets of weight less than HH is surjective for all sufficiently
large divisible HH.



The target is the direct sum over the KK centers of the finite quotients
ℚ⁡[[t,s,u]]/FV≥H\mathbb{Q}[[t,s,u]]/F_{V}^{\geq H}, with basis the monomials
tλ​sρ​uβt^{\lambda}s^{\rho}u^{\beta} of weight strictly less than HH.
Equivalently one can first work over ℂ\mathbb{C}: because the resulting finite
matrix has rational entries, full row rank over ℂ\mathbb{C} is full row rank
over ℚ\mathbb{Q}. The quantifiers are important. The base data
(m,K,θ,w0,v,σ)(m,K,\theta,w_{0},v,\sigma) are fixed first. Each threshold for wiw_{i}
depends only on those data and the earlier weights. After all weights
and all slopes have been fixed, the degree may be required to exceed
a threshold and to run through multiples of a fixed denominator.
Neither the slopes nor this final degree enter the successive weight
thresholds.


Proof.

Put L0=m+3L_{0}=m+3. Label the torus directions Y,ZY,Z below every positive
index 1,…,m1,\ldots,m. Write WY=WZ=w0W_{Y}=W_{Z}=w_{0}, VY=VZ=vV_{Y}=V_{Z}=v,
Wi=wiW_{i}=w_{i}, and Vi=wi/θV_{i}=w_{i}/\theta for i>0i>0. Impose




∏h∈AWh>C∗​∏h∈BVh,C∗=max1≤k≤m+1⁡2​k!​(L0/σ)k,\prod_{h\in A}W_{h}>C_{*}\prod_{h\in B}V_{h},\qquad C_{*}=\max_{1\leq k\leq m+1}2k!(L_{0}/\sigma)^{k},

(11.5)

whenever |A|=|B||A|=|B| lies between 11 and m+1m+1, some positive index
lies in A△BA\mathbin{\triangle}B, and the largest such index belongs
to AA. The two torus indices are tied: no condition is imposed merely
because they differ. This causes no conflict in the successive choice.
For largest differing positive index ii, the ratio of the two products
is wiw_{i} times a positive expression in previously chosen weights and
fixed torus weights. Higher common indices contribute the fixed factor
θ\theta; all other higher indices are absent. A sufficiently large
wiw_{i} therefore meets every new inequality and preserves all earlier
ones. Enlarge these thresholds to meet the one-torus separation
conditions of (5.5) after deleting any one positive index. This is possible for
all the finitely many deletions at once and is independent of rir_{i}.


For an irreducible algebraic curve CC meeting the open chart Y​Z≠0YZ\neq 0,
on its complete smooth model define




dW​(C)=∑Pmax⁡(0,−ordP⁡Yw0,−ordP⁡Zw0,−ordP⁡X1w1,…,−ordP⁡Xmwm),d_{W}(C)=\sum_{P}\max\left(0,-\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}Y}{w_{0}},-\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}Z}{w_{0}},-\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}X_{1}}{w_{1}},\ldots,-\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}X_{m}}{w_{m}}\right),


and at each branch through a center put




hP=min⁡(ordP⁡tv,ordP⁡sv,mini⁡ordP⁡uiwi/θ).h_{P}=\min\left(\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}t}{v},\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}s}{v},\min_{i}\frac{\operatorname{ord}_{P}u_{i}}{w_{i}/\theta}\right).


We prove dW​(C)≥(1+σ)​∑PhPd_{W}(C)\geq(1+\sigma)\sum_{P}h_{P}.
Suppose otherwise. The leading dimension count (11.3) gives,
for every sufficiently large NN, a nonzero polynomial of WW-degree
at most NN having contact at least (1+3​σ)​N(1+3\sigma)N at all centers.
The commuting, degree-preserving frame




DY=Y∂Y−∑iri∂Xi,DZ=Z∂Z+∑i∂Xi,Di=∂XiD_{Y}=Y\partial_{Y}-\sum_{i}r_{i}\partial_{X_{i}},\quad D_{Z}=Z\partial_{Z}+\sum_{i}\partial_{X_{i}},\quad D_{i}=\partial_{X_{i}}


is (1+t)∂t,(1+s)∂s,∂ui(1+t)\partial_{t},(1+s)\partial_{s},\partial_{u_{i}} locally.
The zero–pole comparison therefore forces all derivatives of cost
at most σ​N\sigma N to vanish on CC.
In detail, differentiating by a frame vector of cost VhV_{h} lowers
local weighted contact by at most VhV_{h} and does not increase polynomial
WW-degree. A derivative of total cost at most σ​N\sigma N therefore
has local contact at least (1+2​σ)​N(1+2\sigma)N. If its restriction to CC
were nonzero, its total zero order would be at least
(1+2​σ)​N​∑PhP(1+2\sigma)N\sum_{P}h_{P}, whereas its total pole order would be at
most N​dW​(C)Nd_{W}(C). The assumed strict failure of the curve inequality
makes the former strictly greater than the latter. A nonzero rational
function on a complete smooth curve has equal total zero and pole
orders, giving the contradiction. This also explains why the spare
contact margin is kept before the persistent-component argument.


For derivative-cost steps δ=σ​N/L0\delta=\sigma N/L_{0}, take nested irreducible
components containing CC backwards through the zero sets of derivatives
of cost at most r​δr\delta, 0≤r≤L00\leq r\leq L_{0}. Their dimensions are between
11 and m+1m+1, so two adjacent components coincide. More explicitly, strict inclusion
of irreducible components would strictly lower dimension, and there
are more stages than the available positive dimensions. Call a persistent
component TT, and let its codimension be kk. Lemma 5.4, the weighted
transverse multiplicity estimate, gives, for every coordinate normal basis
AA and every frame normal basis BB,




∏h∈AWh≤2​k!​(L0/σ)k​∏h∈BVh.\prod_{h\in A}W_{h}\leq 2k!(L_{0}/\sigma)^{k}\prod_{h\in B}V_{h}.

(11.6)

These bases are evaluated on a common generic smooth open subset of TT.
For the application of the multiplicity lemma, use the equations from
the earlier of the equal adjacent stages. Any further derivative of
cost less than δ\delta belongs to the equations of the next stage,
so vanishes on the same component. Both stages are considered inside
Y​Z≠0YZ\neq 0, where the displayed fields are a frame. The initial zero
set is proper because its defining polynomial is nonzero, and every
component in the chain contains the curve; consequently
1≤k≤m+11\leq k\leq m+1. Thus the constants used in
(11.5) cover every possible persistent component.


If all positive coordinate directions are tangent to TT, then
k≤2k\leq 2. Modulo the tangent space, DYD_{Y} and DZD_{Z} are the nonzero
scalar multiples Y∂YY\partial_{Y} and Z∂ZZ\partial_{Z} of their coordinate
directions; the additive terms are tangent. Both types of normal bases
can thus be chosen entirely from the two torus indices. Equation
(11.6) gives




(w0/v)k≤2​k!​(L0/σ)k,(w_{0}/v)^{k}\leq 2k!(L_{0}/\sigma)^{k},


contradicting (11.3) for k=1k=1 or 22.


Otherwise let ii be the largest positive index whose coordinate
direction is nontangent. Its nonzero image in the normal quotient
extends to a coordinate normal basis AA. Every positive index larger
than ii is tangent, hence occurs in no coordinate or frame normal
basis, since Dj=∂XjD_{j}=\partial_{X_{j}} for positive jj. If a frame normal
basis BB omitted ii, the largest positive index in
A△BA\mathbin{\triangle}B would be exactly ii, belonging to AA.
Equation (11.5) would contradict (11.6). Therefore
every frame normal basis contains DiD_{i}.


Consequently the images of all other frame vectors do not span the
normal quotient. Their ambient span is the hyperplane




Qi=ker⁡(d​Xi+ri​d​Y/Y−d​Z/Z).Q_{i}=\ker(dX_{i}+r_{i}\,dY/Y-dZ/Z).


For an ambient hyperplane QQ and a tangent space SS, its image in the
quotient by SS fails to be surjective exactly when S⊆QS\subseteq Q:
if SS contains a vector outside QQ, then Q+SQ+S is the full ambient
space; otherwise Q+S=QQ+S=Q. Thus the tangent space of TT is contained
in QiQ_{i}, giving on TT, with ri=pi/qir_{i}=p_{i}/q_{i} reduced,




qi​d​Xi=d​log⁡(Zqi/Ypi).q_{i}\,dX_{i}=d\log(Z^{q_{i}}/Y^{p_{i}}).


The character on the right is constant on TT. Indeed, if nonconstant,
restrict through a point of nonzero differential to an algebraic curve
and pass to its complete smooth model. A zero or pole of this character
has a nonzero residue in its logarithmic differential, whereas the exact
differential qi​d​Xiq_{i}\,dX_{i} has residue zero. This is impossible. Then
XiX_{i} too is constant. Its value on CC is zero, since CC meets a center.
The character takes the distinct values (2qi/3pi)j(2^{q_{i}}/3^{p_{i}})^{j} at the
centers, so CC meets just one, say cjc_{j}.
To make the rigidity step explicit, the differential identity holds
in the function field of TT. A nonconstant character can be restricted
to a curve on which it remains nonconstant; on the complete model of
that curve it has a zero or pole. In a local parameter zz, writing
that character as zn​u​(z)z^{n}u(z) gives residue n≠0n\neq 0 for its logarithmic
differential. By contrast, differentiating any Laurent expansion has
zero coefficient of d​z/zdz/z, so an exact differential has residue zero.
Once the character is constant, d​Xi=0dX_{i}=0 forces XiX_{i} to be constant
in characteristic zero. Finally, 2qi/3pi2^{q_{i}}/3^{p_{i}} is a positive real
number different from one, by multiplicative independence. Its integer
powers are therefore distinct, even when pip_{i} is negative.


On this character fiber, the local relation is




log⁡(Z/2j)=ri​log⁡(Y/3j).\log(Z/2^{j})=r_{i}\log(Y/3^{j}).


Since ri≠0r_{i}\neq 0, the orders of the two torus shifts coincide, including
the case where both vanish identically. Also ui=0u_{i}=0 and, for l≠il\neq i,




ul=Xl−(ri−rl)​log⁡(Y/3j).u_{l}=X_{l}-(r_{i}-r_{l})\log(Y/3^{j}).


Dropping Z,XiZ,X_{i}, and dividing XlX_{l} by ri−rlr_{i}-r_{l}, gives precisely the
one-torus, one-center contact of Proposition 5.5 in mm ambient coordinates. Its
volume hypotheses follow from (11.3), and its
successive separation conditions were included above.
There are m−1m-1 remaining additive variables and one image center.
The one-torus volume conditions required there are




(1+3​σ)m​(w0/v)​θm−1<1,(1+3​σ)m−1​θm−1<1,(1+σ)​θ<1.(1+3\sigma)^{m}(w_{0}/v)\theta^{m-1}<1,\qquad(1+3\sigma)^{m-1}\theta^{m-1}<1,\qquad(1+\sigma)\theta<1.


The first follows from the stronger second line of
(11.3), and the other two are already among its
hypotheses. The nonzero rescaling Xl↦Xl/(ri−rl)X_{l}\mapsto X_{l}/(r_{i}-r_{l}) changes
neither pole orders nor local contact orders, since ri≠rlr_{i}\neq r_{l}.
When the torus shifts vanish identically their orders are +∞+\infty;
this convention is compatible with deleting the redundant shift.


This projection need not be birational. It is nonconstant and finite on
complete smooth curve models: ZZ is algebraic over YY and Xi=0X_{i}=0;
if all remaining coordinates were constant, CC would be constant.
Let its degree be ee. The reduced weighted pole degree on CC equals
ee times that of the image and is at most dW​(C)d_{W}(C). Local contacts
pull back with ramification. The sum of contacts over the single
original center is at most ee times the full image-center contact.
Proposition 5.5 on the image thus implies




dW​(C)≥e​dW′​(image⁡(C))≥e⁡(1+σ)​∑P′​ over image centerhP′≥(1+σ)​∑P↦cjhP,d_{W}(C)\geq e\,d_{W^{\prime}}(\mathrm{image}(C))\geq e(1+\sigma)\sum_{P^{\prime}\text{ over image center}}h_{P^{\prime}}\geq(1+\sigma)\sum_{P\mapsto c_{j}}h_{P},


the required contradiction.


Finally replace
each logarithm by a Taylor polynomial whose omitted
terms have weight strictly greater than wi/θw_{i}/\theta. This is an
invertible coordinate change preserving the weighted ideal: a replacement
of uiu_{i} adds only monomials of strictly higher weight, and its inverse
subtracts the same series. Thus contacts and the finite jet quotient
are preserved. The resulting coordinates are algebraic and regular.
The curve-to-jet construction in the proof of
Theorem 5.1 now applies: use ordinary blow-ups of the
finite-colength local ideals, the nef class supplied by the curve
inequality, its ample strict perturbation, eventual ordinary-power
pushdown, and Serre vanishing. The argument uses the curve inequality
and local regular parameters, not the number of torus coordinates.
It therefore supplies the asserted surjection here.
∎




11.2 The arithmetic minor and the exact logarithm replacement


Fix a rational F0>2/θF_{0}>2/\theta and put




Ti=⌈F0​wi/v⌉,Gi​(t)=∑1≤k<Ti(−1)k+1​tk/k,Li=lcm⁡(1,…,Ti−1).T_{i}=\lceil F_{0}w_{i}/v\rceil,\qquad G_{i}(t)=\sum_{1\leq k<T_{i}}(-1)^{k+1}t^{k}/k,\qquad L_{i}=\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,T_{i}-1).


Use columns P=Yh​Zk​XγP=Y^{h}Z^{k}X^{\gamma} of weighted degree
w0​(h+k)+w⋅γ≤Hw_{0}(h+k)+w\cdot\gamma\leq H, and all rows




(j,λ,ρ,β),0≤j<K,v⁡(λ+ρ)+w⋅β/θ<H.(j,\lambda,\rho,\beta),\qquad 0\leq j<K,\qquad v(\lambda+\rho)+w\cdot\beta/\theta<H.


Let AHtrA_{H}^{\rm tr} be the matrix with entry




[tλ​sρ​uβ]​P​(3j​(1+t),2j​(1+s),(Gi​(s)−ri​Gi​(t)+ui)i),[t^{\lambda}s^{\rho}u^{\beta}]P\bigl(3^{j}(1+t),2^{j}(1+s),(G_{i}(s)-r_{i}G_{i}(t)+u_{i})_{i}\bigr),

(11.7)

and let AHfullA_{H}^{\rm full} use full logarithms instead of GiG_{i}.


Lemma 11.3 (The same selected minor uses full logarithms).

For every choice of MM columns, where MM is the number of rows,
the corresponding determinants of AHtrA_{H}^{\rm tr} and
AHfullA_{H}^{\rm full} are equal. In particular the interpolation lemma
provides a nonzero rational full-row minor ΔH\Delta_{H} of both matrices.



Proof.

Put Ri​(t)=Gi​(t)−log⁡(1+t)R_{i}(t)=G_{i}(t)-\log(1+t). The truncated substitution is the
full substitution followed by




ui⟼ui+Ri​(s)−ri​Ri​(t).u_{i}\longmapsto u_{i}+R_{i}(s)-r_{i}R_{i}(t).


The added terms have weight at least v​Ti>wi/θvT_{i}>w_{i}/\theta.
Consequently this substitution preserves the ideal of monomials of
weight at least HH in ℚ⁡[[t,s,u]]\mathbb{Q}[[t,s,u]]. On the quotient, order the
monomials by increasing weight, breaking ties in any fixed way.
The substitution has a triangular coefficient matrix with diagonal
one: every nonidentity term has strictly larger weight. Its inverse
subtracts the same series and preserves the quotient as well.
The row change is independent of the selected column. Apply this at
each of the KK centers; the resulting block triangular matrix has
determinant one. Left multiplication by it takes the full matrix to
the truncated one, proving the assertion. This does not claim
coefficientwise equality of the two matrices.
∎



Elementary lattice-point counting in the weighted simplex gives




M∼K​θm​Hm+2(m+2)!​v2​∏iwi,b¯H=1M​H​∑rowsw⋅β,0≤b¯H≤θ.M\sim\frac{K\theta^{m}H^{m+2}}{(m+2)!v^{2}\prod_{i}w_{i}},\qquad\overline{b}_{H}=\frac{1}{MH}\sum_{\rm rows}w\cdot\beta,\qquad 0\leq\overline{b}_{H}\leq\theta.

(11.8)

The weights and mm are fixed in this asymptotic. Choose one nonzero
full-row minor and write




Ji=∑columnsγi−∑rowsβi.J_{i}=\sum_{\rm columns}\gamma_{i}-\sum_{\rm rows}\beta_{i}.


The number of selected columns is exactly MM. Full row rank supplies
at least one such nonzero determinant for each sufficiently large
admissible HH; no fixed choice of columns for all degrees is needed.
Within a fixed degree, however, that one column set is retained through
every subsequent arithmetic and analytic expansion.


As before, a nonzero determinant term proves Ji≥0J_{i}\geq 0.
Indeed, a coefficient entry is zero unless βi≤γi\beta_{i}\leq\gamma_{i} for
every ii, because the original monomial has degree γi\gamma_{i} in its
iith additive variable. In a nonzero term of the determinant expansion,
one nonzero entry is chosen in each row and column. Summing these
inequalities over that permutation proves Ji≥0J_{i}\geq 0 coordinatewise.
Scale column
γ\gamma by ∏i(qi​Li)γi\prod_{i}(q_{i}L_{i})^{\gamma_{i}} and row β\beta by
∏i(qi​Li)−βi\prod_{i}(q_{i}L_{i})^{-\beta_{i}}. The identity




qi​Li​(Gi​(s)−ri​Gi​(t))=qi​Li​Gi​(s)−pi​Li​Gi​(t)q_{i}L_{i}(G_{i}(s)-r_{i}G_{i}(t))=q_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(s)-p_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(t)


shows that every scaled entry is integral.
For clarity, the nonzero entry with row β\beta and column γ\gamma
contains the factor




∏i(γiβi)​(Gi​(s)−ri​Gi​(t))γi−βi.\prod_{i}\binom{\gamma_{i}}{\beta_{i}}(G_{i}(s)-r_{i}G_{i}(t))^{\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}}.


After the stated row and column scaling, its remaining powers are
powers of the integer-coefficient polynomials
qi​Li​Gi​(s)−pi​Li​Gi​(t)q_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(s)-p_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(t); the binomial factors and the coefficients
of 3j​h​2j​k​(1+t)h​(1+s)k3^{jh}2^{jk}(1+t)^{h}(1+s)^{k} are integers as well. Zero entries
remain zero even when their row scaling has negative exponent.
This is an entrywise integrality statement for the truncated matrix.
Lemma 11.3 then transfers its nonzero determinant, with
exactly the same value, to the full-logarithm matrix.
Therefore




CH​ΔH∈ℤ∖{0},CH=∏i(qi​Li)Ji∈ℤ>0.C_{H}\Delta_{H}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},\qquad C_{H}=\prod_{i}(q_{i}L_{i})^{J_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.


Since log⁡qi≤wi\log q_{i}\leq w_{i}, log⁡Li≤Λ​F0​wi/v\log L_{i}\leq\Lambda F_{0}w_{i}/v with
Λ=4​log⁡2\Lambda=4\log 2, and ∑iwi​Ji≤M​H​(1−b¯H)\sum_{i}w_{i}J_{i}\leq MH(1-\overline{b}_{H}),
we obtain




log⁡|ΔH|M​H≥−(1−b¯H)−Ear,Ear=Λ​F0/v.\frac{\log|\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\geq-(1-\overline{b}_{H})-E_{\rm ar},\qquad E_{\rm ar}=\Lambda F_{0}/v.

(11.9)

The row factors matter: clearing all columns without returning the
row grades would lose the favorable term b¯H\overline{b}_{H}.
Indeed ∑iwi​Ji≤M​H−∑rowsw⋅β=M​H​(1−b¯H)\sum_{i}w_{i}J_{i}\leq MH-\sum_{\rm rows}w\cdot\beta=MH(1-\overline{b}_{H}), by the degree bound on each selected column.
Thus the integer determinant gives the sharper intermediate estimate




log⁡|ΔH|M​H≥−(1+Ear)​(1−b¯H),\frac{\log|\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\geq-(1+E_{\rm ar})(1-\overline{b}_{H}),


which implies (11.9) because
0≤b¯H≤θ<10\leq\overline{b}_{H}\leq\theta<1. The least-common-multiple estimate
uses Ti−1<F0​wi/vT_{i}-1<F_{0}w_{i}/v, so the ceiling in TiT_{i} introduces no additional
term into the displayed arithmetic error.



11.3 Translation to an entire family in two variables


For a transverse multi-index a∈ℤ≥0ma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m} and a selected column
PP, define




fa,P​(z,w)=[ua]​P​(ez,ew,w−ξ​z+u1,…,w−ξ​z+um).f_{a,P}(z,w)=[u^{a}]P(e^{z},e^{w},w-\xi z+u_{1},\ldots,w-\xi z+u_{m}).

(11.10)

All these functions are entire. The selected column set is the same
for every row. Set




zj​(t)=j​log⁡3+log⁡(1+t),wj​(s)=j​log⁡2+log⁡(1+s),ϵi​(t)=(ξ−ri)​log⁡(1+t).z_{j}(t)=j\log 3+\log(1+t),\qquad w_{j}(s)=j\log 2+\log(1+s),\qquad\epsilon_{i}(t)=(\xi-r_{i})\log(1+t).


The central additive term vanishes because j​log⁡2−ξ​j​log⁡3=0j\log 2-\xi j\log 3=0.
Expanding the additive variables gives the exact finite identity





[uβ]​P​(3j​(1+t),2j​(1+s),(log⁡(1+s)−ri​log⁡(1+t)+ui)i)\displaystyle[u^{\beta}]P\bigl(3^{j}(1+t),2^{j}(1+s),(\log(1+s)-r_{i}\log(1+t)+u_{i})_{i}\bigr)




=∑a≥β(aβ)​ϵ​(t)a−β​fa,P​(zj​(t),wj​(s)).\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt=\sum_{a\geq\beta}\binom{a}{\beta}\,\epsilon(t)^{a-\beta}f_{a,P}(z_{j}(t),w_{j}(s)).

(11.11)

Only aa with w⋅a≤Hw\cdot a\leq H occur, because the selected columns
have that bound.
Here a≥βa\geq\beta means coordinatewise inequality, and
(aβ)=∏i(aiβi)\binom{a}{\beta}=\prod_{i}\binom{a_{i}}{\beta_{i}}.
At (z,w)=(zj​(t),wj​(s))(z,w)=(z_{j}(t),w_{j}(s)) the common additive variable in
(11.10) is
log⁡(1+s)−ξ​log⁡(1+t)\log(1+s)-\xi\log(1+t).
Adding ϵi​(t)\epsilon_{i}(t) changes it to
log⁡(1+s)−ri​log⁡(1+t)\log(1+s)-r_{i}\log(1+t), which proves
(11.11) directly by the multivariable binomial
formula. Although the functions are entire in two variables, this
translation in the transverse index is finite for every polynomial.
Extracting [tλ​sρ][t^{\lambda}s^{\rho}] introduces a finite
convolution over 0≤ℓ≤λ0\leq\ell\leq\lambda. Its scalars are




(aβ)⁡[tℓ]​ϵ​(t)a−β,\binom{a}{\beta}[t^{\ell}]\epsilon(t)^{a-\beta},


and its remaining row tests are
[tλ−ℓ​sρ]​fa,P​(zj​(t),wj​(s))[t^{\lambda-\ell}s^{\rho}]f_{a,P}(z_{j}(t),w_{j}(s)).
This expansion is independent of the selected column.


On |t|=1/2|t|=1/2, |log⁡(1+t)|≤log⁡2<1|\log(1+t)|\leq\log 2<1. Since
qi−ν≤exp⁡(−ν​wi+ν)q_{i}^{-\nu}\leq\exp(-\nu w_{i}+\nu), Cauchy’s estimate and
(aβ)≤2|a|\binom{a}{\beta}\leq 2^{|a|} give




|(aβ)[tℓ]ϵ(t)a−β|≤2ℓexp(−νw⋅(a−β)+(ν+log⁡2)​Hw∗).\left|\binom{a}{\beta}[t^{\ell}]\epsilon(t)^{a-\beta}\right|\leq 2^{\ell}\exp\left(-\nu w\cdot(a-\beta)+\frac{(\nu+\log 2)H}{w_{*}}\right).

(11.12)

For fixed mm and weights there are only polynomially many choices
of (a,ℓ)(a,\ell) per row. Their total contribution to the logarithm of
the determinant bound is O⁡(M​log⁡H)=o⁡(M​H)O(M\log H)=o(MH).
For example there are at most
∏i(1+H/wi)​(1+H/v)\prod_{i}(1+H/w_{i})(1+H/v) choices per row, with constants depending
only on the already fixed weights. Raising this polynomial count to
the MMth power contributes O⁡(M​log⁡H)O(M\log H) to the logarithm. This
counting statement uses the order of limits: mm and all weights are
fixed while HH grows.


Let




R=4​K​(1+log⁡3+log⁡2),BR=2​R​(1+|ξ|).R=4K(1+\log 3+\log 2),\qquad B_{R}=2R(1+|\xi|).


On |t|=|s|=1/2|t|=|s|=1/2 the points (zj​(t),wj​(s))(z_{j}(t),w_{j}(s)) lie in the
polydisc of radius R/2R/2. On the radius-RR polydisc,




|fa,P​(z,w)|≤exp⁡(H⁡(Rw0+log⁡BRw∗)).|f_{a,P}(z,w)|\leq\exp\left(H\left(\frac{R}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log B_{R}}{w_{*}}\right)\right).

(11.13)

One way to see the coefficient factor is to extract uau^{a} on
|ui|=1|u_{i}|=1: the additive factors have modulus at most
R⁡(1+|ξ|)+1≤BRR(1+|\xi|)+1\leq B_{R}. The torus exponents satisfy h+k≤H/w0h+k\leq H/w_{0}.


Expand each entire function in normalized Taylor monomials
(z/R)k​(w/R)l(z/R)^{k}(w/R)^{l}. Cauchy’s estimate bounds the coefficient by
(11.13), whereas a remaining row test is bounded by




2λ−ℓ+ρ​2−k−l.2^{\lambda-\ell+\rho}2^{-k-l}.


Together with 2ℓ2^{\ell} in (11.12), the total row test
cost is at most 2λ+ρ≤exp⁡(H​log⁡2/v)2^{\lambda+\rho}\leq\exp(H\log 2/v).


Lemma 11.4 (Bivariate Taylor collision).

For nn distinct pairs (k,l)∈ℤ≥02(k,l)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2},




∑(k+l)≥2​23​n3/2−n.\sum(k+l)\geq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}n^{3/2}-n.


If a term of the row expansion assigns nan_{a} rows to transverse
index aa, the analytic determinant factor has the upper bound




exp(−c2∑ana3/2+MH(Ehol+o(1))),c2=2​log⁡23,Ehol=Rw0+log⁡BRw∗+log⁡2v.\exp\left(-c_{2}\sum_{a}n_{a}^{3/2}+MH(E_{\rm hol}+o(1))\right),\quad c_{2}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\log 2}{3},\quad E_{\rm hol}=\frac{R}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log B_{R}}{w_{*}}+\frac{\log 2}{v}.





Proof.

Attach a unit square to each pair. The union has area nn and its
integral of x+yx+y is ∑(k+l)+n\sum(k+l)+n. Among sets of area nn in the
positive quadrant, the sublevel triangle x+y≤2​nx+y\leq\sqrt{2n}
minimizes that integral. Integrating on the triangle gives
(2​2/3)​n3/2(2\sqrt{2}/3)n^{3/2}, proving the combinatorial inequality.


For a fixed aa, the Taylor coefficient row indexed by (k,l)(k,l) is
the vector of those coefficients across the selected columns.
Consequently repeated triples (a,k,l)(a,k,l) give identical coefficient
rows and zero determinant. Every surviving term therefore has distinct
pairs within each aa-block. Reserve one half of 2−k−l2^{-k-l} to use the
combinatorial inequality; summing the other half contributes only a
fixed geometric factor per row. The coefficient determinant is bounded
by M!M! times the product of its coefficient bounds.
There are only M!M! summands in its permutation expansion, each with
one coefficient from each row. The relevant coefficients are those of
normalized Taylor monomials, so Cauchy’s bound is exactly
(11.13), with no factorial derivative normalization left
to account for.
Its logarithmic
factor is O⁡(M​log⁡M)=o⁡(M​H)O(M\log M)=o(MH), by (11.8). The linear
correction ∑ana=M\sum_{a}n_{a}=M is likewise negligible at that scale.


For completeness the Taylor expansion can be truncated at
k+l≤Jk+l\leq J before applying multilinearity. The geometric majorant
just used is summable independently of JJ, while the omitted entire
series converge uniformly on the smaller testing polydisc. Passing to
the limit therefore preserves the determinant bound. No exchange of
a divergent determinant expansion and summation is involved.
∎



The compiled argument uses the sufficient weaker collision constant
log⁡2/8\log 2/8, and obtains the same endpoint three. The sharper displayed
constant in Lemma 11.4 is not asserted to be the
identical formally verified numerical lemma.


There is a useful dichotomy for each term of the finite row translation.
Fix 0<A<10<A<1 and 0<η<10<\eta<1. The number of transverse indices with
w⋅a≤A​Hw\cdot a\leq AH satisfies




DA​(H)∼Am​Hmm!​∏iwi.D_{A}(H)\sim\frac{A^{m}H^{m}}{m!\prod_{i}w_{i}}.


If at least η​M\eta M rows have these indices, convexity gives




∑ana3/2≥(η​M)3/2DA​(H).\sum_{a}n_{a}^{3/2}\geq\frac{(\eta M)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{D_{A}(H)}}.


After division by M​HMH its leading value is




L=η3/2​K​θm(m+1)​(m+2)​v2​Am.L=\eta^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{K\theta^{m}}{(m+1)(m+2)v^{2}A^{m}}}.

(11.14)

If fewer than η​M\eta M rows have low transverse weight, then more
than (1−η)​M(1-\eta)M have w⋅a>A​Hw\cdot a>AH. The approximation factors in
(11.12) save at least
ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯H)\nu(A(1-\eta)-\overline{b}_{H}) per M​HMH. The analytic estimate is
therefore




log⁡|ΔH|M​H≤Ean+o⁡(1)+max⁡{−c2​L+o⁡(1),−ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯H)},\frac{\log|\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm an}+o(1)+\max\{-c_{2}L+o(1),-\nu(A(1-\eta)-\overline{b}_{H})\},

(11.15)

where one can take




Ean=Rw0+log⁡2v+log⁡BR+ν+log⁡2w∗.E_{\rm an}=\frac{R}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log 2}{v}+\frac{\log B_{R}+\nu+\log 2}{w_{*}}.


In the first alternative the omitted approximation factors are at
most one after removal of the displayed ceiling loss; in the second
alternative we simply omit the favorable collision factor. Thus the
two estimates apply term by term before summing all row choices.
The subtraction of b¯H\overline{b}_{H} in the second alternative is
particularly useful. Summing the approximation exponents over all rows
gives




1M​H​∑rowsw⋅(a−β)=1M​H​∑rowsw⋅a−b¯H≥A⁡(1−η)−b¯H.\frac{1}{MH}\sum_{\rm rows}w\cdot(a-\beta)=\frac{1}{MH}\sum_{\rm rows}w\cdot a-\overline{b}_{H}\geq A(1-\eta)-\overline{b}_{H}.


In the first alternative, Hölder’s inequality on the at most
DA​(H)D_{A}(H) low-weight indices gives the stated collision bound regardless
of how the remaining rows are distributed. Substitution of
(11.8) into η3/2​M/DA​(H)/H\eta^{3/2}\sqrt{M/D_{A}(H)}/H yields exactly
(11.14). The same arithmetic row grade therefore participates
in both alternatives; it is not a separately chosen approximation
parameter.



11.4 The exponent-three parameter balance


Comparing (11.9) with (11.15), it suffices that




g=ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−θ)−(1−θ)>0,Ear+Ean<g,c2L>1+Ear+Ean.\begin{split}g&=\nu(A(1-\eta)-\theta)-(1-\theta)>0,\\
E_{\rm ar}+E_{\rm an}&<g,\qquad c_{2}L>1+E_{\rm ar}+E_{\rm an}.\end{split}

(11.16)

Indeed the approximation gap is
ν​A​(1−η)−1−(ν−1)​b¯H\nu A(1-\eta)-1-(\nu-1)\overline{b}_{H}, minimized at
b¯H=θ\overline{b}_{H}=\theta, while 1−b¯H≤11-\overline{b}_{H}\leq 1 handles the
collision alternative.
More explicitly, put E=Ear+EanE=E_{\rm ar}+E_{\rm an}. If the maximum in
(11.15) is supplied by the approximation alternative,
comparison with (11.9) would imply




ν​A​(1−η)−1−(ν−1)​b¯H≤E+o⁡(1).\nu A(1-\eta)-1-(\nu-1)\overline{b}_{H}\leq E+o(1).


Its left side is at least gg, contradicting g>Eg>E for all sufficiently
large HH. If the maximum is supplied by the collision alternative,
the two bounds would instead imply




c2​L≤1−b¯H+E+o⁡(1)≤1+E+o⁡(1),c_{2}L\leq 1-\overline{b}_{H}+E+o(1)\leq 1+E+o(1),


contradicting the other strict inequality in (11.16).
Thus neither alternative is possible. All o⁡(1)o(1) terms are taken only
after the fixed positive gaps have been established.


Fix ν>3\nu>3. Choose rational b0b_{0} with




2/3<b0<1−1/ν.2/3<b_{0}<1-1/\nu.


For small rational δ>0\delta>0 put θ=1−δ\theta=1-\delta and
A=1−b0​δA=1-b_{0}\delta. Then




ν⁡(A−θ)>1−θ,A3<θ2.\nu(A-\theta)>1-\theta,\qquad A^{3}<\theta^{2}.


The first inequality is exactly ν⁡(1−b0)>1\nu(1-b_{0})>1, multiplied by
δ>0\delta>0. For the second, the function
(1−b0​δ)3−(1−δ)2(1-b_{0}\delta)^{3}-(1-\delta)^{2} vanishes at zero and has derivative
2−3​b0<02-3b_{0}<0 there. It is consequently negative for all sufficiently
small positive δ\delta. This is the only place that requires the
interval 2/3<b0<1−1/ν2/3<b_{0}<1-1/\nu to be nonempty, and hence the condition
ν>3\nu>3.


The second strict inequality permits rational choices




A<B<θ2/3,B/θ<C<1/B.A<B<\theta^{2/3},\qquad B/\theta<C<1/\sqrt{B}.


The second interval is nonempty precisely when B3<θ2B^{3}<\theta^{2}.
Also A>θA>\theta because b0<1b_{0}<1, hence B>θB>\theta and
B/θ>1B/\theta>1. From C<1/BC<1/\sqrt{B} and B>θ>θ2B>\theta>\theta^{2} one gets
C​θ<θ/B<1C\theta<\theta/\sqrt{B}<1. These inequalities verify all four
bases of the exponential limits used below.


They satisfy C>1C>1, C​θ<1C\theta<1, C​B<1C\sqrt{B}<1, and C​θ/B>1C\theta/B>1.
Choose η>0\eta>0 sufficiently small to preserve the positive gap,
and fix F0>2/θF_{0}>2/\theta.
One explicit admissible restriction is
0<η<(ν⁡(A−θ)−(1−θ))/(2​ν​A)0<\eta<(\nu(A-\theta)-(1-\theta))/(2\nu A), with
η<1\eta<1. It makes
g=ν⁡(A−θ)−(1−θ)−ν​A​η>0g=\nu(A-\theta)-(1-\theta)-\nu A\eta>0 a fixed number independent
of mm, the approximants, and HH. Both η\eta and F0F_{0} may be chosen
rational.
For even integers mm set




K=⌊Cm⌋,w0=B−m/2,v=⌈2​K​θm​w02⌉,σ=1100​(m+3).K=\lfloor C^{m}\rfloor,\quad w_{0}=B^{-m/2},\quad v=\left\lceil\sqrt{2K\theta^{m}w_{0}^{2}}\right\rceil,\qquad\sigma=\frac{1}{100(m+3)}.

(11.17)

The weights are rational. Their relevant limits are




K/w0\displaystyle K/w_{0}
≤(C​B)m⟶0,\displaystyle\leq(C\sqrt{B})^{m}\longrightarrow 0,



v\displaystyle v
∼2​(C​θ/B)m/2⟶∞,\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{2}(C\theta/B)^{m/2}\longrightarrow\infty,



w0/v\displaystyle w_{0}/v
∼2−1/2(Cθ)−m/2⟶∞,\displaystyle\sim 2^{-1/2}(C\theta)^{-m/2}\longrightarrow\infty,



(w0/v)​θm−1\displaystyle(w_{0}/v)\theta^{m-1}
∼(2​θ)−1​(θ/C)m/2⟶0,\displaystyle\sim(\sqrt{2}\theta)^{-1}(\theta/C)^{m/2}\longrightarrow 0,



L\displaystyle L
∼η3/2​(B/A)m/22​(m+1)​(m+2)⟶∞.\displaystyle\sim\frac{\eta^{3/2}(B/A)^{m/2}}{\sqrt{2(m+1)(m+2)}}\longrightarrow\infty.


Also K​(w0/v)2​θm≤1/2K(w_{0}/v)^{2}\theta^{m}\leq 1/2. The factor
(1+3​σ)m+2(1+3\sigma)^{m+2} stays close to e3/100<2e^{3/100}<2, so all strict
geometric inequalities of Lemma 11.2 hold for
large mm, including w0/v>4​(m+3)/σw_{0}/v>4(m+3)/\sigma. The remaining two
volume inequalities tend to zero exponentially. The errors
R/w0R/w_{0} and Λ​F0/v\Lambda F_{0}/v tend to zero, while LL tends to infinity.


We now fix one such finite dimension mm. Only next do we choose
the mm rational approximants successively far out in the assumed
sequence, meeting every separation threshold and making w∗−1w_{*}^{-1}
errors small. Finally HH tends to infinity through divisible values.
Thus (11.16) is a contradiction for each ν>3\nu>3.
For an explicit ordering with margins, first enlarge the even mm
until every geometric condition holds,




c2​L>3,R/w0+(Λ​F0+log⁡2)/v<min⁡(g/4,1/4).c_{2}L>3,\qquad R/w_{0}+(\Lambda F_{0}+\log 2)/v<\min(g/4,1/4).


With this mm fixed, RR and BRB_{R} are fixed. Choose w1w_{1}, and then
the later wiw_{i}, far enough along the approximation sequence to satisfy
all successive separation thresholds and




(log⁡BR+ν+log⁡2)/w∗<min⁡(g/4,1/4).(\log B_{R}+\nu+\log 2)/w_{*}<\min(g/4,1/4).


All later weights can be required to exceed the same common lower
bound, so enlarging them preserves this last inequality. Then
E<min⁡(g/2,1/2)E<\min(g/2,1/2) and c2​L>1+Ec_{2}L>1+E.
Only now are the truncations, interpolation threshold, and nonzero
minors fixed degree by degree, and only now is HH sent to infinity.
There is no simultaneous limit in dimension, denominators, and degree.


For general positive multiplicatively independent rational a,ba,b,
replace the centers by (bj,aj,0)(b^{j},a^{j},0) and put ξ=log⁡a/log⁡b\xi=\log a/\log b.
The characters (aqi/bpi)j(a^{q_{i}}/b^{p_{i}})^{j} are distinct. If va​av_{a}a and
vb​bv_{b}b are integral, an additional column factor
vb(K−1)​h​va(K−1)​kv_{b}^{(K-1)h}v_{a}^{(K-1)k} clears the torus denominators. It adds
only O⁡(K/w0)O(K/w_{0}) to the arithmetic error. The Cauchy radius changes
by a fixed constant depending on |log⁡a|,|log⁡b||\log a|,|\log b|. All limits
above persist.
Take va,vbv_{a},v_{b} to be fixed positive integers clearing the denominators
of a,ba,b. The additional factor in an entry at center jj is




vb(K−1)​h​va(K−1)​k​bj​h​aj​k=(vb​b)j​h​vb(K−1−j)​h​(va​a)j​k​va(K−1−j)​k,v_{b}^{(K-1)h}v_{a}^{(K-1)k}b^{jh}a^{jk}=(v_{b}b)^{jh}v_{b}^{(K-1-j)h}(v_{a}a)^{jk}v_{a}^{(K-1-j)k},


which is integral since 0≤j≤K−10\leq j\leq K-1. For the determinant its
logarithmic cost divided by M​HMH is at most
(K−1)​max⁡(log⁡va,log⁡vb)/w0(K-1)\max(\log v_{a},\log v_{b})/w_{0}.
This tends to zero in the dimension selection, because
K/w0→0K/w_{0}\to 0. For the analytic bounds one may replace RR by
4​K​(1+|log⁡a|+|log⁡b|)4K(1+|\log a|+|\log b|) and use the corresponding BRB_{R}.
The character aqi/bpia^{q_{i}}/b^{p_{i}} remains positive and different from
one, so its powers still separate the centers. These facts cover also
positive rationals below one and ratios ξ\xi of either sign.
Irrationality follows from multiplicative independence,
and Lemma 3.1 gives the lower bound two. This
completes Theorem 11.1.


Corollary 11.5 (A fixed homogeneous two-logarithm measure).

For positive multiplicatively independent rationals a,ba,b and every
ε>0\varepsilon>0, there is ca,b,ε>0c_{a,b,\varepsilon}>0 such that




|p​log⁡a+q​log⁡b|≥ca,b,ε​max⁡(1,|p|,|q|)−2−ε((p,q)∈ℤ2∖{(0,0)}).|p\log a+q\log b|\geq c_{a,b,\varepsilon}\max(1,|p|,|q|)^{-2-\varepsilon}\quad((p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}).





Proof.

For p≠0p\neq 0, factor the form as p​log⁡b​(ξ+q/p)p\log b\,(\xi+q/p).
When −q/p-q/p lies in a fixed bounded neighborhood of ξ\xi,
the coefficient height is comparable to |p||p| and the exponent-three
ratio bound gives the assertion after multiplication by |p​log⁡b||p\log b|.
Outside that neighborhood the form is bounded below by a fixed
positive multiple of the height, after separating the region where
|q/p||q/p| is large. If p=0p=0, use |q​log⁡b||q\log b| directly. Adjusting the
constant handles the finitely many small denominators.
Here is a direct uniform version of that last step. The ratio theorem
implies, for every ε>0\varepsilon>0, a constant c0>0c_{0}>0 such that




|ξ−r/s|≥c0​s−3−ε(r∈ℤ,s∈ℤ≥1).|\xi-r/s|\geq c_{0}s^{-3-\varepsilon}\qquad(r\in\mathbb{Z},\ s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}).


Indeed infinitely many exceptions with unbounded reduced denominator
would give an irrationality exponent at least 3+ε3+\varepsilon;
for bounded denominator the positive distances have a positive minimum
after multiplication by s3+εs^{3+\varepsilon}. Reducing a fraction only
strengthens this lower bound. Applying it with s=|p|s=|p| and the
corresponding signed numerator gives, for p≠0p\neq 0,




|p​log⁡a+q​log⁡b|≥c0​|log⁡b|​|p|−2−ε≥c0|log⁡b|max⁡(1,|p|,|q|)−2−ε.|p\log a+q\log b|\geq c_{0}|\log b|\,|p|^{-2-\varepsilon}\geq c_{0}|\log b|\,\max(1,|p|,|q|)^{-2-\varepsilon}.


For p=0p=0 and q≠0q\neq 0, the left side is at least |log⁡b||\log b|.
Thus ca,b,ε=|log⁡b|​min⁡(c0,1)c_{a,b,\varepsilon}=|\log b|\min(c_{0},1) is sufficient.
Multiplicative independence guarantees that none of the finitely many
forms used in choosing a constant vanishes.


∎



This measure concerns two homogeneous terms. It asserts no identical
exponent for a third, independently varying, integer constant term.
The uniform positive constant and the homogeneous two-logarithm
conclusion are now exported by
QuadraticPi/RationalTwoLogLinearForm.lean;
Appendix A gives the exact declarations.
In particular, the completed endpoint is
QuadraticPi.rationalTwoLog_linearForm_lowerBound.
The next section also has checked general number-field and algebraically normalized ratio endpoints, with their exact branch hypotheses.




12 Conjugate ratios and algebraic normalizations


The 3​d/s3d/s comparison and the algebraically normalized ratio families
below now have checked Lean endpoints. Their general number-field
interpolation, one-minor norm comparison, analytic estimates, and
parameter choices are assembled in the accompanying sources. Irrational
algebraic slopes are handled by the checked normalized interpolation
adapter. Appendix A records the public declarations
and the exact matching, branch, and nonvanishing hypotheses.


The essential arithmetic point is to select one nonzero minor over
the number field and use its images at every embedding. Selecting
unrelated minors at different embeddings would not supply the norm
inequality used below. Exact matching of the chosen logarithm
branches, rather than matching their absolute values, supplies the
analytic smallness at each good embedding.


Theorem 12.1 (Partial matching for a quotient of logarithms).

Let EE have degree dd and let a,b∈E×a,b\in E^{\times}.
Let ξ∈ℝ∖ℚ\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q} and suppose
there are ss distinct embeddings, with 1≤s≤d1\leq s\leq d,
σ:E↪ℂ\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C} admitting
nonzero logarithms λa,σ,λb,σ\lambda_{a,\sigma},\lambda_{b,\sigma} with




eλa,σ=σ⁡(a),eλb,σ=σ⁡(b),λa,σ=ξ​λb,σ.e^{\lambda_{a,\sigma}}=\sigma(a),\qquad e^{\lambda_{b,\sigma}}=\sigma(b),\qquad\lambda_{a,\sigma}=\xi\lambda_{b,\sigma}.


Then




2≤μ⁡(ξ)≤3​ds.2\leq\mu(\xi)\leq\frac{3d}{s}.





Proof.

Let SS be the specified set of good embeddings and put D=d/sD=d/s.
Fix ν>3​D\nu>3D and suppose there are infinitely many reduced fractions
r=p/qr=p/q, with q>0q>0, satisfying |ξ−r|≤q−ν|\xi-r|\leq q^{-\nu}.
Their denominators are unbounded and the fractions converge to ξ\xi.
We shall choose finitely many distinct nonzero fractions
ri=pi/qir_{i}=p_{i}/q_{i} successively from this sequence, with
wi=⌈log⁡qi⌉w_{i}=\lceil\log q_{i}\rceil as large as required. As before, all
such choices precede the limit in HH.


Exceptional slopes and interpolation.
The elements a,ba,b cannot both be roots of unity. Indeed, every
logarithm of a root of unity is a rational multiple of 2​π​i2\pi i;
the quotient of two nonzero such logarithms is rational, contrary
to the hypothesis at any embedding in SS. Consider the subgroup




ℛ={(p,q)∈ℤ2:aq/bp​ is a root of unity}.\mathcal{R}=\{(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:a^{q}/b^{p}\text{ is a root of unity}\}.


If (p1,q1),(p2,q2)∈ℛ(p_{1},q_{1}),(p_{2},q_{2})\in\mathcal{R} are linearly independent,
eliminating aa and then bb from their two relations shows that
nonzero integer powers of both aa and bb are roots of unity.
Both elements would themselves be roots of unity. Thus
rank⁡ℛ≤1\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{R}\leq 1. All its elements with q≠0q\neq 0
therefore have at most one rational slope p/qp/q. This proves that
there is at most one exceptional rational slope for which
aq/bpa^{q}/b^{p} is a root of unity. A sequence converging to irrational
ξ\xi eventually avoids that slope and zero. We choose all rir_{i}
from this tail; we may also require |ri|≤1+|ξ||r_{i}|\leq 1+|\xi|.


At the centers (bj,aj,0)(b^{j},a^{j},0), the character attached to rir_{i} takes
the values (aqi/bpi)j(a^{q_{i}}/b^{p_{i}})^{j}. They are pairwise distinct for
0≤j<K0\leq j<K, since equality of two would make the base a root of
unity. These are exactly the character distinctions used in
Lemma 11.2. The commuting frame, the separated
normal-basis inequalities, and the reduction of a character fiber
to a one-torus curve are unchanged over an algebraic closure of
EE. No positivity or reality of the centers is used there. The
