lemma consequently gives full row rank over that algebraic closure,
and hence a nonzero full-row minor over EE.


One minor and its denominator.
Use the truncations GiG_{i}, integers LiL_{i}, columns
P=Yh​Zk​XγP=Y^{h}Z^{k}X^{\gamma}, and rows (j,ℓ,ρ,β)(j,\ell,\rho,\beta) of
Section 11, with 3j,2j3^{j},2^{j} replaced by bj,ajb^{j},a^{j}.
The entry is




[tℓ​sρ​uβ]​P​(bj​(1+t),aj​(1+s),(Gi​(s)−ri​Gi​(t)+ui)i)∈E.[t^{\ell}s^{\rho}u^{\beta}]\,P\bigl(b^{j}(1+t),a^{j}(1+s),(G_{i}(s)-r_{i}G_{i}(t)+u_{i})_{i}\bigr)\in E.


For each sufficiently large divisible HH, select one nonzero
M×MM\times M minor ΔH\Delta_{H} and retain these same columns throughout
the proof. Lemma 11.3 is a rational unitriangular row
change; it applies over EE and at every embedding. In particular,
the corresponding full-logarithm minor equals ΔH\Delta_{H} exactly.
The row count and




b¯H=1M​H​∑rowsw⋅β,0≤b¯H≤θ\overline{b}_{H}=\frac{1}{MH}\sum_{\rm rows}w\cdot\beta,\qquad 0\leq\overline{b}_{H}\leq\theta


are still those of (11.8).


Choose positive integers va,vbv_{a},v_{b} such that va​a,vb​bv_{a}a,v_{b}b are
algebraic integers. For the selected columns put




Ji=∑columnsγi−∑rowsβi,CH=∏i(qi​Li)Ji​vb(K−1)​∑columnsh​va(K−1)​∑columnsk.J_{i}=\sum_{\rm columns}\gamma_{i}-\sum_{\rm rows}\beta_{i},\qquad C_{H}=\prod_{i}(q_{i}L_{i})^{J_{i}}v_{b}^{(K-1)\sum_{\rm columns}h}v_{a}^{(K-1)\sum_{\rm columns}k}.


A nonzero term of the determinant has βi≤γi\beta_{i}\leq\gamma_{i} at
each paired entry, so Ji≥0J_{i}\geq 0. Multiply column PP by
∏i(qi​Li)γi​vb(K−1)​h​va(K−1)​k\prod_{i}(q_{i}L_{i})^{\gamma_{i}}v_{b}^{(K-1)h}v_{a}^{(K-1)k} and row
β\beta by ∏i(qi​Li)−βi\prod_{i}(q_{i}L_{i})^{-\beta_{i}}. The resulting entries
are algebraic integers: the additive coefficients are integral
because qi​Li​Gi​(s)−pi​Li​Gi​(t)q_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(s)-p_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(t) has integral coefficients,
and j≤K−1j\leq K-1 ensures that the column torus factor clears
bj​h​aj​kb^{jh}a^{jk}. Consequently




CH​ΔH∈𝒪E∖{0}.C_{H}\Delta_{H}\in\mathcal{O}_{E}\setminus\{0\}.


Set Λ=4​log⁡2\Lambda=4\log 2 and




Ear=Λ​F0v+(K−1)​max⁡(log⁡va,log⁡vb)w0.E_{\rm ar}=\frac{\Lambda F_{0}}{v}+\frac{(K-1)\max(\log v_{a},\log v_{b})}{w_{0}}.


Using log⁡(qi​Li)≤(1+Λ​F0/v)​wi\log(q_{i}L_{i})\leq(1+\Lambda F_{0}/v)w_{i} and the column
weighted-degree bound gives




log⁡CHM​H≤1−b¯H+Ear.\frac{\log C_{H}}{MH}\leq 1-\overline{b}_{H}+E_{\rm ar}.


The nonzero rational integer NE/ℚ​(CH​ΔH)N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(C_{H}\Delta_{H})
has absolute value at least one. Thus the exact same minor satisfies




∑σ:E↪ℂlog⁡|σ⁡(ΔH)|M​H≥−d​log⁡CHM​H≥−d(1−b¯H+Ear).\sum_{\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\frac{\log|\sigma(\Delta_{H})|}{MH}\geq-\frac{d\log C_{H}}{MH}\geq-d(1-\overline{b}_{H}+E_{\rm ar}).

(12.1)

Every embedding is counted once, including both members of a
nonreal conjugate pair. None of the embedded minors vanishes,
because each embedding is injective.


The good embeddings.
For every selected column use the common entire families




fγ,P​(z,w)=[uγ]​P​(ez,ew,w−ξ​z+u1,…,w−ξ​z+um).f_{\gamma,P}(z,w)=[u^{\gamma}]P(e^{z},e^{w},w-\xi z+u_{1},\ldots,w-\xi z+u_{m}).


At σ∈S\sigma\in S their coefficient tests occur at




z=j​λb,σ+log⁡(1+t),w=j​λa,σ+log⁡(1+s).z=j\lambda_{b,\sigma}+\log(1+t),\qquad w=j\lambda_{a,\sigma}+\log(1+s).


Here w−ξ​z=log⁡(1+s)−ξ​log⁡(1+t)w-\xi z=\log(1+s)-\xi\log(1+t), because the constant
term vanishes by the specified branch matching. In the equal
full-logarithm minor, the difference between its iith additive
coordinate and this analytic coordinate is precisely
(ξ−ri)​log⁡(1+t)(\xi-r_{i})\log(1+t). It does not depend on σ\sigma.
Therefore the translation identity (11.11),
the scalar estimate (11.12), and the bivariate Taylor
collision apply to this same selected minor at every good embedding.


For a uniform radius and growth constant take




R=4​K​(1+maxσ∈S⁡(|λa,σ|+|λb,σ|)),BR=2​R​(1+|ξ|).R=4K\left(1+\max_{\sigma\in S}(|\lambda_{a,\sigma}|+|\lambda_{b,\sigma}|)\right),\qquad B_{R}=2R(1+|\xi|).


The testing points on |t|=|s|=1/2|t|=|s|=1/2 lie in the radius-R/2R/2
polydisc. The proof of (11.15) consequently gives,
uniformly for σ∈S\sigma\in S,




log⁡|σ⁡(ΔH)|M​H≤Ean+o⁡(1)+max⁡{−c2​L+o⁡(1),−ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯H)},\frac{\log|\sigma(\Delta_{H})|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm an}+o(1)+\max\{-c_{2}L+o(1),-\nu(A(1-\eta)-\overline{b}_{H})\},

(12.2)

where c2c_{2} and LL are as in Lemma 11.4 and
(11.14), and a sufficient error is




Ean=Rw0+log⁡2v+log⁡BR+ν+log⁡2w∗.E_{\rm an}=\frac{R}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log 2}{v}+\frac{\log B_{R}+\nu+\log 2}{w_{*}}.


The o⁡(1)o(1) terms tend to zero as H→∞H\to\infty, after all other
parameters and fractions have been fixed.


The other embeddings.
No matching logarithms are required here. Define the fixed constants




cE=maxσ:E↪ℂmax(0,log|σ(a)|,log|σ(b)|),Qξ=1+(2+|ξ|)log2.c_{E}=\max_{\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\max(0,\log|\sigma(a)|,\log|\sigma(b)|),\qquad Q_{\xi}=1+(2+|\xi|)\log 2.


On |t|=|s|=1/2|t|=|s|=1/2 one has |Gi​(t)|,|Gi​(s)|≤log⁡2|G_{i}(t)|,|G_{i}(s)|\leq\log 2.
On the additional circles |ui|=1|u_{i}|=1, each additive factor is
bounded by QξQ_{\xi}, using |ri|≤1+|ξ||r_{i}|\leq 1+|\xi|. Each torus factor
is bounded by 32​exp⁡((K−1)​cE)\frac{3}{2}\exp((K-1)c_{E}). Cauchy’s estimate for
[tℓ​sρ​uβ][t^{\ell}s^{\rho}u^{\beta}], followed by the determinant expansion,
therefore gives at every remaining embedding




log⁡|σ⁡(ΔH)|M​H≤Ebad+o⁡(1),Ebad=log⁡(3/2)+(K−1)​cEw0+log⁡2v+log⁡Qξw∗.\frac{\log|\sigma(\Delta_{H})|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm bad}+o(1),\qquad E_{\rm bad}=\frac{\log(3/2)+(K-1)c_{E}}{w_{0}}+\frac{\log 2}{v}+\frac{\log Q_{\xi}}{w_{*}}.

(12.3)

Indeed h+k≤H/w0h+k\leq H/w_{0}, ∑iγi≤H/w∗\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}\leq H/w_{*}, and
ℓ+ρ<H/v\ell+\rho<H/v bound each entry by exp⁡(H​Ebad)\exp(HE_{\rm bad}).
The determinant factor contributes log⁡(M!)/(M​H)≤log⁡M/H=o⁡(1)\log(M!)/(MH)\leq\log M/H=o(1)
by (11.8). This is a crude growth bound, not a claim
of analytic smallness at a bad embedding.


The averaged comparison.
Combine (12.1)–(12.3)
and divide by ss. With




E=D​Ear+Ean+(D−1)​Ebad,E=DE_{\rm ar}+E_{\rm an}+(D-1)E_{\rm bad},


it suffices to arrange




g=ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−θ)−D⁡(1−θ)>0,E<g,c2​L>D+E.g=\nu(A(1-\eta)-\theta)-D(1-\theta)>0,\qquad E<g,\qquad c_{2}L>D+E.

(12.4)

To see this directly, the approximation alternative minus the
norm cost is




ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯H)−D⁡(1−b¯H)=ν​A​(1−η)−D−(ν−D)​b¯H≥g,\nu(A(1-\eta)-\overline{b}_{H})-D(1-\overline{b}_{H})=\nu A(1-\eta)-D-(\nu-D)\overline{b}_{H}\geq g,


since ν>D\nu>D and b¯H≤θ\overline{b}_{H}\leq\theta. The collision
alternative has norm cost at most DD, since
1−b¯H≤11-\overline{b}_{H}\leq 1. Under (12.4) both
alternatives contradict the norm inequality for sufficiently large HH.


The explicit order of parameter choices.
Because ν>3​D\nu>3D, choose rational b0b_{0} with




23<b0<1−Dν.\frac{2}{3}<b_{0}<1-\frac{D}{\nu}.


For sufficiently small positive rational δ\delta put
θ=1−δ\theta=1-\delta and A=1−b0​δA=1-b_{0}\delta. Then




ν⁡(A−θ)−D⁡(1−θ)=(ν⁡(1−b0)−D)​δ>0,A3<θ2.\nu(A-\theta)-D(1-\theta)=\bigl(\nu(1-b_{0})-D\bigr)\delta>0,\qquad A^{3}<\theta^{2}.


The second inequality follows since the derivative at δ=0\delta=0
of (1−b0​δ)3−(1−δ)2(1-b_{0}\delta)^{3}-(1-\delta)^{2} is 2−3​b0<02-3b_{0}<0.
Choose η>0\eta>0 small enough that the first strict margin persists,
and fix rational F0>2/θF_{0}>2/\theta. Choose rational B,CB,C satisfying




A<B<θ2/3,B/θ<C<1/B.A<B<\theta^{2/3},\qquad B/\theta<C<1/\sqrt{B}.


The interval for CC is nonempty because B3<θ2B^{3}<\theta^{2}.
As A>θA>\theta, these choices give C>1C>1, C​θ<1C\theta<1,
C​B<1C\sqrt{B}<1, and C​θ/B>1C\theta/B>1. For even integers mm use
exactly (11.17):




K=⌊Cm⌋,w0=B−m/2,v=⌈2​K​θm​w02⌉,σ=1100​(m+3).K=\lfloor C^{m}\rfloor,\quad w_{0}=B^{-m/2},\quad v=\left\lceil\sqrt{2K\theta^{m}w_{0}^{2}}\right\rceil,\qquad\sigma=\frac{1}{100(m+3)}.


The geometric inequalities hold for all sufficiently large mm
by the calculation in Section 11.4. More explicitly,
K​(w0/v)2​θm≤1/2K(w_{0}/v)^{2}\theta^{m}\leq 1/2, the remaining volume expressions
tend to zero, and w0/vw_{0}/v grows exponentially faster than the required
4​(m+3)/σ4(m+3)/\sigma. In addition,




Kw0⟶0,v⟶∞,L∼η3/2​(B/A)m/22​(m+1)​(m+2)⟶∞.\frac{K}{w_{0}}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad v\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad L\sim\frac{\eta^{3/2}(B/A)^{m/2}}{\sqrt{2(m+1)(m+2)}}\longrightarrow\infty.


Thus the arithmetic torus cost, the common-radius cost R/w0R/w_{0},
and all 1/v1/v costs tend to zero, including their fixed factors
DD and D−1D-1. First fix a sufficiently large finite even mm
so these costs are smaller than the strict margin and c2​Lc_{2}L is
larger than the required norm cost. Next select the mm fractions
successively far out in the nonexceptional tail. This meets every
slope-independent interpolation threshold and makes all remaining
w∗−1w_{*}^{-1} errors small enough for (12.4).
Finally let HH tend to infinity through sufficiently divisible values
to remove the o⁡(1)o(1) terms. This contradicts infinitely many
approximations of exponent ν\nu. Since ν>3​d/s\nu>3d/s was arbitrary,
μ⁡(ξ)≤3​d/s\mu(\xi)\leq 3d/s. Irrationality is a hypothesis, so
Lemma 3.1 supplies μ⁡(ξ)≥2\mu(\xi)\geq 2.
∎



Corollary 12.2 (Full matching for a quotient of logarithms).

Under the hypotheses of Theorem 12.1, if every
embedding of EE admits the specified nonzero logarithms with the
same real irrational quotient ξ\xi, then




2≤μ⁡(ξ)≤3.2\leq\mu(\xi)\leq 3.





Proof.

Take s=ds=d in Theorem 12.1. In this case the norm
averages precisely dd good-embedding estimates, with no
bad-embedding contribution. This is a bound between two and three;
the argument does not identify the exact exponent in general.
∎



Corollary 12.3 (Quadratic norm-one ratios).

Let E/ℚE/\mathbb{Q} be quadratic with nontrivial involution τ\tau.
Let a,b∈E×a,b\in E^{\times} satisfy
τ⁡(a)=a−1\tau(a)=a^{-1}, τ⁡(b)=b−1\tau(b)=b^{-1}. If
λa,λb\lambda_{a},\lambda_{b} are nonzero logarithms and
ξ=λa/λb∈ℝ∖ℚ\xi=\lambda_{a}/\lambda_{b}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}, then




2≤μ⁡(ξ)≤3.2\leq\mu(\xi)\leq 3.





Proof.

At the given embedding use λa,λb\lambda_{a},\lambda_{b}; at its conjugate
use −λa,−λb-\lambda_{a},-\lambda_{b}. Their exponentials are a−1,b−1a^{-1},b^{-1}
and their quotient is unchanged. Thus all s=d=2s=d=2 embeddings are
good, and Corollary 12.2 applies.
∎



For positive a,ba,b in a real quadratic field, individual norms
±1\pm 1 suffice after replacing both by their squares. Their real
logarithm quotient is unchanged. Two positive integral units in a
real quadratic field have a rational real-logarithm quotient, because
the unit rank is one; norm-one elements with allowed denominators
can give irrational quotients. For instance




a=3+2​2,b=3+23−2a=3+2\sqrt{2},\qquad b=\frac{3+\sqrt{2}}{3-\sqrt{2}}


are independent: aa is a unit, whereas bb has nonzero valuation
at a prime above 77. A multiplicative relation first forces the
exponent of bb to vanish by that valuation, and then that of aa
to vanish since a>1a>1. A rational logarithm quotient would give
such a relation. Hence




2≤μ⁡(log⁡(3+2​2)log⁡((3+2)/(3−2)))≤3.2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\log(3+2\sqrt{2})}{\log((3+\sqrt{2})/(3-\sqrt{2}))}\right)\leq 3.




An explicit pair of arctangents.


In ℚ⁡(i)\mathbb{Q}(i) take




a=2+i2−i,b=4+i4−i.a=\frac{2+i}{2-i},\qquad b=\frac{4+i}{4-i}.


Choose λa=2​i​arctan⁡(1/2)\lambda_{a}=2i\arctan(1/2) and
λb=2​i​arctan⁡(1/4)\lambda_{b}=2i\arctan(1/4). Their conjugates are their negatives.
The Gaussian primes 2+i2+i and 4+i4+i have norms 55 and 1717.
Their valuations in a relation au​bv=ζa^{u}b^{v}=\zeta for a root of unity
ζ\zeta force u=0u=0 and then v=0v=0. In particular the quotient
of the displayed logarithms is irrational: if it were p/qp/q,
exponentiating q​λa=p​λbq\lambda_{a}=p\lambda_{b} would give
aq​b−p=1a^{q}b^{-p}=1. Therefore




2≤μ⁡(arctan⁡(1/2)arctan⁡(1/4))≤3.\boxed{\displaystyle 2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\arctan(1/2)}{\arctan(1/4)}\right)\leq 3.}


Equivalently, for every ε>0\varepsilon>0 a positive constant cc
satisfies




|p​arctan⁡(1/2)+q​arctan⁡(1/4)|≥c​max⁡(1,|p|,|q|)−2−ε|p\arctan(1/2)+q\arctan(1/4)|\geq c\max(1,|p|,|q|)^{-2-\varepsilon}


for all nonzero integer pairs (p,q)(p,q). This follows from the
exponent-three ratio bound by the same height comparison as in
Corollary 11.5.



Arctangent divided by the circular period.


Let r∈ℚ∖{0,1,−1}r\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0,1,-1\}. In ℚ⁡(i)\mathbb{Q}(i) put




a=1+i​r1−i​r,b=−1,λa=2​i​arctan⁡r,λb=i​π.a=\frac{1+ir}{1-ir},\qquad b=-1,\qquad\lambda_{a}=2i\arctan r,\qquad\lambda_{b}=i\pi.


The only roots of unity in ℚ⁡(i)\mathbb{Q}(i) are 1,−1,i,−i1,-1,i,-i;
the displayed aa is none of them for these finite rr.
Therefore 2​arctan⁡r/π2\arctan r/\pi is irrational, since a rational value
would make aa a root of unity. The two embeddings are good,
using the negative logarithms at complex conjugation. Apply
Corollary 12.2 to 2​arctan⁡r/π2\arctan r/\pi and then
the rational-scaling case of Lemma 3.2. Hence




2≤μ⁡(arctan⁡r/π)≤3.\boxed{\displaystyle 2\leq\mu(\arctan r/\pi)\leq 3.}


This is a statement for each fixed rr; no uniform dependence of
the approximation constant on its height is asserted.


More generally, an involution in a degree-2​m2m field that inverts
both aa and bb supplies two good embeddings and gives
2≤μ⁡(λa/λb)≤3​m2\leq\mu(\lambda_{a}/\lambda_{b})\leq 3m. This includes relative
norm-one elements in quadratic extensions of higher-degree fields
whenever the specified logarithm quotient is real and irrational.
The upper bound improves to three if the required matching extends
to every embedding. In contrast with the single-logarithm scalar
matching problem, no unconditional classification of all possible
ratio-matching conjugates is asserted here.



12.1 Algebraic slope normalization and mixed circular–real logarithms


The irrational-algebraic-slope interpolation, normalized matching
ratios, and mixed circular–real applications in this subsection have
checked Lean endpoints. The endpoint map in
Appendix A distinguishes their actual branch and
embedding assumptions from the rational-base special case.


Lemma 12.4 (Irrational algebraic slopes in the two-torus interpolation).

In the two-torus interpolation statement, one may replace all distinct
nonzero rational slopes rir_{i} by c​ricr_{i}, where cc is any fixed
irrational algebraic number. Suppose the paired centers (bj,aj)(b^{j},a^{j})
are distinct for all j≥0j\geq 0. No exceptional character slope has
to be removed. The same sufficient weight inequalities
and slope-independent successive affine-weight thresholds apply.



Proof.

Work over an algebraic closure of the field containing the data. The
commuting frame becomes




DY=Y∂Y−∑icri∂Xi,DZ=Z∂Z+∑i∂Xi,Di=∂Xi.D_{Y}=Y\partial_{Y}-\sum_{i}cr_{i}\partial_{X_{i}},\qquad D_{Z}=Z\partial_{Z}+\sum_{i}\partial_{X_{i}},\qquad D_{i}=\partial_{X_{i}}.


The coefficients c​ricr_{i} are constants. Consequently the frame still
preserves weighted degree, and in the local parameters




t=Y/bj−1,s=Z/aj−1,ui=Xi−log⁡(1+s)+c​ri​log⁡(1+t)t=Y/b^{j}-1,\quad s=Z/a^{j}-1,\quad u_{i}=X_{i}-\log(1+s)+cr_{i}\log(1+t)


it is (1+t)∂t,(1+s)∂s,∂ui(1+t)\partial_{t},(1+s)\partial_{s},\partial_{u_{i}}.
The dimension count, zero–pole comparison, persistent-component
construction, and normal-basis inequalities in the proof of
Lemma 11.2 therefore apply without any new
coefficient-dependent weight restriction.


If every affine coordinate direction is tangent to the persistent
component TT, the same torus-weight contradiction finishes the
argument. Otherwise choose the largest index ii whose affine
coordinate direction is nontangent. The separated normal-basis
argument says that every frame normal basis must contain DiD_{i}.
Equivalently, the tangent space of TT is contained in




ker⁡(d​Xi+c​ri​d​Y/Y−d​Z/Z).\ker(dX_{i}+cr_{i}\,dY/Y-dZ/Z).


Thus the displayed differential vanishes on TT.


Here the irrationality of c​ricr_{i} gives a stronger conclusion than
the rational-character argument. Restrict to an algebraic curve
meeting the smooth open part of TT, and pass to its complete smooth
normalization. At every point PP the residue of an exact
differential is zero, whereas the residue of d​Y/YdY/Y is
ordP⁡Y\operatorname{ord}_{P}Y. Hence




c​ri​ordP​Y=ordP⁡Z.cr_{i}\operatorname{ord}_{P}Y=\operatorname{ord}_{P}Z.


Both orders are integers and c​ri∉ℚcr_{i}\notin\mathbb{Q}, since
rir_{i} is a nonzero rational number. Both orders must therefore
vanish. The rational functions YY and ZZ have no zeros or poles
on the complete curve, so they are constant. The differential
identity then gives d​Xi=0dX_{i}=0, and characteristic zero makes XiX_{i}
constant as well.


These functions are consequently constant on TT: if one were
nonconstant, a curve through a general smooth point, chosen with
tangent direction on which its differential is nonzero, would give
a nonconstant restriction, contrary to the preceding argument.
In particular Y,Z,XiY,Z,X_{i} are constant on the putative violating
curve C⊆TC\subseteq T. Since the paired centers are distinct, CC
can meet only one center, say P0P_{0}.


On CC both torus shifts and all their logarithmic shifts vanish.
Thus the remaining local parameters restrict to
ul=Xl−Xl​(P0)u_{l}=X_{l}-X_{l}(P_{0}). Some XlX_{l} is nonconstant, because otherwise
every ambient coordinate would be constant on the curve. On the
complete normalization of CC, its branch contacts satisfy




∑P↦P0hP≤θwl​∑P↦P0ordP⁡(Xl−Xl​(P0))≤θ​deg⁡Xlwl≤θ​dW​(C).\sum_{P\mapsto P_{0}}h_{P}\leq\frac{\theta}{w_{l}}\sum_{P\mapsto P_{0}}\operatorname{ord}_{P}(X_{l}-X_{l}(P_{0}))\leq\theta\frac{\deg X_{l}}{w_{l}}\leq\theta\,d_{W}(C).


The middle inequality bounds the selected zeros by all zeros of
Xl−Xl​(P0)X_{l}-X_{l}(P_{0}), whose total multiplicity is the pole degree
deg⁡Xl\deg X_{l}. The last inequality follows directly from the
definition of the weighted pole degree dW​(C)d_{W}(C). Since
(1+σ)​θ<1(1+\sigma)\theta<1, this contradicts
dW​(C)<(1+σ)​∑hPd_{W}(C)<(1+\sigma)\sum h_{P}. No character-fiber projection is
needed in this case.


Finally, replacing the logarithms by sufficiently long Taylor
polynomials changes each uiu_{i} only by terms of strictly larger
weight; multiplication by the fixed scalar c​ricr_{i} does not change
that fact. The weighted jet ideals are preserved. The same
curve-to-jet argument as in Theorem 5.1 therefore
proves the required interpolation surjection. The determinant
equality in Lemma 11.3 also holds over the present
coefficient field, because its proof is a triangular change of
rows with diagonal one.
∎



Theorem 12.5 (Normalized quotient with matching embeddings).

Let a,b,c∈E×a,b,c\in E^{\times}, c∉ℚc\notin\mathbb{Q},
d=[E:ℚ]d=[E:\mathbb{Q}], and suppose a,ba,b
are not both roots of unity. Let x∈ℝ∖ℚx\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}.
Suppose that s≥1s\geq 1 embeddings σ:E↪ℂ\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
admit nonzero logarithms satisfying




eλa,σ=σ⁡(a),eλb,σ=σ⁡(b),λa,σ=σ⁡(c)​x​λb,σ.e^{\lambda_{a,\sigma}}=\sigma(a),\quad e^{\lambda_{b,\sigma}}=\sigma(b),\quad\lambda_{a,\sigma}=\sigma(c)x\lambda_{b,\sigma}.


Then




2≤μ⁡(x)≤3​d/s.2\leq\mu(x)\leq 3d/s.





Proof.

Dirichlet’s theorem, Lemma 3.1, gives the lower
bound because xx is real and irrational. For the upper bound,
suppose that an exponent ν>3​d/s\nu>3d/s admits arbitrarily large reduced
approximants ri=pi/qir_{i}=p_{i}/q_{i} with
|x−ri|≤qi−ν|x-r_{i}|\leq q_{i}^{-\nu}. Select distinct nonzero approximants
successively as in the rational-slope proof, and put
wi=⌈log⁡qi⌉w_{i}=\lceil\log q_{i}\rceil.


Use the matrix of that proof with torus centers (bj,aj)(b^{j},a^{j}) and
slopes c​ricr_{i}. Its entries lie in EE. The centers are distinct:
equality for two different indices would make both aa and bb
roots of unity. The preceding lemma therefore supplies a nonzero
full-row minor ΔH∈E\Delta_{H}\in E, with one fixed set of selected
columns. Those columns remain fixed throughout the norm argument.


We spell out the additional denominator clearing. Choose positive
integers u,va,vbu,v_{a},v_{b} such that u​c,va​a,vb​buc,v_{a}a,v_{b}b are algebraic
integers. With the previous LiL_{i} and GiG_{i}, set
Ui=u​qi​LiU_{i}=uq_{i}L_{i}. Then




Ui​(Gi​(s)−c​ri​Gi​(t))=u​qi​Li​Gi​(s)−pi​(u​c)​Li​Gi​(t)U_{i}\bigl(G_{i}(s)-cr_{i}G_{i}(t)\bigr)=uq_{i}L_{i}G_{i}(s)-p_{i}(uc)L_{i}G_{i}(t)


has algebraic-integer coefficients. Scale a column
Yh​Zk​XγY^{h}Z^{k}X^{\gamma} by




vb(K−1)​h​va(K−1)​k​∏iUiγi,v_{b}^{(K-1)h}v_{a}^{(K-1)k}\prod_{i}U_{i}^{\gamma_{i}},


and a row of transverse index β\beta by
∏iUi−βi\prod_{i}U_{i}^{-\beta_{i}}. Every resulting entry is an algebraic
integer: the torus factors clear (bj)h​(aj)k(b^{j})^{h}(a^{j})^{k} for j<Kj<K,
and the additive factors clear precisely the powers remaining
after [uβ][u^{\beta}] extraction.


As before, a nonzero determinant term gives
Ji=∑columnsγi−∑rowsβi≥0J_{i}=\sum_{\rm columns}\gamma_{i}-\sum_{\rm rows}\beta_{i}\geq 0.
Thus the total determinant multiplier CHC_{H} is a positive rational
integer, and CH​ΔHC_{H}\Delta_{H} is a nonzero algebraic integer. In the
notation of the preceding section,




log⁡CHM​H≤1−b¯H+Λ​F0v+log⁡uw∗+O⁡(K/w0),0≤b¯H≤θ.\frac{\log C_{H}}{MH}\leq 1-\overline{b}_{H}+\frac{\Lambda F_{0}}{v}+\frac{\log u}{w_{*}}+O(K/w_{0}),\qquad 0\leq\overline{b}_{H}\leq\theta.


Indeed the extra uu contributes
(log⁡u)​∑iJi/(M​H)≤(log⁡u)/w∗(\log u)\sum_{i}J_{i}/(MH)\leq(\log u)/w_{*}. No degree factor
is introduced until the field norm is taken.


At a good embedding σ\sigma, replace the truncated logarithms
by full logarithms in this same selected minor. The shear adds
only terms of weight greater than wi/θw_{i}/\theta, so its coefficient
matrix is triangular with diagonal one over EE. Applying
σ\sigma preserves this determinant identity. Define




fγ,P,σ​(z,w)=[vγ]​P​(ez,ew,w−σ⁡(c)​x​z+v1,…,w−σ⁡(c)​x​z+vm).f_{\gamma,P,\sigma}(z,w)=[v^{\gamma}]P(e^{z},e^{w},w-\sigma(c)xz+v_{1},\ldots,w-\sigma(c)xz+v_{m}).


The tests occur at




z=j​λb,σ+log⁡(1+t),w=j​λa,σ+log⁡(1+s).z=j\lambda_{b,\sigma}+\log(1+t),\qquad w=j\lambda_{a,\sigma}+\log(1+s).


By the matching hypothesis the constant term of
w−σ⁡(c)​x​zw-\sigma(c)xz is zero. The exact difference between the full
matrix’s iith additive coordinate and this common analytic
coordinate is




σ⁡(c)​(x−ri)​log⁡(1+t).\sigma(c)(x-r_{i})\log(1+t).


Consequently the previous finite translation identity and
bivariate Taylor collision estimate apply. The extra factor
σ⁡(c)\sigma(c) in each translation power contributes only
O⁡(1/w∗)O(1/w_{*}) to the normalized logarithmic bound. The fixed complex
logarithms merely change the constant multiplying the Cauchy
radius KK. Entire-function coefficient vectors need only be
common across rows within a single embedding; the determinant
argument never requires them to agree at different embeddings.


There are finitely many embeddings. We may therefore use one
upper error EanE_{\rm an} for all good embeddings, obtaining




log⁡|σ​ΔH|M​H≤Ean+o⁡(1)+max⁡{−c2​L+o⁡(1),−ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−b¯H)}.\frac{\log|\sigma\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm an}+o(1)+\max\{-c_{2}L+o(1),-\nu(A(1-\eta)-\overline{b}_{H})\}.


At a bad embedding, bound the truncated matrix directly on
|t|=|s|=1/2|t|=|s|=1/2 and |ui|=1|u_{i}|=1. The truncated logarithms are bounded
uniformly in their lengths, the slopes σ⁡(c)​ri\sigma(c)r_{i} are bounded
once the approximants lie in a fixed neighborhood of xx, and the
torus centers have logarithmic size O⁡(K)O(K). Coefficient extraction
and the determinant inequality then give




log⁡|σ​ΔH|M​H≤Ebad+o⁡(1),Ebad=O⁡(K/w0+1/v+1/w∗).\frac{\log|\sigma\Delta_{H}|}{MH}\leq E_{\rm bad}+o(1),\qquad E_{\rm bad}=O(K/w_{0}+1/v+1/w_{*}).


All constants here depend only on the fixed algebraic data and
chosen logarithms, not on HH or the heights of the selected
approximants.


Put D=d/sD=d/s. The nonzero integer norm satisfies




0≤log⁡|NE/ℚ​(CH​ΔH)|=d​log⁡CH+∑σlog⁡|σ​ΔH|.0\leq\log\bigl|N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(C_{H}\Delta_{H})\bigr|=d\log C_{H}+\sum_{\sigma}\log|\sigma\Delta_{H}|.


Using the preceding estimates and dividing by s​M​HsMH gives the
same two sufficient strict comparisons as in
Theorem 12.1:




ν⁡(A⁡(1−η)−θ)>D⁡(1−θ)+E,c2​L>D+E,\nu(A(1-\eta)-\theta)>D(1-\theta)+E,\qquad c_{2}L>D+E,


where E=D​Ear+Ean+(D−1)​EbadE=DE_{\rm ar}+E_{\rm an}+(D-1)E_{\rm bad} and
EarE_{\rm ar} includes the displayed denominator costs. For the
first comparison the gap at row grade b¯H\overline{b}_{H} is




ν​A​(1−η)−D−(ν−D)​b¯H.\nu A(1-\eta)-D-(\nu-D)\overline{b}_{H}.


Because ν>D\nu>D, its minimum on [0,θ][0,\theta] occurs at θ\theta.
The second comparison uses D⁡(1−b¯H)≤DD(1-\overline{b}_{H})\leq D.


Finally choose 2/3<b0<1−D/ν2/3<b_{0}<1-D/\nu, put
θ=1−δ\theta=1-\delta and A=1−b0​δA=1-b_{0}\delta, and take δ>0\delta>0
small. Then ν⁡(A−θ)>D⁡(1−θ)\nu(A-\theta)>D(1-\theta) and A3<θ2A^{3}<\theta^{2}.
Take η\eta small enough to retain the first strict gap. The
parameter choices of Section 11.4 make LL
arbitrarily large and K/w0,1/vK/w_{0},1/v arbitrarily small. After fixing
that finite dimension, choose the approximants successively to
meet the interpolation thresholds and make all 1/w∗1/w_{*} errors
small; finally let HH tend to infinity. Both comparisons hold,
contradicting the norm inequality. This excludes every
ν>3​d/s\nu>3d/s and proves the upper bound.
∎



For nonzero rational cc, the same conclusion follows from the
unnormalized quotient theorem applied to c​xcx, and the
rational-scaling case of Lemma 3.2.


Corollary 12.6 (A circular period divided by a rational logarithm).

For every positive rational a≠1a\neq 1 and every positive rational DD,




2≤μ⁡(πD​log⁡a)≤3.\boxed{\displaystyle 2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{D}\log a}\right)\leq 3.}


In particular 2≤μ⁡(π/log⁡2)≤32\leq\mu(\pi/\log 2)\leq 3.



Proof.

Write x=π/(D​log⁡a)x=\pi/(\sqrt{D}\log a). The denominator is nonzero, so
xx is real and nonzero. First verify the irrationality required
by Theorem 12.5. If xx were algebraic, then
i​D​xi\sqrt{D}\,x would be a nonreal algebraic number, hence an
algebraic irrational number. Gel’fond–Schneider applied to the
algebraic base a≠0,1a\neq 0,1 says that its power defined using the
real logarithm of aa is transcendental. But that power is




exp⁡(i​D​x​log⁡a)=exp⁡(i​π)=−1,\exp(i\sqrt{D}\,x\log a)=\exp(i\pi)=-1,


a contradiction. Thus xx is transcendental, in particular
irrational.


Now work in the imaginary quadratic field
E=ℚ⁡(−D)E=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-D}), using torus bases −1,a-1,a and
normalization c=i​Dc=i\sqrt{D}. The second torus base is not a root
of unity, since it is positive and different from one. At the
identity choose logarithms i​π,log⁡ai\pi,\log a. At conjugation choose
−i​π,log⁡a-i\pi,\log a; conjugation also sends cc to −c-c. Thus at
both embeddings the first logarithm equals σ⁡(c)​x\sigma(c)x times
the second. Both embeddings are good, so s=d=2s=d=2 and the
theorem gives 2≤μ⁡(x)≤32\leq\mu(x)\leq 3.
∎



For D=1D=1 the arithmetic also has a direct description: the
cleared nonzero determinant lies in ℤ⁡[i]\mathbb{Z}[i], whence




|CH​ΔH|≥1.|C_{H}\Delta_{H}|\geq 1.


More generally, a nonzero algebraic integer α\alpha in an
imaginary quadratic field has
|α|2=NE/ℚ​(α)∈ℤ>0|\alpha|^{2}=N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.
Thus |α|≥1|\alpha|\geq 1, and the principal arithmetic cost is
1−b¯H1-\overline{b}_{H}, exactly as in the rational two-logarithm
argument. This agrees with the averaged-norm calculation at
d/s=1d/s=1.


Corollary 12.7 (Arctangent divided by a rational logarithm).

For every nonzero rational rr and every positive rational a≠1a\neq 1,




2≤μ⁡(arctan⁡rlog⁡a)≤3.\boxed{\displaystyle 2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\arctan r}{\log a}\right)\leq 3.}





Proof.

Set x=arctan⁡r/log⁡ax=\arctan r/\log a, which is real and nonzero. If xx
were algebraic, the exponent 2​i​x2ix would be nonreal algebraic
and therefore irrational. Gel’fond–Schneider would make
exp⁡(2​i​x​log⁡a)\exp(2ix\log a) transcendental, whereas




exp⁡(2​i​x​log⁡a)=exp⁡(2​i​arctan⁡r)=1+i​r1−i​r∈ℚ⁡(i).\exp(2ix\log a)=\exp(2i\arctan r)=\frac{1+ir}{1-ir}\in\mathbb{Q}(i).


This contradiction proves transcendence, hence irrationality.


In E=ℚ⁡(i)E=\mathbb{Q}(i) use torus bases (1+i​r)/(1−i​r)(1+ir)/(1-ir) and aa,
normalization c=2​ic=2i, and logarithms 2​i​arctan⁡r,log⁡a2i\arctan r,\log a.
They are both nonzero. Conjugation negates the first logarithm
and cc, while preserving the second logarithm. Hence both
embeddings give the same normalized target xx. The base aa
is not a root of unity, even when the first base is one (as can
happen for r=±1r=\pm 1). The normalized theorem applies with
s=d=2s=d=2 and proves both bounds.
∎



Corollary 12.8 (A real quadratic logarithm divided by a rational logarithm).

Let D>0D>0 be rational and nonsquare, and let
a∈ℚ⁡(D)a\in\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}) be positive, different from 11, and
have norm 11 or −1-1. For every positive rational b≠1b\neq 1,




2≤μ⁡(log⁡aD​log⁡b)≤3.\boxed{\displaystyle 2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\log a}{\sqrt{D}\,\log b}\right)\leq 3.}


In particular, writing φ=(1+5)/2\varphi=(1+\sqrt{5})/2,




2≤μ⁡(log⁡(1+2)2​log⁡2)≤3,2≤μ⁡(log⁡φ5​log⁡2)≤3.2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\log(1+\sqrt{2})}{\sqrt{2}\log 2}\right)\leq 3,\qquad 2\leq\mu\!\left(\frac{\log\varphi}{\sqrt{5}\log 2}\right)\leq 3.





Proof.

First suppose NE/ℚ​(a)=1N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(a)=1, where
E=ℚ⁡(D)E=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}), and write
x=log⁡a/(D​log⁡b)x=\log a/(\sqrt{D}\log b). This is a nonzero real number.
If xx were algebraic and D​x\sqrt{D}\,x were irrational, then
Gel’fond–Schneider applied to bb would contradict




exp⁡(D​x​log⁡b)=a.\exp(\sqrt{D}\,x\log b)=a.


The remaining possibility is a nonzero rational exponent
D​x=u/v\sqrt{D}\,x=u/v, with integers u≠0u\neq 0 and v>0v>0. The real
logarithm equality then gives av=bua^{v}=b^{u}. Taking quadratic norms
gives




1=N​(a)v=N​(b)u=b2​u,1=N(a)^{v}=N(b)^{u}=b^{2u},


which is impossible for positive b≠1b\neq 1 and u≠0u\neq 0.
Thus xx is transcendental and in particular irrational.


Use torus bases a,ba,b and normalization c=Dc=\sqrt{D}.
At the identity take real logarithms log⁡a,log⁡b\log a,\log b.
The other conjugate of aa is a−1>0a^{-1}>0, while bb is fixed;
at that embedding take −log⁡a,log⁡b-\log a,\log b. Since conjugation
also negates cc, both embeddings satisfy the matching identity
for xx. The rational base bb is not a root of unity.
Theorem 12.5, with s=d=2s=d=2, therefore gives
2≤μ⁡(x)≤32\leq\mu(x)\leq 3.


If N⁡(a)=−1N(a)=-1, put a′=a2a^{\prime}=a^{2}. Then a′>0a^{\prime}>0, a′≠1a^{\prime}\neq 1, and
N⁡(a′)=1N(a^{\prime})=1. The norm-one case already proved that
log⁡a′/(D​log⁡b)=2​x\log a^{\prime}/(\sqrt{D}\log b)=2x is transcendental; hence xx
is also transcendental. Apply the normalized theorem directly
to torus bases a′,ba^{\prime},b and normalization c′=2​Dc^{\prime}=2\sqrt{D}.
At the two embeddings the first logarithms are
2​log⁡a2\log a and −2​log⁡a-2\log a, respectively, while the second is
log⁡b\log b and c′c^{\prime} changes sign. They therefore match the
unchanged target xx, proving the same two bounds.
Finally 1+21+\sqrt{2} and φ\varphi have norm minus one, which
establishes the displayed examples.
∎



For example, the circular-period corollary with D=1D=1 and a=2a=2
is equivalent to the following fixed two-term bound: for every
ε>0\varepsilon>0 a constant Cε>0C_{\varepsilon}>0 satisfies




|p​π+q​log⁡2|≥Cε​max⁡(1,|p|,|q|)−2−ε((p,q)∈ℤ2∖{(0,0)}).|p\pi+q\log 2|\geq C_{\varepsilon}\max(1,|p|,|q|)^{-2-\varepsilon}\qquad((p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}).


For p≠0p\neq 0, factor the form as
p​log⁡2​(π/log⁡2+q/p)p\log 2\,(\pi/\log 2+q/p) and apply the exponent-three bound;
when −q/p-q/p stays near the fixed target, the height is comparable
to |p||p|. Outside such a neighborhood, the form is bounded below
by a positive multiple of the height after also separating the
region of large |q/p||q/p|. The case p=0p=0 is immediate. Conversely,
substitution of (p,q)=(q,−p)(p,q)=(q,-p) in this linear-form inequality and
division by q​|log⁡2|q|\log 2| gives the corresponding exponent-three
lower bounds for rational approximation. These statements concern
fixed bases; no uniform dependence of CεC_{\varepsilon} on their
heights is asserted.




13 Quadratic logarithmic families: arithmetic and analytic proofs


This section supplies the arithmetic and analytic details for the quadratic
families used in the preceding discussion. The arithmetic assertions concern
rational parameters and ordinary rational approximation to real numbers.
The analytic assertions hold more generally for nonzero real parameters,
on the domains stated below. Each proof is accompanied by its exact Lean
source correspondence. The general interpolation and determinant theorem
is used through its established scalar-matching consequence; the work here
is to construct its algebraic data, verify every embedding, and identify
the actual real and complex functions to which it applies.


13.1 The common-scalar principle and rational linear combinations


For clarity, the all-embedding consequence needed throughout this section
has the following form. Let EE be a number field, let A,c∈EA,c\in E with
c≠0c\neq 0, and let x∈ℝ∖{0}x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}. Suppose that




exp⁡(σ⁡(c)​x)=σ⁡(A)for every embedding ​σ:E↪ℂ.\exp\bigl(\sigma(c)x\bigr)=\sigma(A)\qquad\text{for every embedding }\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}.

(13.1)

Then, for every ν>2\nu>2, there is an integer Q≥2Q\geq 2 such that




q−ν≤|x−pq|(p∈ℤ,q∈ℤ,q≥Q).q^{-\nu}\leq\left|x-\frac{p}{q}\right|\qquad(p\in\mathbb{Z},\ q\in\mathbb{Z},\ q\geq Q).

(13.2)

This is the precise all-denominator lower bound used here. It first implies
that xx is irrational: a rational xx would have exact representations
with arbitrarily large denominator multiples. It then implies
μ⁡(x)≤2\mu(x)\leq 2, and the real Dirichlet lower bound gives μ⁡(x)=2\mu(x)=2.


The scalar cc need not be an algebraic integer. Choose a positive integer
MM for which M​cMc is integral. Equation (13.1) gives




exp⁡(σ⁡(M​c)​x)=σ⁡(AM),\exp\bigl(\sigma(Mc)x\bigr)=\sigma(A^{M}),


so the integral-scalar theorem applies without changing xx. In particular,
none of the applications below presupposes that a rational square root
is integral, or that multiplication by an arbitrary algebraic number
preserves an irrationality exponent.


Lean correspondence.


The endpoint is
QuadraticPi.scalarMatchingLogarithm_eventualLowerBound
in QuadraticPi/FullEmbeddingLogarithmTheorem.lean; its hypotheses
are exactly the nonzero scalar, nonzero real target, and the identities
for all complex embeddings above. The integral-denominator reduction is
QuadraticPi.ScalarMatchingPower.exists_integral_matching_power.
The property (13.2) is
QuadraticPi.EventualLowerBound, defined in
QuadraticPi/ExponentConclusion.lean; the conclusion uses
QuadraticPi.irrationalityExponent_eq_two_of_eventualLowerBound.


Proposition 13.1 (Rational closure for one common scalar).

Let II be a finite index set. Suppose c∈E∖{0}c\in E\setminus\{0\},
Aj∈EA_{j}\in E, and xj∈ℝx_{j}\in\mathbb{R} satisfy




exp(σ(c)xj)=σ(Aj)(j∈I,σ:E↪ℂ).\exp\bigl(\sigma(c)x_{j}\bigr)=\sigma(A_{j})\qquad(j\in I,\ \sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}).


For qj∈ℚq_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}, put X=∑j∈Iqj​xjX=\sum_{j\in I}q_{j}x_{j}. If X≠0X\neq 0, then
XX satisfies (13.2) and μ⁡(X)=2\mu(X)=2.



Proof.

Choose one positive integer NN with N​qj=kj∈ℤNq_{j}=k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z} for every jj.
Each AjA_{j} is nonzero, since its image under a complex embedding is an
exponential. Define B=∏j∈IAjkj∈EB=\prod_{j\in I}A_{j}^{k_{j}}\in E. For each σ\sigma,




exp⁡(σ⁡(N​c)​X)\displaystyle\exp\bigl(\sigma(Nc)X\bigr)
=exp⁡(∑j∈Ikj​σ​(c)​xj)\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{j\in I}k_{j}\sigma(c)x_{j}\right)




=∏j∈Iexp⁡(σ⁡(c)​xj)kj=∏j∈Iσ​(Aj)kj=σ⁡(B).\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in I}\exp\bigl(\sigma(c)x_{j}\bigr)^{k_{j}}=\prod_{j\in I}\sigma(A_{j})^{k_{j}}=\sigma(B).


These equalities hold also for negative kjk_{j}, by the exponential identity
exp⁡(−z)=exp⁡(z)−1\exp(-z)=\exp(z)^{-1}. Since N​c≠0Nc\neq 0 and X≠0X\neq 0, the common-scalar
principle applies. The argument is an identity for the actual real sum
XX; it requires no addition formula for principal logarithms.
∎




Lean correspondence.


The integer NN and the coefficients kjk_{j} are supplied by

Corollary 13.2 (Rational linear combinations of rational logarithms).

Let a1,…,ana_{1},\ldots,a_{n} be positive rational numbers and
r1,…,rn∈ℚr_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}. If X=∑jrj​log⁡aj≠0X=\sum_{j}r_{j}\log a_{j}\neq 0, then
μ⁡(X)=2\mu(X)=2.



Proof.

Choose N≥1N\geq 1 with kj=N​rj∈ℤk_{j}=Nr_{j}\in\mathbb{Z} and set
A=∏jajkj∈ℚ>0A=\prod_{j}a_{j}^{k_{j}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}. The real product and integer-power
identities give N​X=log⁡ANX=\log A. In particular A≠1A\neq 1.
The rational-logarithm case of Theorem 4.1 gives
μ⁡(log⁡A)=2\mu(\log A)=2, and invariance under multiplication by the nonzero
rational number 1/N1/N gives the claim. Alternatively this is the case
E=ℚE=\mathbb{Q}, c=1c=1, xj=log⁡ajx_{j}=\log a_{j} of
Proposition 13.1.
∎



The latter formulation is a direct specialization of the completed Lean
theorem QuadraticPi.scalarMatching_rational_sum_irrationalityExponent_eq_two.
Thus a linear combination is permitted to involve several distinct
rational arguments. The hypothesis is that its actual value is nonzero,
not that each coefficient or each logarithm is nonzero.




13.2 Normalized real quadratic logarithms and the actual field norm


Theorem 13.3 (The coordinate form, including rational square parameters).

Let D∈ℚD\in\mathbb{Q}, D>0D>0, and u,v∈ℚu,v\in\mathbb{Q}. Put
α=u+v​D\alpha=u+v\sqrt{D}. If




α>0,α≠1,(u2−D​v2)2=1,\alpha>0,\qquad\alpha\neq 1,\qquad(u^{2}-Dv^{2})^{2}=1,

(13.3)

then




μ⁡(log⁡αD)=2.\mu\left(\frac{\log\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)=2.


The target also satisfies the all-denominator bound
(13.2).



Proof.

Take the actual field E=ℚ⁡(D)⊂ℝE=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})\subset\mathbb{R} and write
δ=D∈E\delta=\sqrt{D}\in E. Use




x=log⁡αD,c=2​δ,A=(u+v​δ)2.x=\frac{\log\alpha}{\sqrt{D}},\qquad c=2\delta,\qquad A=(u+v\delta)^{2}.


The parameter condition gives δ≠0\delta\neq 0, and positivity together with
α≠1\alpha\neq 1 gives x≠0x\neq 0. Every embedding σ:E↪ℂ\sigma:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
satisfies σ​(δ)2=D\sigma(\delta)^{2}=D, hence
σ⁡(δ)=D\sigma(\delta)=\sqrt{D} or −D-\sqrt{D}.


If σ⁡(δ)=D\sigma(\delta)=\sqrt{D}, then
σ⁡(A)=α2=exp⁡(2​log⁡α)=exp⁡(σ⁡(c)​x)\sigma(A)=\alpha^{2}=\exp(2\log\alpha)=\exp(\sigma(c)x).
For the other sign, put α′=u−v​D\alpha^{\prime}=u-v\sqrt{D}. The squared norm condition
gives




α2​(α′)2=(u2−D​v2)2=1,(α′)2=α−2.\alpha^{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}=(u^{2}-Dv^{2})^{2}=1,\qquad(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}=\alpha^{-2}.


Consequently
σ⁡(A)=α−2=exp⁡(−2​log⁡α)=exp⁡(σ⁡(c)​x)\sigma(A)=\alpha^{-2}=\exp(-2\log\alpha)=\exp(\sigma(c)x). This establishes
(13.1) for every embedding.


Squaring α\alpha is essential to this proof when u2−D​v2=−1u^{2}-Dv^{2}=-1:
the conjugate of α\alpha is then −α−1-\alpha^{-1}, whereas the conjugate
of AA is still exactly A−1A^{-1}. No logarithm of a negative real
conjugate is introduced. If DD is a rational square, EE has degree one;
the same proof checks its actual embedding and does not posit a second
embedding that the field does not possess.
∎



Lean correspondence.


In QuadraticPi/RationalNormalizedQuadraticLogarithmTheorem.lean,
the namespace QuadraticPi.RationalNormalizedQuadraticLogField
defines FieldOf, delta, gamma, algebraicArgument,
and target. Its lemmas embedding_delta,

QuadraticPi.rationalNormalizedQuadraticLogarithm_eventualLowerBound,
QuadraticPi.rationalNormalizedQuadraticLogarithm_irrational, and
QuadraticPi.rationalNormalizedQuadraticLogarithm_irrationalityExponent_eq_two.


Theorem 13.4 (The actual quadratic-field norm formulation).

Let D>0D>0 be rational and not a square in ℚ\mathbb{Q}, and let
E=ℚ⁡(D)⊂ℝE=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})\subset\mathbb{R}. For α∈E\alpha\in E with




α>0,α≠1,NE/ℚ​(α)∈{1,−1},\alpha>0,\qquad\alpha\neq 1,\qquad N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha)\in\{1,-1\},


one has μ⁡(log⁡α/D)=2\mu(\log\alpha/\sqrt{D})=2 and
(13.2).



Proof.

Here the field norm is the determinant of multiplication by α\alpha
on the actual two-dimensional ℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space EE.
The map from the quadratic algebra ℚ⁡[T]/(T2−D)\mathbb{Q}[T]/(T^{2}-D) to EE sending
TT to D\sqrt{D} is surjective because its image contains the field
generator. The nonsquare hypothesis makes the source a field, so this
nonzero homomorphism is injective and is an algebra isomorphism.
Thus α=u+v​D\alpha=u+v\sqrt{D} for rational u,vu,v. Relative to the basis
1,D1,\sqrt{D}, multiplication by α\alpha has matrix




(uD​vvu).\begin{pmatrix}u&Dv\\
v&u\end{pmatrix}.


Its determinant is u2−D​v2u^{2}-Dv^{2}. Transport of the determinant norm through
the algebra isomorphism therefore gives
NE/ℚ​(α)=u2−D​v2N_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha)=u^{2}-Dv^{2}.
Either allowed value of the field norm implies
(u2−D​v2)2=1(u^{2}-Dv^{2})^{2}=1, and Theorem 13.3 applies.
∎




Lean correspondence.

using the determinant-defined Algebra.norm and its invariance under

QuadraticPi.realQuadraticLogarithm_norm_irrationalityExponent_eq_two.


Remark 13.5.

The nonsquare hypothesis in Theorem 13.4 cannot simply
be deleted from its norm interpretation. For square DD, the embedded
field ℚ⁡(D)\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}) is ℚ\mathbb{Q}, and its field norm is the identity, rather
than a determinant in two independent coordinates. Theorem
13.3 is the separately proved statement that
allows square DD, with its explicit coordinate condition. Neither
theorem assumes that α\alpha is an algebraic integer.



Corollary 13.6 (Rational linear combinations of conjugate logarithms).

Fix D∈ℚ>0D\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}. For finitely many uj,vj,qj∈ℚu_{j},v_{j},q_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}, suppose




αj=uj+vj​D>0,(uj2−D​vj2)2=1.\alpha_{j}=u_{j}+v_{j}\sqrt{D}>0,\qquad(u_{j}^{2}-Dv_{j}^{2})^{2}=1.


If




X=1D​∑jqj​log⁡αj≠0,X=\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}\sum_{j}q_{j}\log\alpha_{j}\neq 0,


then μ⁡(X)=2\mu(X)=2 and the all-denominator lower bound
(13.2) holds for XX.



Proof.

Write αj∗=uj−vj​D\alpha_{j}^{*}=u_{j}-v_{j}\sqrt{D} and
εj=uj2−D​vj2∈{1,−1}\varepsilon_{j}=u_{j}^{2}-Dv_{j}^{2}\in\{1,-1\}. The elementary identity
αj​αj∗=εj\alpha_{j}\alpha_{j}^{*}=\varepsilon_{j} gives




(αj∗)2=αj−2.(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{2}=\alpha_{j}^{-2}.


Squaring is useful here because it treats both norm signs with the same
positive real logarithms. We do not take the real logarithm of
αj∗\alpha_{j}^{*} when that conjugate is negative.


Choose a positive integer NN such that kj=N​qj∈ℤk_{j}=Nq_{j}\in\mathbb{Z} for every jj,
and put




E=ℚ⁡(D),c=2​N​D,B=∏jαj2​kj∈E×.E=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}),\qquad c=2N\sqrt{D},\qquad B=\prod_{j}\alpha_{j}^{2k_{j}}\in E^{\times}.


Negative kjk_{j} cause no difficulty, since every αj\alpha_{j} is positive.
At the specified real embedding, the ordinary real logarithm gives




exp⁡(c​X)=exp⁡(2​∑jkj​log⁡αj)=B.\exp(cX)=\exp\left(2\sum_{j}k_{j}\log\alpha_{j}\right)=B.


If DD is nonsquare, the other embedding τ\tau sends D\sqrt{D} to
−D-\sqrt{D}. Consequently




τ⁡(c)=−c,τ⁡(B)=∏j(αj∗)2​kj=∏jαj−2​kj=B−1=exp⁡(τ⁡(c)​X).\tau(c)=-c,\qquad\tau(B)=\prod_{j}(\alpha_{j}^{*})^{2k_{j}}=\prod_{j}\alpha_{j}^{-2k_{j}}=B^{-1}=\exp(\tau(c)X).


Thus the same real target XX has the required exponential identity at
both embeddings of EE. If DD is square, E=ℚE=\mathbb{Q} has only its identity
embedding, and the first identity already verifies every embedding.
Since c≠0c\neq 0 and X≠0X\neq 0, Theorem 10.1 with all embeddings
matched proves the upper bound two. Its irrationality argument and
Dirichlet’s theorem give the lower bound two. Equivalently, the
all-embedding endpoint in Section 13.1 gives
(13.2) directly.
∎




Completed Lean theorem.


This entire assertion, including rational coefficients of either sign,
is formalized in
QuadraticPi/NormalizedQuadraticLogarithmLinearCombination.lean as
QuadraticPi.normalizedQuadraticLogarithm_rational_sum_eventualLowerBound
and
QuadraticPi.normalizedQuadraticLogarithm_rational_sum_irrationalityExponent_eq_two.
The exact exponent declaration was rechecked with its transitive axioms
in the audit of 12 October 2026 described in
Section A. Individual terms, or their coefficients,
may vanish; the hypothesis concerns the final sum. The parameter DD is
common to all terms. No assertion about unrelated quadratic fields is
needed or used.




13.3 Rational values of the positive and circular families


For D>0D>0, define the real branch




fD​(x)=1D​log⁡1+D​x1−D​x,|D​x|<1.f_{D}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}\log\frac{1+\sqrt{D}\,x}{1-\sqrt{D}\,x},\qquad|\sqrt{D}\,x|<1.

(13.4)

For d>0d>0, the corresponding negative-parameter branch is




f−d​(x)=2d​arctan⁡(d​x),x∈ℝ.f_{-d}(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{d}}\arctan(\sqrt{d}\,x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

(13.5)

In the next two theorems the parameters and evaluation points are
rational; the wider real-variable definitions will be used for analysis.


Theorem 13.7 (Positive rational parameters).

Let D∈ℚ>0D\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0} and r∈ℚ∖{0}r\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\} with
|D​r|<1|\sqrt{D}\,r|<1. Then μ​(fD​(r))=2\mu(f_{D}(r))=2.
More generally, every nonzero finite rational linear combination of
admissible fD​(rj)f_{D}(r_{j}), at the same DD, has exponent two.
Both conclusions have the all-denominator lower bound
(13.2).



Proof.

The branch condition makes 1+D​r1+\sqrt{D}\,r and 1−D​r1-\sqrt{D}\,r positive.
Rationalizing the denominator gives




1+D​r1−D​r=u+v​D,u=1+D​r21−D​r2,v=2​r1−D​r2.\frac{1+\sqrt{D}\,r}{1-\sqrt{D}\,r}=u+v\sqrt{D},\qquad u=\frac{1+Dr^{2}}{1-Dr^{2}},\quad v=\frac{2r}{1-Dr^{2}}.

(13.6)

Here 1−D​r2=(1−D​r)​(1+D​r)>01-Dr^{2}=(1-\sqrt{D}\,r)(1+\sqrt{D}\,r)>0, so all displayed
denominators are nonzero. Direct calculation gives




u2−D​v2=(1+D​r2)2−4​D​r2(1−D​r2)2=1.u^{2}-Dv^{2}=\frac{(1+Dr^{2})^{2}-4Dr^{2}}{(1-Dr^{2})^{2}}=1.


The quotient in (13.6) equals one only
when 2​D​r=02\sqrt{D}\,r=0, which would force r=0r=0.
Theorem 13.3 proves the single-value assertion.
For a finite sum, use the same coordinates separately for each rjr_{j} and
apply Corollary 13.6. Evaluation points equal to
zero are allowed in the sum, provided that its total value is nonzero.
∎



Lean correspondence.


The coordinate and domain facts are denominator_pos,
argument_pos, argument_eq_coordinates, coordinate_norm,
and argument_ne_one in the namespace
QuadraticPi.PositiveQuadraticLogarithm, module
QuadraticPi/PositiveQuadraticLogarithmCoordinates.lean.

and

with corresponding eventualLowerBound declarations.


Theorem 13.8 (Circular rational parameters and an additional normalized constant).

Let d∈ℚ>0d\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}. For every nonzero r∈ℚr\in\mathbb{Q},




μ⁡(2​arctan⁡(d​r)d)=2,μ⁡(πd)=2.\mu\left(\frac{2\arctan(\sqrt{d}\,r)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)=2,\qquad\mu\left(\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{d}}\right)=2.


More generally, if q0,qj,rj∈ℚq_{0},q_{j},r_{j}\in\mathbb{Q} and




X=q0​πd+∑jqj​f−d​(rj)≠0,X=q_{0}\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{d}}+\sum_{j}q_{j}f_{-d}(r_{j})\neq 0,


then μ⁡(X)=2\mu(X)=2. Every assertion also has
(13.2). There is no size restriction on the
rational rjr_{j} in this theorem.



Proof.

Work in the actual imaginary quadratic field
E=ℚ⁡(i​d)⊂ℂE=\mathbb{Q}(\mathrm{i}\sqrt{d})\subset\mathbb{C}, and put δ=i​d\delta=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{d}.
Every embedding sends δ\delta to δ\delta or −δ-\delta, because
δ2=−d\delta^{2}=-d. For a rational rr, use the algebraic base




Ar=1+r​δ1−r​δ∈E.A_{r}=\frac{1+r\delta}{1-r\delta}\in E.


The denominator is nonzero: at the distinguished embedding its real
part is one. The elementary identity




exp⁡(2​i​arctan⁡t)=1+i​t1−i​t(t∈ℝ)\exp(2\mathrm{i}\arctan t)=\frac{1+\mathrm{i}t}{1-\mathrm{i}t}\qquad(t\in\mathbb{R})

(13.7)

shows that exp⁡(δ​f−d​(r))=Ar\exp(\delta f_{-d}(r))=A_{r} at that embedding. Under the
negative sign, the base becomes Ar−1A_{r}^{-1} and the exponential also
becomes its reciprocal. Thus every embedding matches with the same
scalar c=δc=\delta. If r≠0r\neq 0, then arctan⁡(d​r)≠0\arctan(\sqrt{d}\,r)\neq 0, so
the common-scalar theorem yields exponent two.


For x0=π/dx_{0}=\pi/\sqrt{d}, the same scalar gives
exp⁡(δ​x0)=exp⁡(i​π)=−1\exp(\delta x_{0})=\exp(\mathrm{i}\pi)=-1. At the other embedding the value
is exp⁡(−i​π)=−1\exp(-\mathrm{i}\pi)=-1 as well, so the algebraic base is A0=−1A_{0}=-1.
This proves the assertion for x0x_{0}, and adjoining this one extra term
to the finite family permits Proposition 13.1
to prove the final statement.
∎

13.4 Real differentiation and the convergent real series


In the remainder of this section, DD may be any nonzero real number.
Define fDf_{D} by (13.4) for D>0D>0 and by
(13.5) with d=−Dd=-D for D<0D<0.
Its stated real domain is




ℐD={{x∈ℝ:|D​x|<1},D>0,ℝ,D<0.\mathcal{I}_{D}=\begin{cases}\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|\sqrt{D}\,x|<1\},&D>0,\\
\mathbb{R},&D<0.\end{cases}

(13.8)



Proposition 13.9 (Derivative and differential identity).

For x∈ℐDx\in\mathcal{I}_{D},




1−D​x2>0,fD′​(x)=21−D​x2,(1−D​x2)​fD′​(x)=2,fD​(0)=0.1-Dx^{2}>0,\qquad f_{D}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{2}{1-Dx^{2}},\qquad(1-Dx^{2})f_{D}^{\prime}(x)=2,\qquad f_{D}(0)=0.





Proof.

If D>0D>0, put s=Ds=\sqrt{D}. The two factors 1±s​x1\pm sx are positive
on ℐD\mathcal{I}_{D}, and hence (1−s​x)​(1+s​x)=1−D​x2>0(1-sx)(1+sx)=1-Dx^{2}>0.
With Q⁡(x)=(1+s​x)/(1−s​x)Q(x)=(1+sx)/(1-sx), the quotient rule gives




Q′​(x)=2​s(1−s​x)2,1s​Q′​(x)Q⁡(x)=21−D​x2.Q^{\prime}(x)=\frac{2s}{(1-sx)^{2}},\qquad\frac{1}{s}\frac{Q^{\prime}(x)}{Q(x)}=\frac{2}{1-Dx^{2}}.


The real logarithm is differentiable at the positive value Q⁡(x)Q(x),
which proves the desired formula.


For D=−d<0D=-d<0, the denominator is 1+d​x2>01+dx^{2}>0 for every real xx.
The chain rule for the arctangent gives




dd​x​(2​arctan⁡(d​x)d)=21+(d​x)2=21+d​x2.\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{2\arctan(\sqrt{d}\,x)}{\sqrt{d}}\right)=\frac{2}{1+(\sqrt{d}\,x)^{2}}=\frac{2}{1+dx^{2}}.


Multiplication by the nonzero denominator proves the differential
identity in either case. Direct substitution gives fD​(0)=0f_{D}(0)=0.
∎



Lean correspondence.


The module QuadraticPi/QuadraticLogarithmicFamilyDerivative.lean
defines the real functions positiveValue, circularValue,
value, and the domain predicate Admissible, all under
QuadraticPi.QuadraticLogarithmicFamily. Its endpoints are
value_hasDerivAt, value_deriv, value_ode, and
value_zero; value_pos_rat and value_neg_rat
identify these real functions with the arithmetic rational-input
definitions. The domain predicate excludes D=0D=0. No derivative-two
claim at that excluded parameter is part of the theorem.


Theorem 13.10 (The real power series).

For D∈ℝ∖{0}D\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\} and |D|​x2<1|D|x^{2}<1,




fD​(x)=2​∑n=0∞Dn​x2​n+12​n+1,f_{D}(x)=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{D^{n}x^{2n+1}}{2n+1},

(13.9)

and the displayed series is absolutely convergent.



Proof.

For D>0D>0, set t=D​xt=\sqrt{D}\,x, so |t|<1|t|<1. The standard convergent
logarithm series gives




log⁡(1+t)−log⁡(1−t)=2​∑n≥0t2​n+12​n+1.\log(1+t)-\log(1-t)=2\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{t^{2n+1}}{2n+1}.


Both 1+t1+t and 1−t1-t are positive, so the left side is exactly
log⁡((1+t)/(1−t))\log((1+t)/(1-t)). Divide by D\sqrt{D} and use




(D​x)2​n+1=D​Dn​x2​n+1(\sqrt{D}\,x)^{2n+1}=\sqrt{D}\,D^{n}x^{2n+1}


to obtain (13.9).
For D=−d<0D=-d<0, use instead
arctan⁡t=∑n≥0(−1)n​t2​n+1/(2​n+1)\arctan t=\sum_{n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}t^{2n+1}/(2n+1) with
t=d​xt=\sqrt{d}\,x and |t|<1|t|<1. Multiplication by 2/d2/\sqrt{d} gives
the coefficient (−d)n=Dn(-d)^{n}=D^{n}.


For either sign, put ρ=|D|​x2<1\rho=|D|x^{2}<1. The absolute value of the
nnth summand is at most 2​|x|​ρn2|x|\rho^{n}, so a convergent geometric
series proves absolute convergence. The circular function itself is
defined for all real xx; the series equality asserted here uses the
smaller open interval |D|​x2<1|D|x^{2}<1.
∎




Lean correspondence.

13.5 The principal complex branch and its derivative


Fix D∈ℝ∖{0}D\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\} and prescribe the square root




δD={D,D>0,i​−D,D<0.δD2=D,δD≠0.\delta_{D}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{D},&D>0,\\
\mathrm{i}\sqrt{-D},&D<0.\end{cases}\qquad\delta_{D}^{2}=D,\quad\delta_{D}\neq 0.


Let Log\operatorname{Log} denote the principal complex logarithm and set




FD​(z)=1δD​Log⁡1+δD​z1−δD​z.F_{D}(z)=\frac{1}{\delta_{D}}\operatorname{Log}\frac{1+\delta_{D}z}{1-\delta_{D}z}.

(13.10)

The logarithm is holomorphic on the slit plane
ℂ∖(−∞,0]\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]. All complex differentiability assertions
below refer to the displayed principal branch.


Proposition 13.11 (Agreement with the real branches).

For every x∈ℐDx\in\mathcal{I}_{D}, the value FD​(x)F_{D}(x) is real and equals
fD​(x)f_{D}(x). When D<0D<0, this holds for every finite real xx.



Proof.

For D>0D>0, the quotient is positive real, so its principal complex
logarithm is the real logarithm. Suppose D=−d<0D=-d<0 and put
t=d​xt=\sqrt{d}\,x. Identity (13.7) gives




1+i​t1−i​t=exp⁡(2​i​arctan⁡t).\frac{1+\mathrm{i}t}{1-\mathrm{i}t}=\exp(2\mathrm{i}\arctan t).


The strict inequalities −π/2<arctant<π/2-\pi/2<\arctan t<\pi/2 imply
−π<2​arctan⁡t<π-\pi<2\arctan t<\pi. Therefore the principal logarithm of this
exponential is exactly 2​i​arctan⁡t2\mathrm{i}\arctan t, with no multiple of 2​π​i2\pi\mathrm{i}.
Dividing by i​d\mathrm{i}\sqrt{d} yields f−d​(x)f_{-d}(x).
These inequalities also show that the quotient lies in the slit plane
for every real tt.
∎



Lean correspondence.


The definitions complexRoot and complexValue, and the
lemmas principal_log_cayley, cayley_mem_slitPlane,
circularValue_complex_branch, positiveValue_complex_branch,
and value_complex_branch, are in the namespace
QuadraticPi.QuadraticLogarithmicFamily, module
QuadraticPi/QuadraticLogarithmicFamilyComplexBranch.lean.
The key branch step applies Complex.log_exp with the explicit
arctangent bounds; agreement is not inferred from equality of exponentials
alone.


Theorem 13.12 (Principal-branch differentiation on a proved domain).

If




1+δD​z1−δD​z∈ℂ∖(−∞,0],\frac{1+\delta_{D}z}{1-\delta_{D}z}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0],


then 1−D​z2≠01-Dz^{2}\neq 0 and




FD′​(z)=21−D​z2.F_{D}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{2}{1-Dz^{2}}.


In particular, FDF_{D} is holomorphic on the open disc




𝒟D={z∈ℂ:|δD​z|<1},\mathcal{D}_{D}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\delta_{D}z|<1\},

(13.11)

and there it satisfies (1−D​z2)​FD′​(z)=2(1-Dz^{2})F_{D}^{\prime}(z)=2 and FD​(0)=0F_{D}(0)=0.



Proof.

Write w=δD​zw=\delta_{D}z. Membership of the quotient in the slit plane
ensures that the quotient is nonzero and that both 1−w1-w and 1+w1+w
are nonzero. Hence




1−D​z2=(1−w)​(1+w)≠0.1-Dz^{2}=(1-w)(1+w)\neq 0.


The complex quotient and chain rules, followed by the derivative of
the principal logarithm on the slit plane, give




FD′​(z)=1δD​2​δD(1−w)2​1−w1+w=21−D​z2.F_{D}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{\delta_{D}}\frac{2\delta_{D}}{(1-w)^{2}}\frac{1-w}{1+w}=\frac{2}{1-Dz^{2}}.


To check the entire disc, rationalize the complex denominator:




Re⁡1+w1−w=1−|w|2|1−w|2>0(|w|<1).\operatorname{Re}\frac{1+w}{1-w}=\frac{1-|w|^{2}}{|1-w|^{2}}>0\qquad(|w|<1).


Thus the quotient lies in the right half-plane and in particular in
the slit plane. The set (13.11) is open and contains
zero. The derivative theorem holds at each of its points, giving
holomorphy and the differential identity. Substitution at zero gives
Log⁡1/δD=0\operatorname{Log}1/\delta_{D}=0.
∎




Lean correspondence.

13.6 The complex Taylor expansion of the specified branch


Theorem 13.13 (Complex Taylor expansion with absolute convergence).

For real D≠0D\neq 0 and z∈𝒟Dz\in\mathcal{D}_{D},




FD​(z)=2​∑n=0∞Dn​z2​n+12​n+1,∑n=0∞|2​Dn​z2​n+12​n+1|<∞.F_{D}(z)=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{D^{n}z^{2n+1}}{2n+1},\qquad\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|\frac{2D^{n}z^{2n+1}}{2n+1}\right|<\infty.

(13.12)

The sum is the same principal-logarithm function as in
(13.10).



Proof.

First let |w|<1|w|<1. Apply the convergent complex arctangent series to
ζ=−i​w\zeta=-\mathrm{i}w:




Arctan⁡ζ=∑n≥0(−1)n​ζ2​n+12​n+1.\operatorname{Arctan}\zeta=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{n}\zeta^{2n+1}}{2n+1}.


With the principal-logarithm convention used in that series theorem,




Arctan⁡ζ=−i2​Log⁡1+i​ζ1−i​ζ.\operatorname{Arctan}\zeta=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\operatorname{Log}\frac{1+\mathrm{i}\zeta}{1-\mathrm{i}\zeta}.


Consequently 2​i​Arctan⁡(−i​w)=Log⁡((1+w)/(1−w))2\mathrm{i}\operatorname{Arctan}(-\mathrm{i}w)=\operatorname{Log}((1+w)/(1-w)).
For each nn,




2​i​(−1)n​(−i​w)2​n+1=2​w2​n+1,2\mathrm{i}(-1)^{n}(-\mathrm{i}w)^{2n+1}=2w^{2n+1},


so multiplication of the convergent series by 2​i2\mathrm{i} yields




Log⁡1+w1−w=∑n≥02​w2​n+12​n+1.\operatorname{Log}\frac{1+w}{1-w}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{2w^{2n+1}}{2n+1}.


This derivation identifies the actual principal logarithm and does not
use an unrestricted identity Log⁡(a/b)=Log⁡a−Log⁡b\operatorname{Log}(a/b)=\operatorname{Log}a-\operatorname{Log}b.


Now take w=δD​zw=\delta_{D}z and divide by δD≠0\delta_{D}\neq 0. The relation
δD2=D\delta_{D}^{2}=D gives




(δD​z)2​n+1=δD​Dn​z2​n+1,(\delta_{D}z)^{2n+1}=\delta_{D}D^{n}z^{2n+1},


which proves the first identity in (13.12).
Also ρ=|D|​|z|2=|δD​z|2<1\rho=|D||z|^{2}=|\delta_{D}z|^{2}<1, and the absolute value of its
nnth summand is bounded by 2​|z|​ρn2|z|\rho^{n}. The geometric series proves
the second assertion.
∎



Lean correspondence.


The source QuadraticPi/QuadraticLogarithmicFamilyComplexTaylor.lean
proves rotated_arctan_odd_pow, principal_log_cayley_hasSum,
and complexRoot_mul_odd_pow in the namespace
QuadraticPi.QuadraticLogarithmicFamily. The final convergence,
sum identity, and absolute convergence statements are, respectively,

complexValue_series_absolutely_summable. The input series theorem
is Mathlib’s Complex.hasSum_arctan.


Remark 13.14 (Scope of the analytic identification).

The arithmetic exponent-two results above apply to rational parameters
under their stated nonvanishing and branch hypotheses. The analytic
proofs establish the displayed functions, derivatives, and convergent
series for nonzero real parameters. The circular real branch is identified
with the principal complex expression on the entire real axis, whereas
the Taylor statements use the open convergence disc. No assertion about
boundary sums, a classification of maximal complex continuation domains,
or uniqueness for the differential equation is needed for these results
or included in their stated formal endpoints.






14 Verified field reductions and reciprocal logarithms


This section supplies a detailed verification companion to
Theorem 10.4 and Corollaries 10.5
and 10.6. It records the additional algebraic arguments
that have now been checked in Lean, including actual intermediate fields,
the counts of their embeddings, explicit polynomial identities, and the
choice of the real square-root branch. The invariant-field bound,
reciprocal-trace consequences, and normalized character-sum application
are checked. The converse classification of all matching embeddings is
also checked relative to the explicit standard Gel’fond–Schneider
input; that input is not needed for the direct upper-bound proof.


All filenames in this section are relative to
anc/lean/QuadraticPi/ in the accompanying
source distribution. Unless another namespace is specified, a module
M.lean uses the namespace QuadraticPi.M.
The sources and their separate #check and
#print axioms audit files give the exact Lean types.


14.1 The precise approximation conclusion


For clarity, the upper estimates proved in the new modules are first
established in the following stronger, explicit form. For a real number
xx and a finite real cutoff κ\kappa, let ℰ⁡(x,κ)\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa) denote




∀ν>κ∃Q≥2∀p∈ℤ∀q∈ℤ≥Q,q−ν≤|x−pq|.\forall\nu>\kappa\quad\exists Q\geq 2\quad\forall p\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\forall q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq Q},\qquad q^{-\nu}\leq\left|x-\frac{p}{q}\right|.

(14.1)

No coprimality condition is imposed on p,qp,q. In Lean this predicate is
QuadraticPi.EventualLowerBoundAbove.


The passage from (14.1) to an exponent bound
has two logically distinct parts. First xx is irrational: if
x=p0/q0x=p_{0}/q_{0}, arbitrarily large multiples (n​p0,n​q0)(np_{0},nq_{0}) give zero error,
contradicting the positive lower bound. Second, for each ν>κ\nu>\kappa,
every reduced rational approximation with error less than q−νq^{-\nu}
has denominator less than the corresponding QQ. There are only
finitely many such rationals in a bounded neighborhood of xx.
Consequently the approximation spectrum is bounded above by κ\kappa.
Dirichlet’s theorem supplies the lower endpoint 22. Thus




ℰ⁡(x,κ)⟹x∉ℚ,2≤μ⁡(x)≤κ.\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x\notin\mathbb{Q},\qquad 2\leq\mu(x)\leq\kappa.

(14.2)

This order of reasoning avoids using the real-valued supremum before
its nonempty, bounded approximation spectrum has been established.


Lean correspondence.


The definition and the implication are in
GeneralExponentConclusion.lean, in the namespace
QuadraticPi; the relevant declarations
are irrational_of_eventualLowerBoundAbove,
bddAbove_approximationExponents_of_eventualLowerBoundAbove,
and irrationalityExponent_bounds_of_eventualLowerBoundAbove.
The Diophantine input used below is the previously checked partial
embedding theorem
QuadraticPi.partialScalarMatchingLogarithm_eventualLowerBound
in PartialEmbeddingLogarithmTheorem.lean. The field
reductions do not replace that input by an assumption about a
logarithm’s exponent.




14.2 Invariant coordinates and compatible embedding actions


Let EE be a field containing ℚ\mathbb{Q}, let a,c∈Ea,c\in E with c≠0c\neq 0, and
let ι,σ:E⟶ℂ\iota,\sigma:E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C} be field embeddings. Define
the actual intermediate field




K=ℚ⁡(c2,a+a−1,c⁡(a−a−1))⊆E.K=\mathbb{Q}\bigl(c^{2},\ a+a^{-1},\ c(a-a^{-1})\bigr)\subseteq E.

(14.3)

For applications aa is nonzero; the coordinate lemma in Lean is even
formulated without this additional hypothesis, using the total inverse
operation in a field. Throughout the mathematical discussion of
logarithms we retain a≠0a\neq 0.


Proposition 14.1 (Exact coordinate criterion).

The embeddings σ\sigma and ι\iota agree on KK if and only if
one of the following two compatible actions holds:





σ⁡(c)=ι⁡(c),\displaystyle\sigma(c)=\iota(c),
σ⁡(a)\displaystyle\quad\sigma(a)
=ι⁡(a),\displaystyle=\iota(a),




σ⁡(c)=−ι⁡(c),\displaystyle\sigma(c)=-\iota(c),
σ⁡(a)\displaystyle\quad\sigma(a)
=ι​(a)−1.\displaystyle=\iota(a)^{-1}.


If x∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R} satisfies
exp⁡(ι⁡(c)​x)=ι⁡(a)\exp(\iota(c)x)=\iota(a), every such σ\sigma also satisfies
exp⁡(σ⁡(c)​x)=σ⁡(a)\exp(\sigma(c)x)=\sigma(a).



Proof.

Agreement on KK is equivalent to agreement on its three displayed
generators: addition, multiplication and inversion preserve agreement,
and both embeddings agree on ℚ\mathbb{Q}. Write
A=ι⁡(a)A=\iota(a), B=σ⁡(a)B=\sigma(a), C=ι⁡(c)C=\iota(c) and C′=σ⁡(c)C^{\prime}=\sigma(c).
The generator equalities are




(C′)2=C2,B+B−1=A+A−1,C′​(B−B−1)=C⁡(A−A−1).(C^{\prime})^{2}=C^{2},\qquad B+B^{-1}=A+A^{-1},\qquad C^{\prime}(B-B^{-1})=C(A-A^{-1}).


Since C≠0C\neq 0, the first equality gives C′=CC^{\prime}=C or C′=−CC^{\prime}=-C.
In the first case cancel CC in the third equality and add it to
the second; this gives 2​B=2​A2B=2A. In the second case the same cancellation
gives B−B−1=−(A−A−1)B-B^{-1}=-(A-A^{-1}), so adding the second equality gives
2​B=2​A−12B=2A^{-1}. These are exactly the asserted actions. Conversely,
substitution of either action fixes all three generators.


The first action preserves the exponential equation directly. For the
second action use




exp⁡(−ι⁡(c)​x)=exp⁡(ι⁡(c)​x)−1=ι​(a)−1.\exp(-\iota(c)x)=\exp(\iota(c)x)^{-1}=\iota(a)^{-1}.


No statement about other matching embeddings is needed in this argument.
∎



If E=ℚ⁡(a,c)E=\mathbb{Q}(a,c), then E=K⁡(c)E=K(c), because




a=12​((a+a−1)+c⁡(a−a−1)c).a=\frac{1}{2}\left((a+a^{-1})+\frac{c(a-a^{-1})}{c}\right).

(14.4)

Also cc is a root of the monic polynomial X2−c2∈K⁡[X]X^{2}-c^{2}\in K[X].
It follows that E/KE/K is finite and




[E:K]∈{1,2}.[E:K]\in\{1,2\}.

(14.5)

The finite-dimensionality assertion matters in the implementation:
Lean’s finite-rank value is zero for an infinite-dimensional vector
space, so proving only a numerical inequality finrank≤2\operatorname{finrank}\leq 2 would not suffice. The construction E=K⁡(c)E=K(c) and integrality
of cc establish finite-dimensionality and positive rank explicitly.


Lean correspondence.


In ScalarMatchingInvariantField.lean, the definitions are
invariantField and FixesInvariantField.
The coordinate and action equivalences are
fixes_iff_coordinates,
coordinates_iff_actions, and
fixes_iff_actions; the exponential implication is
fixing_embedding_matches. Formula (14.4)
is used in adjoin_invariantField_c_eq_top.
The degree argument is recorded by
c_isIntegral_invariantField,
finrank_invariantField_le_two,
finrank_invariantField_pos,
finiteDimensional_invariantField, and
finrank_invariantField_eq_one_or_two.




14.3 Counting a proved matching set and cancelling the field degree


Theorem 14.2 (Verified invariant-field bound).

Let EE be a number field, a,c∈Ea,c\in E, c≠0c\neq 0, and let
ι:E⟶ℂ\iota:E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C} be an embedding. Suppose x∈ℝ∖{0}x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and exp⁡(ι⁡(c)​x)=ι⁡(a)\exp(\iota(c)x)=\iota(a). With KK defined
by (14.3), one has




ℰ(x,2[K:ℚ]),x∉ℚ,2≤μ(x)≤2[K:ℚ].\mathcal{E}(x,2[K:\mathbb{Q}]),\qquad x\notin\mathbb{Q},\qquad 2\leq\mu(x)\leq 2[K:\mathbb{Q}].


In particular, [K:ℚ]=1[K:\mathbb{Q}]=1 implies μ⁡(x)=2\mu(x)=2.
The hypothesis E=ℚ⁡(a,c)E=\mathbb{Q}(a,c) is not required.



Proof.

Consider the set of embeddings




S={σ:E⟶ℂ:σ|K=ι|K}.S=\{\sigma:E\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}:\sigma|_{K}=\iota|_{K}\}.


There are exactly [E:K][E:K] such embeddings. Indeed, use ι|K\iota|_{K}
to regard ℂ\mathbb{C} as a KK-algebra. The extensions of this embedding
are precisely the KK-algebra embeddings of EE into ℂ\mathbb{C}.
The finite extension E/KE/K is separable in characteristic zero, and
the target is algebraically closed, so their number is [E:K][E:K].
The set is nonempty because it contains ι\iota.


By Proposition 14.1, every member of SS
is a matching embedding. Apply the partial embedding bound to this
specific set. It gives




ℰ(x,2[E:ℚ]|S|).\mathcal{E}\left(x,\frac{2[E:\mathbb{Q}]}{|S|}\right).


The field tower identity now gives




2[E:ℚ]|S|=2[K:ℚ][E:K][E:K]=2[K:ℚ].\frac{2[E:\mathbb{Q}]}{|S|}=\frac{2[K:\mathbb{Q}][E:K]}{[E:K]}=2[K:\mathbb{Q}].


The denominator is positive. Apply (14.2);
when [K:ℚ]=1[K:\mathbb{Q}]=1, its two endpoint inequalities are equalities.
∎



This proof requires a sufficiently large set of matching embeddings,
not a classification of every matching embedding. In the minimal field
E=ℚ⁡(a,c)E=\mathbb{Q}(a,c) the constructed set has one or two members by
(14.5). In a larger ambient field it can have more
members, and the same tower cancellation still applies.
The converse assertion in Theorem 10.4, that an arbitrary
matching embedding agrees with ι\iota on KK, now has a checked adapter
to the explicit Gel’fond–Schneider input. It is not required by the
checked proof of Theorem 14.2.


Lean correspondence.


ScalarMatchingInvariantEmbeddings.lean constructs the finite
set of actual ring embeddings and proves its exact membership and
cardinality in exists_embeddings_over_intermediateField.
Its minimal-field specializations are
exists_fixing_embeddings and
exists_matching_embeddings.
In ScalarMatchingInvariantDegreeTheorem.lean, the tower
cancellation is degree_ratio; the conclusions are
invariantField_eventualLowerBound,
invariantField_irrational,
invariantField_irrationalityExponent_bounds, and
invariantField_irrationalityExponent_eq_two.




14.4 The reciprocal trace and its square-root parameter


For a>0a>0, a≠1a\neq 1, put




T⁡(a)=a+a−1,X⁡(a)=log⁡aa−a−1.T(a)=a+a^{-1},\qquad X(a)=\frac{\log a}{a-a^{-1}}.


The denominator is nonzero: equality a=a−1a=a^{-1} would give a2=1a^{2}=1,
and positivity would force a=1a=1. Likewise log⁡a≠0\log a\neq 0, so X⁡(a)≠0X(a)\neq 0.


Theorem 14.3 (Reciprocal-trace form).

If a>0a>0 is algebraic and a≠1a\neq 1, then




ℰ(X(a),2[ℚ(T(a)):ℚ]),2≤μ(X(a))≤2[ℚ(T(a)):ℚ].\mathcal{E}\bigl(X(a),2[\mathbb{Q}(T(a)):\mathbb{Q}]\bigr),\qquad 2\leq\mu(X(a))\leq 2[\mathbb{Q}(T(a)):\mathbb{Q}].


These statements do not assume a reciprocal minimal polynomial.



Proof.

Take E=ℚ⁡(a)⊆ℝE=\mathbb{Q}(a)\subseteq\mathbb{R} and c=a−a−1c=a-a^{-1}. At the distinguished
real embedding, c​X​(a)=log⁡acX(a)=\log a, so the exponential matching equation
is elog⁡a=ae^{\log a}=a. Moreover




c2=T​(a)2−4,c⁡(a−a−1)=c2.c^{2}=T(a)^{2}-4,\qquad c(a-a^{-1})=c^{2}.


The invariant field (14.3) is therefore exactly
ℚ⁡(T⁡(a))\mathbb{Q}(T(a)). Apply Theorem 14.2.
The formal proof also transports this field from its representation
inside ℚ⁡(a)\mathbb{Q}(a) to the actual subfield of ℝ\mathbb{R} generated by T⁡(a)T(a),
so the degree in the displayed result is the degree of the stated real
number, rather than an unconnected auxiliary field.
∎



For a real algebraic parameter t>2t>2, define




s=t2−4,A⁡(t)=t+s2,V⁡(t)=log⁡A⁡(t)s.s=\sqrt{t^{2}-4},\qquad A(t)=\frac{t+s}{2},\qquad V(t)=\frac{\log A(t)}{s}.


Here s>0s>0 and A⁡(t)>1A(t)>1. Since
(t+s)​(t−s)=4(t+s)(t-s)=4, one has the exact identities




A​(t)−1=t−s2,A⁡(t)+A​(t)−1=t,A⁡(t)−A​(t)−1=s.A(t)^{-1}=\frac{t-s}{2},\qquad A(t)+A(t)^{-1}=t,\qquad A(t)-A(t)^{-1}=s.

(14.6)

The number ss is algebraic because its square is algebraic; hence
A⁡(t)A(t) is algebraic. Applying Theorem 14.3 gives




ℰ(V(t),2[ℚ(t):ℚ]),2≤μ(V(t))≤2[ℚ(t):ℚ].\mathcal{E}(V(t),2[\mathbb{Q}(t):\mathbb{Q}]),\qquad 2\leq\mu(V(t))\leq 2[\mathbb{Q}(t):\mathbb{Q}].

(14.7)

In particular, for every rational t>2t>2,




μ⁡(log⁡((t+t2−4)/2)t2−4)=2.\boxed{\displaystyle\mu\left(\frac{\log((t+\sqrt{t^{2}-4})/2)}{\sqrt{t^{2}-4}}\right)=2.}

(14.8)

This rational specialization needs no nonsquare hypothesis on t2−4t^{2}-4.

14.5 Inversion and the unambiguous root above one


Both the target and trace are invariant under inversion:




X⁡(a−1)=−log⁡a−(a−a−1)=X⁡(a),T⁡(a−1)=T⁡(a).X(a^{-1})=\frac{-\log a}{-(a-a^{-1})}=X(a),\qquad T(a^{-1})=T(a).


For a>0a>0, a≠1a\neq 1, one has T⁡(a)>2T(a)>2, since
a⁡(T⁡(a)−2)=(a−1)2>0a(T(a)-2)=(a-1)^{2}>0. Furthermore




T​(a)2−4=|a−a−1|.\sqrt{T(a)^{2}-4}=|a-a^{-1}|.


Thus A⁡(T⁡(a))=aA(T(a))=a when a>1a>1, and A⁡(T⁡(a))=a−1A(T(a))=a^{-1} when 0<a<10<a<1.
In either case




X⁡(a)=V⁡(T⁡(a)).X(a)=V(T(a)).

(14.9)

This proves the equivalence of the two formulations for all admissible
positive aa, including the interval (0,1)(0,1). No branch choice is left
implicit. The chosen representative b∈{a,a−1}b\in\{a,a^{-1}\} above one
has the same trace and target; if aa is algebraic, so is bb.

14.6 Reciprocal minimal polynomials and the full fixed field


Suppose additionally that the actual monic minimal polynomial
f∈ℚ⁡[X]f\in\mathbb{Q}[X] of aa satisfies




frev=f.f^{\mathrm{rev}}=f.

(14.10)

The hypothesis concerns the minimal polynomial itself; a reciprocal
polynomial that merely annihilates aa does not supply this assumption.


Theorem 14.4 (Verified reciprocal field structure).

Let a>0a>0 be algebraic with a≠1a\neq 1 and assume
(14.10). In E=ℚ⁡(a)E=\mathbb{Q}(a) there is an involutive
ℚ\mathbb{Q}-automorphism τ\tau with τ⁡(a)=a−1\tau(a)=a^{-1}. If
K=ℚ⁡(a+a−1)K=\mathbb{Q}(a+a^{-1}), then




[E:K]=2,E⟨τ⟩=K,[E:ℚ]=2[K:ℚ].[E:K]=2,\qquad E^{\langle\tau\rangle}=K,\qquad[E:\mathbb{Q}]=2[K:\mathbb{Q}].


Consequently, if deg⁡f=2​m\deg f=2m, then [K:ℚ]=m[K:\mathbb{Q}]=m and




ℰ⁡(X⁡(a),deg⁡f),2≤μ⁡(X⁡(a))≤deg⁡f.\mathcal{E}(X(a),\deg f),\qquad 2\leq\mu(X(a))\leq\deg f.





Proof.

Because a≠0a\neq 0, reversal of a polynomial gives
frev​(a−1)=a−deg⁡f​f​(a)=0f^{\mathrm{rev}}(a^{-1})=a^{-\deg f}f(a)=0.
By reciprocity, a−1a^{-1} is therefore another root of ff.
The minimal polynomials of aa and a−1a^{-1} agree. Sending the
generator aa to a−1a^{-1} gives an isomorphism
ℚ⁡(a)⟶ℚ⁡(a−1)\mathbb{Q}(a)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(a^{-1}); these are the same subfield,
since each generator is the inverse of the other. This constructs
τ\tau as an automorphism of the actual simple field. Applying τ\tau
twice fixes aa, and hence fixes every element of EE.


The automorphism fixes a+a−1a+a^{-1}, so it fixes KK. It is nontrivial:
if a=a−1a=a^{-1}, positivity would give a=1a=1. On the other hand aa
satisfies X2−(a+a−1)​X+1∈K⁡[X]X^{2}-(a+a^{-1})X+1\in K[X], so [E:K]≤2[E:K]\leq 2.
Nontriviality excludes degree one, proving [E:K]=2[E:K]=2.


Let J=E⟨τ⟩J=E^{\langle\tau\rangle} be the standard fixed field.
Then K⊆J⊆EK\subseteq J\subseteq E. It is a proper subfield because
τ⁡(a)≠a\tau(a)\neq a. The tower identity, or equivalently the divisibility
[E:J]∣[E:K]=2[E:J]\mid[E:K]=2, now forces [E:J]=2[E:J]=2 and J=KJ=K.
This is a full fixed-field equality, not just the observation that the
trace is fixed. Finally apply the tower identity over ℚ\mathbb{Q} and the
bound of Theorem 14.3.
∎

14.7 A completely explicit quartic example


Take




t=2+2,a=2+2+2+4​22,x=log⁡a2+4​2.t=2+\sqrt{2},\qquad a=\frac{2+\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{2+4\sqrt{2}}}{2},\qquad x=\frac{\log a}{\sqrt{2+4\sqrt{2}}}.


Since t2−4=2+4​2t^{2}-4=2+4\sqrt{2}, these are precisely a=A⁡(t)a=A(t) and x=V⁡(t)x=V(t).
The trace field is
ℚ⁡(t)=ℚ⁡(2)\mathbb{Q}(t)=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}), of degree two. Therefore the checked parameter
theorem gives




ℰ⁡(x,4),2≤μ⁡(x)≤4.\mathcal{E}(x,4),\qquad 2\leq\mu(x)\leq 4.

(14.11)

The exact degree of the logarithm’s algebraic argument can also be
verified without an unproved irreducibility assumption. From
a+a−1=2+2a+a^{-1}=2+\sqrt{2}, multiply by aa to get




a2−2​a+1=2​a.a^{2}-2a+1=\sqrt{2}\,a.


Squaring and collecting coefficients gives




f⁡(a)=0,f⁡(X)=X4−4​X3+4​X2−4​X+1.f(a)=0,\qquad f(X)=X^{4}-4X^{3}+4X^{2}-4X+1.


The translation by one is




f⁡(X+1)=X4−2​X2−4​X−2.f(X+1)=X^{4}-2X^{2}-4X-2.


This polynomial is Eisenstein at 22: its leading coefficient is not
divisible by 22, all other coefficients are divisible by 22, and
its constant coefficient is not divisible by 44. Thus it is
irreducible over ℤ\mathbb{Z} and over ℚ\mathbb{Q}. Translation is a polynomial
ring automorphism, so ff is irreducible over ℚ\mathbb{Q} as well. Since it
is monic and annihilates aa, it is the minimal polynomial of aa.
Consequently




[ℚ(a):ℚ]=4,[ℚ(t):ℚ]=2.[\mathbb{Q}(a):\mathbb{Q}]=4,\qquad[\mathbb{Q}(t):\mathbb{Q}]=2.


This degree-four computation is additional algebraic information;
it does not sharpen (14.11) to μ⁡(x)=2\mu(x)=2.
The exact exponent of this example remains undetermined by the results
proved here.


Lean correspondence.

trace_field_eq, trace_field_finrank,
alpha_trace, alpha_quartic_eq_zero,
definingPolynomial_aeval,
quartic_eventualLowerBound, and
quartic_irrationalityExponent_bounds.

QuadraticPi.ReciprocalQuarticExample.
Its declarations definingPolynomial_taylor_one,
