Eisenstein argument. The exact minimal polynomial and degree are
alpha_minpoly, alpha_minpoly_natDegree,
and alpha_field_finrank.




14.8 Quadratic-character logarithmic combinations: complete descent


We now give the complete argument for Corollary 10.6,
including the quadratic field, the actual field norm, the real logarithmic
identity, and the exponent-two conclusion. All these steps have formal
counterparts in the saved Lean sources. The earlier description of this
family as only a completed product construction is superseded by the
degree, norm, logarithm, exponent, stabilizer and input-field increments
of 11 October 2026. The normalization and the nonzero-sum condition are
essential parts of the statement.


The actual product and its action.


Let E/FE/F be a field extension whose full automorphism group
G=AutF⁡(E)G=\operatorname{Aut}_{F}(E) is finite. Let
χ:G→{1,−1}\chi:G\to\{1,-1\} be a group homomorphism. For a∈E×a\in E^{\times} set




βχ​(a)=∏g∈Gg​(a)χ⁡(g).\beta_{\chi}(a)=\prod_{g\in G}g(a)^{\chi(g)}.


The product is over the full automorphism group; when aa does not
generate EE, repeated conjugate values retain their multiplicities.


Proposition 14.5 (Signed product and kernel descent).

The element βχ​(a)\beta_{\chi}(a) is nonzero and, for every h∈Gh\in G,




h⁡(βχ​(a))=βχ​(a)χ⁡(h).h(\beta_{\chi}(a))=\beta_{\chi}(a)^{\chi(h)}.

(14.12)

Consequently βχ​(a)∈Eker⁡χ\beta_{\chi}(a)\in E^{\ker\chi}; every automorphism
outside the kernel sends it to its reciprocal.



Proof.

Every g⁡(a)g(a) is nonzero, so every signed power and their product is
nonzero. Applying hh to the product, and setting k=h​gk=hg, gives




h⁡(βχ​(a))=∏g∈G(h​g)​(a)χ⁡(g)=∏k∈Gk​(a)χ⁡(h−1​k).h(\beta_{\chi}(a))=\prod_{g\in G}(hg)(a)^{\chi(g)}=\prod_{k\in G}k(a)^{\chi(h^{-1}k)}.


Since χ​(h)−1=χ⁡(h)\chi(h)^{-1}=\chi(h), the last exponent is
χ⁡(h)​χ​(k)\chi(h)\chi(k). Integer-power laws in E×E^{\times} therefore give
(14.12). For h∈ker⁡χh\in\ker\chi the value
is fixed, which is exactly membership in the kernel fixed field.
∎




Degree and norm.


Suppose now that E/FE/F is finite Galois and that χ\chi is nontrivial.
It takes both values 1,−11,-1, so
[G:kerχ]=2[G:\ker\chi]=2. The finite Galois correspondence gives




K:=Eker⁡χ,[K:F]=2.K:=E^{\ker\chi},\qquad[K:F]=2.

(14.13)

The kernel is normal, so K/FK/F is Galois. Choose h∉ker⁡χh\notin\ker\chi.
Its restriction ι\iota to KK is the nonidentity automorphism of
K/FK/F: otherwise hh would belong to the subgroup fixing KK, which
is precisely ker⁡χ\ker\chi. Equation (14.12)
gives ι⁡(βχ)=βχ−1\iota(\beta_{\chi})=\beta_{\chi}^{-1}. Thus the genuine
field norm, defined as the determinant of multiplication, satisfies




NK/F​(βχ)=βχ​ι​(βχ)=1.N_{K/F}(\beta_{\chi})=\beta_{\chi}\,\iota(\beta_{\chi})=1.

(14.14)

Here we used the standard Galois expression of the norm as the product
over all embeddings of K/FK/F. There are exactly two such embeddings.
The norm is taken in K/FK/F, not in the larger ambient extension E/FE/F.


Lemma 14.6 (Compatible real quadratic realization).

Let K/ℚK/\mathbb{Q} have degree two and let ψ:K↪ℝ\psi:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R} be
a field embedding. There is a positive nonsquare rational number DD
and a ℚ\mathbb{Q}-isomorphism




e:K⟶ℚ⁡(D)⊂ℝsuch thate⁡(x)=ψ⁡(x)(x∈K).e:K\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})\subset\mathbb{R}\quad\hbox{such that}\quad e(x)=\psi(x)\quad(x\in K).


These choices depend on K,ψK,\psi, and can be made before any element
whose logarithm is to be considered.



Proof.

Choose r∈K∖ℚr\in K\setminus\mathbb{Q} and write its monic minimal polynomial
as X2+b​X+cX^{2}+bX+c, with b,c∈ℚb,c\in\mathbb{Q}. The element s=r+b/2s=r+b/2 generates
KK and satisfies




s2=D:=b2/4−c∈ℚ.s^{2}=D:=b^{2}/4-c\in\mathbb{Q}.


It is nonzero, since otherwise rr would be rational. Injectivity of
ψ\psi gives D=ψ​(s)2>0D=\psi(s)^{2}>0. If D=q2D=q^{2} for q∈ℚq\in\mathbb{Q}, then
(s−q)​(s+q)=0(s-q)(s+q)=0 would make ss rational, a contradiction. Thus DD
is nonsquare. The real identity ψ​(s)2=D\psi(s)^{2}=D gives
ψ⁡(s)=D\psi(s)=\sqrt{D} or −D-\sqrt{D}, and either root generates the
same subfield of ℝ\mathbb{R}. It follows that ψ⁡(K)=ℚ⁡(D)\psi(K)=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}).
Regard ψ\psi as an isomorphism onto this image to obtain ee.
The Lean proof obtains s2=Ds^{2}=D using the quadratic instance of
Mathlib’s cyclic/Kummer extension theorem and proves the same positivity,
nonsquareness and compatibility statements.
∎



Theorem 14.7 (Exponent two for the character logarithmic sum).

Let E/ℚE/\mathbb{Q} be finite Galois, let
χ:Autℚ⁡(E)→{1,−1}\chi:\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{Q}}(E)\to\{1,-1\} be nontrivial,
and fix a field embedding ϕ:E↪ℝ\phi:E\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}.
There exists a positive nonsquare D∈ℚD\in\mathbb{Q}, chosen before aa, such
that every a∈Ea\in E satisfying




ϕ⁡(g⁡(a))>0(g∈G),βχ​(a)≠1\phi(g(a))>0\quad(g\in G),\qquad\beta_{\chi}(a)\neq 1

(14.15)

obeys




μ⁡(1D​∑g∈Gχ⁡(g)​log⁡ϕ⁡(g⁡(a)))=2.\mu\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)\log\phi(g(a))\right)=2.

(14.16)

One may instead specify any positive nonsquare rational DD for which




ϕ⁡(Eker⁡χ)=ℚ⁡(D)as subfields of ​ℝ.\phi(E^{\ker\chi})=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D})\quad\hbox{as subfields of }\mathbb{R}.


No canonical, integral or squarefree choice of DD is asserted.



Proof.

First form K=Eker⁡χK=E^{\ker\chi}, whose degree is two by
(14.13). Apply
Lemma 14.6 to the restriction of ϕ\phi
to KK. This chooses DD and the compatible map ee once for the
field and the character, before choosing aa.


Now take an admissible aa. The identity automorphism in
(14.15) shows a≠0a\neq 0. Let
b=βχ​(a)∈Kb=\beta_{\chi}(a)\in K. All factors of ϕ⁡(b)\phi(b) are positive,
so ϕ⁡(b)>0\phi(b)>0. The ordinary real logarithm is additive on finite
products of positive numbers and satisfies log⁡(z−1)=−log⁡z\log(z^{-1})=-\log z
for z>0z>0. Therefore




log⁡ϕ⁡(b)=∑g∈Gχ⁡(g)​log⁡ϕ⁡(g⁡(a)).\log\phi(b)=\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)\log\phi(g(a)).

(14.17)

The hypothesis b≠1b\neq 1 and injectivity of ϕ\phi imply
ϕ⁡(b)≠1\phi(b)\neq 1; its logarithm is consequently nonzero.


The element e⁡(b)e(b) of the actual real field ℚ⁡(D)\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}) has
real value ϕ⁡(b)>0\phi(b)>0, is different from one, and has norm one.
The last statement follows from (14.14) and
invariance of the field norm under ℚ\mathbb{Q}-algebra isomorphisms.
The real quadratic norm logarithm theorem
(Corollary 10.2) applies to e⁡(b)e(b) and gives




μ⁡(log⁡(e⁡(b))/D)=2.\mu\bigl(\log(e(b))/\sqrt{D}\bigr)=2.


Compatibility of ee with ϕ\phi and
(14.17) identify its argument with that
in (14.16). For a specified DD with the asserted
field-image equality, ϕ|K\phi|_{K} itself supplies the compatible
isomorphism onto ℚ⁡(D)\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{D}); the remaining steps are identical.
∎



This theorem concerns a normalized signed sum. It does not say
that every unnormalized combination of conjugate logarithms has exponent
two, nor that arbitrary rational coefficients may replace the character
without another argument. The assumptions that all the evaluated
conjugates are positive and that the actual signed product is not one
are explicit. Under positivity the latter is equivalent to nonvanishing
of the real logarithmic sum, by (14.17).


Proposition 14.8 (Cancellation and the necessary input field).

For finite Galois E/FE/F and a≠0a\neq 0, a nontrivial product
βχ​(a)≠1\beta_{\chi}(a)\neq 1 implies




Eker⁡χ⊆F⁡(a).E^{\ker\chi}\subseteq F(a).


If χ\chi is nontrivial, it follows that 2∣[F(a):F]2\mid[F(a):F].
Thus every nonzero input of odd degree has βχ​(a)=1\beta_{\chi}(a)=1.
With a real embedding and positive evaluated conjugates, its character
logarithmic sum is zero.



Proof.

We first establish cancellation in the more general setting of a finite
automorphism group, without assuming finite degree or Galoisness.
Suppose h⁡(a)=ah(a)=a and χ⁡(h)=−1\chi(h)=-1. Write




P+=∏χ⁡(g)=1g⁡(a),P−=∏χ⁡(g)=−1g⁡(a),βχ​(a)=P+/P−.P_{+}=\prod_{\chi(g)=1}g(a),\qquad P_{-}=\prod_{\chi(g)=-1}g(a),\qquad\beta_{\chi}(a)=P_{+}/P_{-}.


Right multiplication g↦g​hg\mapsto gh bijects the two sign classes,
while (g​h)​(a)=g⁡(h⁡(a))=g⁡(a)(gh)(a)=g(h(a))=g(a). Hence P+=P−P_{+}=P_{-}. All factors are
nonzero, so P−≠0P_{-}\neq 0 and βχ​(a)=1\beta_{\chi}(a)=1. This argument uses
right multiplication and does not assume that GG is commutative.


By contraposition, βχ​(a)≠1\beta_{\chi}(a)\neq 1 forces every automorphism
fixing aa into ker⁡χ\ker\chi. For finite Galois E/FE/F this says
Gal⁡(E/F⁡(a))⊆ker⁡χ\operatorname{Gal}(E/F(a))\subseteq\ker\chi. Fixed fields
reverse inclusions, and the Galois correspondence now gives
Eker⁡χ⊆F⁡(a)E^{\ker\chi}\subseteq F(a). If χ\chi is nontrivial, the first
field has degree two over FF, so the tower law makes [F(a):F][F(a):F]
even. Odd degree therefore forces product one. Under positivity,
(14.17) then gives the zero sum.
∎



Containment of the quadratic field, or even input degree, is necessary
for a nontrivial product; neither condition is asserted sufficient.

15 Graded approximation and a coefficient-uniform principle


The preceding fixed-value theorems and the results in this section have
different quantifiers. Here the evaluation point is required to be
sufficiently small. Once that point has been fixed, the resulting
inequalities hold simultaneously for a varying target polynomial and a
varying rational approximant. We give the construction in detail because
this uniformity cannot be deduced from the irrationality exponent of each
individual target.


The rank input is the general differential-system part of
Fischler–Rivoal’s work [20], not their irrationality theorem for
values of EE-functions. Logarithms are not EE-functions. We use
Theorem 3, Lemma 1, the lift of Section 4.5, and Theorem 4 of that paper.
In particular the required independence is over
ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}, not merely over ℚ\mathbb{Q}.


15.1 Adjacent grades and the rank theorem


Fix a positive integer mm and analytic functions f1,…,fmf_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
satisfying a rational inhomogeneous differential system




f′=s0+S​f,s0∈ℚ​(z)m,S∈Mm​(ℚ⁡(z)).f^{\prime}=s_{0}+Sf,\qquad s_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}(z)^{m},\quad S\in M_{m}(\mathbb{Q}(z)).

(15.1)

For an integer G≥1G\geq 1, write




Θ={κ∈ℤ≥0m:|κ|=G},Ω=Θ∪{λ:|λ|=G−1},\Theta=\{\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m}:|\kappa|=G\},\qquad\Omega=\Theta\cup\{\lambda:|\lambda|=G-1\},






θ=(G+m−1m−1),ω=θ+(G+m−2m−1),ωθ=1+GG+m−1.\theta=\binom{G+m-1}{m-1},\qquad\omega=\theta+\binom{G+m-2}{m-1},\qquad\frac{\omega}{\theta}=1+\frac{G}{G+m-1}.


The letters Ω,Θ\Omega,\Theta in this section denote finite index sets,
not periods. A polynomial indexed by a vector with a negative coordinate
is, by convention, zero.


The lift can be defined without a lengthy matrix formula. Solve the
signed dual system




a′=−S𝖳​a,c′=s0𝖳​a.a^{\prime}=-S^{\mathsf{T}}a,\qquad c^{\prime}=s_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}a.


Differentiation preserves the list of coordinates




aκ(|κ|=G),c​aλ(|λ|=G−1).a^{\kappa}\quad(|\kappa|=G),\qquad ca^{\lambda}\quad(|\lambda|=G-1).

(15.2)

Consequently this list satisfies Y′=𝒜​YY^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}Y for an
ω×ω\omega\times\omega rational matrix 𝒜\mathcal{A}.
For a polynomial column P=(Pλ)λ∈ΩP=(P_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Omega} put




∇P=P′+𝒜𝖳​P.\nabla P=P^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{T}}P.


If Y′=𝒜​YY^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}Y, the product rule gives




dkd​zk​(P𝖳​Y)=(∇kP)𝖳​Y.\frac{d^{k}}{dz^{k}}(P^{\mathsf{T}}Y)=(\nabla^{k}P)^{\mathsf{T}}Y.

(15.3)

This identity explains why covariant derivatives of the polynomial
column produce derivatives of the same small analytic remainder.


Continuation convention for the rank statement.


The value at the ordinary point is obtained by one coherent analytic
continuation of the original solution germ along a path avoiding the
system’s poles. The lifted Nilsson solutions and contact estimates refer
to that same continuation. This is the branch convention used by the
checked rank endpoint; unrelated values on different branches are not
combined.


Lemma 15.1 (Short derivatives give full rank).

Let r∈ℚ∖{0}r\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\} be an ordinary point of the
system, and suppose that 1,f1​(r),…,fm​(r)1,f_{1}(r),\ldots,f_{m}(r) are linearly
independent over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}. Suppose also that the
solutions of the lifted system used below belong to the Nilsson class
at zero, and that multiplication by their coefficients lowers the
order of a holomorphic function by at most a fixed constant.
Choose




0<η≤1ω+1,σ=ω−ηθ>1.0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{\omega+1},\qquad\sigma=\frac{\omega-\eta}{\theta}>1.


There is a constant CrC_{r} such that the following holds for all
sufficiently large integers MM. If Pλ∈ℚ⁡[z]P_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Q}[z],
not all zero, have degrees less than MM, and




Rκ=Pκ+∑j=1mPκ−ejfj,ord0Rκ≥K:=⌊σM⌋(κ∈Θ),R_{\kappa}=P_{\kappa}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}P_{\kappa-e_{j}}f_{j},\qquad\operatorname{ord}_{0}R_{\kappa}\geq K:=\lfloor\sigma M\rfloor\quad(\kappa\in\Theta),

(15.4)

then the columns




∇kP​(r)k!,0≤k≤⌊η​M⌋+Cr,\frac{\nabla^{k}P(r)}{k!},\qquad 0\leq k\leq\lfloor\eta M\rfloor+C_{r},


span ℚΩ\mathbb{Q}^{\Omega}.



Here a Nilsson function is a finite sum of terms
zα​(log⁡z)jz^{\alpha}(\log z)^{j} multiplied by holomorphic functions, with the
order interpreted as the smallest relevant real part of α\alpha.
Ordinary points at zero cause no loss of order. The explicit transport
used later has only powers of z\sqrt{z} and is also covered.


Proof.

Normalize the homogeneous dual solution by a⁡(r)=ρa(r)=\rho, where
ρ1,…,ρm\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{m} are independent formal parameters, and set
c⁡(z)=∑jaj​(z)​fj​(z)c(z)=\sum_{j}a_{j}(z)f_{j}(z). Equation
(15.1) gives c′=s0𝖳​ac^{\prime}=s_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}a.
Taking the coefficient of ρκ\rho^{\kappa}, κ∈Θ\kappa\in\Theta, in
(15.2) gives θ\theta solutions
YκY_{\kappa} of the lifted system. Their endpoint values are




Zκ=Eκ+∑jfj​(r)​Eκ−ej.Z_{\kappa}=E_{\kappa}+\sum_{j}f_{j}(r)E_{\kappa-e_{j}}.


They are independent, since their upper-grade coordinates form the
identity matrix. Their solution span ℱ\mathcal{F} therefore has
dimension θ\theta. Pairing any member of ℱ\mathcal{F} with PP
expresses its remainder as a fixed linear combination of the
RκR_{\kappa}, with coefficients coming from the dual transport. Hence
all these remainders have order at least K−wK-w for a fixed w≥0w\geq 0.


Theorem 4 of [20], applied to the verified
ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}-independence of the endpoint values, says
that every proper nonzero subspace RR defined over
ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}} satisfies




dimR>ωθ​dim(R∩span⁡{Zκ}).\dim R>\frac{\omega}{\theta}\dim\bigl(R\cap\operatorname{span}\{Z_{\kappa}\}\bigr).


Lemma 1 of that source says that the evaluation image at rr of
the solutions whose remainder vanishes identically is defined over
ℚ\mathbb{Q}. The strict inequality consequently proves the
nondegeneracy required by its Theorem 3.


Apply that theorem with polynomial degree bound M−1M-1, dimension
ω\omega, parameter η\eta, and the fixed loss ww. Because
σ>1\sigma>1, one has
⌊σ​M⌋≥σ⁡(M−1)\lfloor\sigma M\rfloor\geq\sigma(M-1), so its vanishing-order
hypothesis holds. Its derivative budget is at most
η⁡(M−1)+c1+(w+1)​ω\eta(M-1)+c_{1}+(w+1)\omega, up to the source’s indexing
convention. Absorb the fixed terms into CrC_{r}. Division by k!k!
does not alter rank. All the evaluated columns are rational, so
their rational and complex ranks agree.
∎



The field in this lemma is essential. Rational independence of a
collection of algebraic numbers does not meet the hypothesis. For
powers of one nonzero logarithm of an algebraic number,
Hermite–Lindemann gives transcendence, hence independence of its
distinct powers over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}. For the two logarithms
treated below we use Baker’s theorem. Neither argument proves
independence of arbitrary products of several logarithms.




15.2 The elementary passage from forms to a measure


Lemma 15.2 (Two independent forms).

Let x∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}. Suppose that for every sufficiently large
integer MM there are two integer pairs (AM,s,BM,s)(A_{M,s},B_{M,s}),
s=1,2s=1,2, with nonzero determinant, such that, for fixed a,c>0a,c>0,




|BM,s|≤ea​M+o⁡(M),|AM,s+BM,s​x|≤e−c​M+o⁡(M).|B_{M,s}|\leq e^{aM+o(M)},\qquad|A_{M,s}+B_{M,s}x|\leq e^{-cM+o(M)}.


Then xx is irrational and μ⁡(x)≤1+a/c\mu(x)\leq 1+a/c.



Proof.

For any integers p,qp,q with q≥1q\geq 1, the two integers
q​AM,s+p​BM,sqA_{M,s}+pB_{M,s} cannot both vanish. Fix a small
τ∈(0,c)\tau\in(0,c) and choose
M=⌈log⁡(2​q)/(c−τ)⌉M=\lceil\log(2q)/(c-\tau)\rceil, increasing a fixed lower
cutoff when necessary. For sufficiently large qq the small-form
bound and one of these nonzero integers give




1≤12+q​e(a+τ)​M​|x−p/q|.1\leq\frac{1}{2}+qe^{(a+\tau)M}|x-p/q|.


This proves the asserted exponent on letting τ↓0\tau\downarrow 0.
If xx were rational, use its fixed numerator and denominator and
let MM tend to infinity in the same nonzero-integer argument.
∎



The next formulation retains the height of a varying target. It will
be used both for polynomial values and for linear combinations of two
logarithms.


Proposition 15.3 (Uniform coefficient contraction).

Fix an endpoint vector ξ=(ξ1,…,ξm)∈ℝm\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Suppose that for each sufficiently large MM a family of integer
columns vM,k∈ℤΩv_{M,k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Omega} spans the whole coordinate
space. For c=(c1,…,cm)∈ℤm∖{0}c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\setminus\{0\},
put H=max⁡(1,|c1|,…,|cm|)H=\max(1,|c_{1}|,\ldots,|c_{m}|) and




AM,k​(c)=∑|κ|=Gcκ​(vM,k)κ,BM,k​(c)=∑|λ|=G−1cλ​(vM,k)λ.A_{M,k}(c)=\sum_{|\kappa|=G}c^{\kappa}(v_{M,k})_{\kappa},\qquad B_{M,k}(c)=\sum_{|\lambda|=G-1}c^{\lambda}(v_{M,k})_{\lambda}.


Assume fixed rates a,c∗>0a,c_{*}>0 give, uniformly in the coefficient
vector and the indicated jets,




|BM,k​(c)|≤C​HG−1​ea​M+o⁡(M),|AM,k​(c)+BM,k​(c)​c⋅ξ|≤C​HG​e−c∗​M+o⁡(M).|B_{M,k}(c)|\leq CH^{G-1}e^{aM+o(M)},\qquad|A_{M,k}(c)+B_{M,k}(c)\,c\cdot\xi|\leq CH^{G}e^{-c_{*}M+o(M)}.


Set μ∗=1+a/c∗\mu_{*}=1+a/c_{*} and ν∗=G​μ∗−1\nu_{*}=G\mu_{*}-1. For every
δ>0\delta>0 there is Cδ>0C_{\delta}>0, independent of c,p,Qc,p,Q, such
that




|c⋅ξ−pQ|≥Cδ​H−ν∗−δ​Q−μ∗−δ(p∈ℤ,Q≥1).\left|c\cdot\xi-\frac{p}{Q}\right|\geq C_{\delta}H^{-\nu_{*}-\delta}Q^{-\mu_{*}-\delta}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z},Q\geq 1).

(15.5)

Equivalently one may use any positive linked slack ζ\zeta and
the exponents ν∗+G​ζ\nu_{*}+G\zeta and μ∗+ζ\mu_{*}+\zeta.



Proof.

The functionals defining AA and BB are nonzero and have disjoint
supports. For example cj≠0c_{j}\neq 0 gives a nonzero upper coefficient
cjGc_{j}^{G} and lower coefficient cjG−1c_{j}^{G-1}; when G=1G=1 the latter
is the constant monomial one. Full rank therefore gives two
independent integer pairs (A,B)(A,B), for every c≠0c\neq 0, at every
MM above the same fixed cutoff.


Fix τ∈(0,c∗)\tau\in(0,c_{*}). Enlarge a constant C≥1C\geq 1 and a cutoff
M0M_{0} so that the two displayed estimates hold with rates
a+τa+\tau and c∗−τc_{*}-\tau for all M≥M0M\geq M_{0}. Choose




M=M0+⌈log⁡(2​C)+log⁡Q+G​log⁡Hc∗−τ⌉.M=M_{0}+\left\lceil\frac{\log(2C)+\log Q+G\log H}{c_{*}-\tau}\right\rceil.


One of Q​A+p​BQA+pB is a nonzero integer. For that pair,




1≤Q​|A+B​c⋅ξ|+Q​|B||c⋅ξ−p/Q|≤12+Q​|B|​|c⋅ξ−p/Q|.1\leq Q|A+B\,c\cdot\xi|+Q|B|\,|c\cdot\xi-p/Q|\leq\frac{1}{2}+Q|B|\,|c\cdot\xi-p/Q|.


In particular its BB cannot be zero. Substitution of the upper
bound for MM gives a constant independent of c,p,Qc,p,Q and
exponents




G−1+G⁡(a+τ)c∗−τ,1+a+τc∗−τ.G-1+\frac{G(a+\tau)}{c_{*}-\tau},\qquad 1+\frac{a+\tau}{c_{*}-\tau}.


Choose τ\tau sufficiently small for any prescribed linked slack;
then choose ζ≤δ/G\zeta\leq\delta/G to obtain
(15.5).
∎





16 Powers of a logarithm at a small rational point


Theorem 16.1.

For each fixed integer n≥1n\geq 1 and every ε>0\varepsilon>0, there
is b0​(n,ε)b_{0}(n,\varepsilon) such that every integer b≥b0b\geq b_{0} satisfies




μ⁡(logn⁡(1+1/b))≤2+ε,μ⁡(arctann⁡(1/b))≤2+ε.\mu\bigl(\log^{n}(1+1/b)\bigr)\leq 2+\varepsilon,\qquad\mu\bigl(\arctan^{n}(1/b)\bigr)\leq 2+\varepsilon.


The logarithm and arctangent have their real branches. This
recovers the small-argument phenomenon stated by Chudnovsky in
[7]; no historical novelty is claimed for that conclusion.



We give quantitative rates, so that the order of all choices is visible.
Let h⁡(z)=log⁡(1+z)h(z)=\log(1+z) or h⁡(z)=arctan⁡zh(z)=\arctan z, and apply the adjacent
grades to fj=hjf_{j}=h^{j}, 1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n. Fix G≥1G\geq 1, choose
0<η≤1/(ω+1)0<\eta\leq 1/(\omega+1), and put




σ=ω−ηθ,ℓ=G⁡(n−1)+1,Hs=∑j=1s1j.\sigma=\frac{\omega-\eta}{\theta},\qquad\ell=G(n-1)+1,\qquad H_{s}=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\frac{1}{j}.


We will choose σ>1+3​η\sigma>1+3\eta. Define




β=ω−ηη​σ​(Hn+log⁡2),Bb={b+1,h⁡(z)=log⁡(1+z),b2+1,h⁡(z)=arctan⁡z.\beta=\frac{\omega-\eta}{\eta}\sigma(H_{n}+\log 2),\qquad B_{b}=\begin{cases}b+1,&h(z)=\log(1+z),\\
b^{2}+1,&h(z)=\arctan z.\end{cases}


For b≥4b\geq 4, set




ab\displaystyle a_{b}
=β+log⁡b+η​log⁡Bb+η⁡(Hℓ+log⁡4),\displaystyle=\beta+\log b+\eta\log B_{b}+\eta(H_{\ell}+\log 4),

(16.1)


cb\displaystyle c_{b}
=(σ−1−η)​log⁡b−η​log⁡Bb−β−σ​log⁡3−η⁡(Hℓ+log⁡2).\displaystyle=(\sigma-1-\eta)\log b-\eta\log B_{b}-\beta-\sigma\log 3-\eta(H_{\ell}+\log 2).

(16.2)

We prove the more precise assertion




cb>0⟹μ⁡(h​(1/b)n)≤1+ab/cb.c_{b}>0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mu(h(1/b)^{n})\leq 1+a_{b}/c_{b}.

(16.3)



16.1 Integral contact polynomials


For s,v≥0s,v\geq 0 define




Ds​(v)=∏j=1slcm⁡(1,…,⌊v/j⌋),D_{s}(v)=\prod_{j=1}^{s}\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,\lfloor v/j\rfloor),


with an empty least common multiple equal to one. A useful elementary
observation is that Ds​(v)D_{s}(v) clears the coefficient of degree at most
vv in a product of at most ss logarithmic series whose coefficient
of degree jj has denominator dividing jj. Indeed, arrange the
positive indices of a product in decreasing order. Its jjth largest
index is at most ⌊v/j⌋\lfloor v/j\rfloor, so the product of its
denominators divides Ds​(v)D_{s}(v). The prime number theorem in the form
log⁡lcm⁡(1,…,v)=v+o⁡(v)\log\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,v)=v+o(v) gives




log⁡Ds​(v)=Hs​v+o⁡(v)(s​ fixed).\log D_{s}(v)=H_{s}v+o(v)\qquad(s\text{ fixed}).

(16.4)



For every sufficiently large MM, impose
(15.4) with K=⌊σ​M⌋K=\lfloor\sigma M\rfloor on
ω​M\omega M integer coefficients. There are at most
θ​K≤(ω−η)​M\theta K\leq(\omega-\eta)M equations. On |z|=1/2|z|=1/2 both
choices of hh have modulus less than one; their fixed powers
therefore have coefficients of degree at most KK bounded by 2K2^{K}.
Multiplication by Dn​(K)D_{n}(K) makes all equations integral. The elementary
box form of Siegel’s lemma gives a nonzero solution with




log⁡H⁡(P)≤β​M+o⁡(M).\log H(P)\leq\beta M+o(M).

(16.5)

More explicitly, a system with UU unknowns, at most E<UE<U equations,
and integer entries of modulus at most LL has a nonzero integer
kernel vector of logarithmic height at most
E⁡(log⁡L+O⁡(log⁡U))/(U−E)+O⁡(1)E(\log L+O(\log U))/(U-E)+O(1). This follows by mapping the
integer box {0,…,T}U\{0,\ldots,T\}^{U} into its bounded box of possible
equation values and choosing TT so that two vectors have the same
image. Here U=ω​MU=\omega M, E≤(ω−η)​ME\leq(\omega-\eta)M, and
log⁡L≤(Hn+log⁡2)​σ​M+o⁡(M)\log L\leq(H_{n}+\log 2)\sigma M+o(M).



16.2 Integral transport and divided derivatives


The lifted connection is 𝒜=h′​(z)​A0\mathcal{A}=h^{\prime}(z)A_{0} for an integral
nilpotent matrix A0A_{0}. Its transport has integral divided powers:




A0ℓ+1=0,A0j/j!∈Mω​(ℤ).A_{0}^{\ell+1}=0,\qquad A_{0}^{j}/j!\in M_{\omega}(\mathbb{Z}).

(16.6)

To verify this directly, use the increment u=h⁡(z)−h⁡(r)u=h(z)-h(r). The
signed dual system is transported by




aj​(u)=∑k=jn(−1)k−j​(kj)​ak​(0)​uk−j,c⁡(u)=c⁡(0)+∑k=1n(−1)k−1​ak​(0)​uk.a_{j}(u)=\sum_{k=j}^{n}(-1)^{k-j}\binom{k}{j}a_{k}(0)u^{k-j},\qquad c(u)=c(0)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k-1}a_{k}(0)u^{k}.


The induced transport on aκa^{\kappa} and c​aλca^{\lambda} is an
integer polynomial matrix in uu. Its degree is at most
G⁡(n−1)+1=ℓG(n-1)+1=\ell, and its value at u=0u=0 is the identity.
It is thus exactly exp⁡(A0​u)\exp(A_{0}u), proving
(16.6).


At r=1/br=1/b, set
vk=∇kP​(r)/k!v_{k}=\nabla^{k}P(r)/k! and ur​(t)=h⁡(r+t)−h⁡(r)u_{r}(t)=h(r+t)-h(r). The Taylor
identity corresponding to (15.3) is




vk=[tk]​exp⁡(A0𝖳​ur​(t))​P​(r+t).v_{k}=[t^{k}]\exp(A_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}u_{r}(t))P(r+t).


For every positive jj, the number
j​Bbj​[tj]​ur​(t)jB_{b}^{j}[t^{j}]u_{r}(t) is integral. In the logarithmic case this
follows from ur​(t)=log⁡(1+b​t/(b+1))u_{r}(t)=\log(1+bt/(b+1)). In the arctangent case
expand
ur′​(t)=b2/(b2+1+2​b​t+b2​t2)u_{r}^{\prime}(t)=b^{2}/(b^{2}+1+2bt+b^{2}t^{2}).
The product-clearing observation and the degree bound for PP now give




dk​vk∈ℤΩ,dk=bM​Bbk​Dℓ​(k).d_{k}v_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Omega},\qquad d_{k}=b^{M}B_{b}^{k}D_{\ell}(k).

(16.7)

On |t|≤1/4|t|\leq 1/4, the transport is bounded by a constant depending
only on n,Gn,G for all b≥4b\geq 4, and |P⁡(r+t)|≤2​H​(P)|P(r+t)|\leq 2H(P).
Cauchy’s inequality, followed by (16.4),
proves the coefficient bound eab​M+o⁡(M)e^{a_{b}M+o(M)} for all
k≤η​M+Ob​(1)k\leq\eta M+O_{b}(1).



16.3 Rank and the small remainder


The value h⁡(r)h(r) is nonzero and transcendental. In the logarithmic
case eh⁡(r)=1+re^{h(r)}=1+r; in the arctangent case
e2​i​h​(r)=(1+i​r)/(1−i​r)e^{2ih(r)}=(1+ir)/(1-ir). If h⁡(r)h(r) were algebraic, these
identities would contradict Hermite–Lindemann. Its first nn powers,
together with one, are therefore independent over
ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}. Lemma 15.1 applies and
gives full rank through k≤η​M+Cbk\leq\eta M+C_{b}.


To identify the relevant scalar remainder, put




aj​(z)=(nj)​(h⁡(r)−h⁡(z))n−j,ℛ∗​(z)=∑|κ|=Ga​(z)κ​Rκ​(z).a_{j}(z)=\binom{n}{j}(h(r)-h(z))^{n-j},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{*}(z)=\sum_{|\kappa|=G}a(z)^{\kappa}R_{\kappa}(z).


The corresponding lifted solution has endpoint vector
EG​en+h​(r)n​E(G−1)​enE_{Ge_{n}}+h(r)^{n}E_{(G-1)e_{n}}. Hence




ℛ∗(k)​(r)k!=(vk)G​en+h​(r)n​(vk)(G−1)​en.\frac{\mathcal{R}_{*}^{(k)}(r)}{k!}=(v_{k})_{Ge_{n}}+h(r)^{n}(v_{k})_{(G-1)e_{n}}.

(16.8)

All the auxiliary factors are uniformly bounded on |z|≤1/2|z|\leq 1/2.
Since ℛ∗\mathcal{R}_{*} has a zero of order at least KK, the maximum
principle applied after division by zKz^{K} gives




|ℛ∗​(z)|≤Cn,G​H​(P)​(2​|z|)K.|\mathcal{R}_{*}(z)|\leq C_{n,G}H(P)(2|z|)^{K}.


On the circle |z−r|=1/(2​b)|z-r|=1/(2b), Cauchy’s inequality therefore gives




|ℛ∗(k)​(r)k!|≤Cn,G​H​(P)​(3/b)K​(2​b)k.\left|\frac{\mathcal{R}_{*}^{(k)}(r)}{k!}\right|\leq C_{n,G}H(P)(3/b)^{K}(2b)^{k}.


Multiplication by dkd_{k} yields precisely the decay rate cbc_{b} in
(16.2). Full rank ensures that two jets have
independent pairs in the two corner coordinates. Apply
Lemma 15.2 to prove
(16.3).


For fixed n,G,ηn,G,\eta, the bound 1+ab/cb1+a_{b}/c_{b} tends as b→∞b\to\infty
to




1+1+ησ−1−2​ηor1+1+2​ησ−1−3​η,1+\frac{1+\eta}{\sigma-1-2\eta}\quad\text{or}\quad 1+\frac{1+2\eta}{\sigma-1-3\eta},


respectively for logarithms and arctangents. First choose GG large,
then η>0\eta>0 small, and finally bb large. Since
ω/θ→2\omega/\theta\to 2 as G→∞G\to\infty, this proves
Theorem 16.1. In every use of the
auxiliary-degree limit, n,G,η,bn,G,\eta,b have already been fixed.


An explicit, coarse threshold is available. Put




A=β+η⁡(Hℓ+log⁡8),C=β+σ​log⁡3+η⁡(Hℓ+log⁡4),A=\beta+\eta(H_{\ell}+\log 8),\qquad C=\beta+\sigma\log 3+\eta(H_{\ell}+\log 4),






Δ=(1+ε)​(σ−1−3​η)−(1+2​η).\Delta=(1+\varepsilon)(\sigma-1-3\eta)-(1+2\eta).


When Δ>0\Delta>0, it suffices to take




b≥max⁡{4,exp⁡A+(1+ε)​CΔ}.b\geq\max\left\{4,\exp\frac{A+(1+\varepsilon)C}{\Delta}\right\}.

(16.9)

Indeed log⁡Bb≤2​log⁡b+log⁡2\log B_{b}\leq 2\log b+\log 2 gives
ab≤(1+2​η)​log⁡b+Aa_{b}\leq(1+2\eta)\log b+A and
cb≥(σ−1−3​η)​log⁡b−Cc_{b}\geq(\sigma-1-3\eta)\log b-C; the threshold makes the
first expression at most (1+ε)(1+\varepsilon) times the second,
which is consequently positive. For example, one may choose




G≥max⁡{1,⌈4​(1+ε)​(n−1)ε⌉},η=min⁡{12​(ω+1),ε4​((1+ε)​(3+1/θ)+2)}.G\geq\max\left\{1,\left\lceil\frac{4(1+\varepsilon)(n-1)}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil\right\},\qquad\eta=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2(\omega+1)},\frac{\varepsilon}{4((1+\varepsilon)(3+1/\theta)+2)}\right\}.


These choices give Δ≥ε/2\Delta\geq\varepsilon/2.




17 Quadratic logarithms and polynomial targets


For a nonzero integer dd, define the real germ




hd​(z)=∑j≥0dj​z2​j+12​j+1,hd′​(z)=11−d​z2.h_{d}(z)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{d^{j}z^{2j+1}}{2j+1},\qquad h_{d}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{1-dz^{2}}.


For d>0d>0 this is
log⁡((1+d​z)/(1−d​z))/(2​d)\log((1+\sqrt{d}\,z)/(1-\sqrt{d}\,z))/(2\sqrt{d}); for d<0d<0
it is arctan⁡(|d|​z)/|d|\arctan(\sqrt{|d|}z)/\sqrt{|d|}. We only evaluate inside
the disk of convergence. Introduce the single fixed function




F⁡(t)=∑j≥0tj2​j+1=arctanh⁡tt,2​t​F′​(t)+F⁡(t)=11−t.F(t)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{t^{j}}{2j+1}=\frac{\operatorname{arctanh}\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{t}},\qquad 2tF^{\prime}(t)+F(t)=\frac{1}{1-t}.

(17.1)

The expression is independent of the square-root choice. It satisfies
b​hd​(1/b)=F⁡(d/b2)bh_{d}(1/b)=F(d/b^{2}). Keeping this one differential system fixed
removes the parameter dd from the contact-height estimate.


Theorem 17.1 (Uniform polynomial targets).

Fix n≥1n\geq 1, ε>0\varepsilon>0, and A>1+1/εA>1+1/\varepsilon. There
is an explicitly specified constant C=C⁡(n,ε,A)C=C(n,\varepsilon,A) such
that, for every nonzero integer dd and every integer
b≥C​|d|Ab\geq C|d|^{A}, every nonconstant rational polynomial PP of degree
at most nn satisfies




μ⁡(P⁡(hd​(1/b)))≤2+ε.\mu\bigl(P(h_{d}(1/b))\bigr)\leq 2+\varepsilon.


The threshold does not depend on PP. Constants in a lower-bound
inequality at a fixed endpoint may depend on that endpoint and on PP.



17.1 Contact height for the fixed function


Use fj=Fjf_{j}=F^{j}, 1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n, and the same adjacent grades.
Their differential system is




(Fj)′=j2​t​(1−t)​Fj−1−j2​t​Fj.(F^{j})^{\prime}=\frac{j}{2t(1-t)}F^{j-1}-\frac{j}{2t}F^{j}.


The origin is regular singular for this system, although the distinguished
functions FjF^{j} themselves are holomorphic there. The coefficients
through degree KK of these powers are cleared by Dn​(2​K+n)D_{n}(2K+n):
in a product of j≤nj\leq n coefficients the sum of the odd denominator
indices is at most 2​K+n2K+n, and the same ordering argument applies.
Also |F⁡(t)|≤2|F(t)|\leq 2 on |t|=1/2|t|=1/2. The contact construction therefore
gives integer columns of degree less than MM with




log⁡H⁡(PM)≤β​M+o⁡(M),β=ω−ηη​σ​(2​Hn+log⁡2),K=⌊σ​M⌋.\log H(P_{M})\leq\beta M+o(M),\qquad\beta=\frac{\omega-\eta}{\eta}\sigma(2H_{n}+\log 2),\qquad K=\lfloor\sigma M\rfloor.

(17.2)

This height bound is independent of the eventual rational endpoint.



17.2 Explicit transport and the degree saving in denominators


Fix integers a≠0a\neq 0, q>0q>0, with |a|≤q/4|a|\leq q/4, and put r=a/qr=a/q.
Near t=rt=r, define




U⁡(t)=t/r,V⁡(t)=arctanh⁡t−arctanh⁡rr,U(t)=\sqrt{t/r},\qquad V(t)=\frac{\operatorname{arctanh}\sqrt{t}-\operatorname{arctanh}\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{r}},


with the local branches chosen so that U⁡(r)=1U(r)=1, V⁡(r)=0V(r)=0.
These local quantities satisfy




U′=U2​t,V′=U2​t​(1−t),U​F​(t)−V=F⁡(r).U^{\prime}=\frac{U}{2t},\qquad V^{\prime}=\frac{U}{2t(1-t)},\qquad UF(t)-V=F(r).


The signed dual transport of the augmented system is therefore




aj​(t)\displaystyle a_{j}(t)
=U​(t)j​∑k=jn(kj)​(−V⁡(t))k−j​ak​(r),\displaystyle=U(t)^{j}\sum_{k=j}^{n}\binom{k}{j}(-V(t))^{k-j}a_{k}(r),

(17.3)


c⁡(t)\displaystyle c(t)
=c⁡(r)+∑k=1n(−1)k−1​ak​(r)​V​(t)k.\displaystyle=c(r)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k-1}a_{k}(r)V(t)^{k}.


Differentiation verifies these formulas directly. The induced lifted
transport is an integer polynomial matrix in U,VU,V. Its degree in
VV is at most ℓ=G⁡(n−1)+1\ell=G(n-1)+1.


Lemma 17.2 (An explicit clearing integer).

For k<Mk<M, the divided covariant jet vk=∇kPM​(r)/k!v_{k}=\nabla^{k}P_{M}(r)/k!
is cleared by the positive integer




dk=qM−1−k​(4​|a⁡(q−a)|)k​Dℓ​(k).d_{k}=q^{M-1-k}\bigl(4|a(q-a)|\bigr)^{k}D_{\ell}(k).

(17.4)




Proof.

Write t=r+st=r+s and put B=4​a​(q−a)B=4a(q-a), which may have either sign.
For every nonnegative integer mm, the binomial series gives




Bjqj​[sj]​U​(r+s)m∈ℤ.\frac{B^{j}}{q^{j}}[s^{j}]U(r+s)^{m}\in\mathbb{Z}.


Indeed [sj]​Um=(m/2j)​(q/a)j[s^{j}]U^{m}=\binom{m/2}{j}(q/a)^{j} and
4j​(m/2j)4^{j}\binom{m/2}{j} is integral. Next, for j≥1j\geq 1,




j⁡[sj]​V​(r+s)=qj+12​a​∑u=0j−1(−1/2u)​a−u​(q−a)−(j−u).j[s^{j}]V(r+s)=\frac{q^{j+1}}{2a}\sum_{u=0}^{j-1}\binom{-1/2}{u}a^{-u}(q-a)^{-(j-u)}.


Multiplication by Bj/qjB^{j}/q^{j} makes this integral: each summand
becomes
q2​ 4j​(−1/2u)​aj−u−1​(q−a)u\frac{q}{2}\,4^{j}\binom{-1/2}{u}a^{j-u-1}(q-a)^{u},
and u≤j−1u\leq j-1 leaves enough factors of two.
Thus, for a monomial in U,VU,V of VV-degree at most ℓ\ell, its
coefficient of sjs^{j} is cleared by
(Bj/qj)​Dℓ​(j)(B^{j}/q^{j})D_{\ell}(j). The ordering-of-indices argument supplies
the factors DℓD_{\ell}, and convolution with the UU coefficients
does not introduce additional denominators.


The coefficient of sk−js^{k-j} in PM​(r+s)P_{M}(r+s) has denominator dividing
qM−1−k+jq^{M-1-k+j}. The factor qjq^{j} in the numerator of the transport
coefficient cancels the extra qjq^{j} in that denominator. Every term
of total order kk is consequently cleared by
qM−1−k​|B|k​Dℓ​(k)q^{M-1-k}|B|^{k}D_{\ell}(k), as claimed.
∎



The saving q−kq^{-k} in this formula is important. It comes from the
degree lost on differentiating the polynomial; bounding all evaluated
polynomial derivatives by the same denominator would discard it.



17.3 Rank and the exact rates


The value F⁡(r)F(r) is nonzero and transcendental. Otherwise




exp⁡(2​r​F​(r))=1+r1−r\exp(2\sqrt{r}\,F(r))=\frac{1+\sqrt{r}}{1-\sqrt{r}}


would contradict Hermite–Lindemann. Its distinct powers are therefore
independent over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}. The explicit functions
U,VU,V near zero are finite combinations of holomorphic functions
and powers of t\sqrt{t}; the transported remainder coefficients
are Nilsson functions of fixed orders. Lemma 15.1
gives full rank through k≤η​M+Or​(1)k\leq\eta M+O_{r}(1). For all sufficiently
large MM these indices satisfy k<Mk<M, as required above.


On the disk |t−r|≤|r|/2|t-r|\leq|r|/2, transport is bounded by a constant
depending on the fixed endpoint, nn, and GG. Polynomial values are
bounded by a constant times H⁡(PM)H(P_{M}) because |t|≤3/8|t|\leq 3/8.
Cauchy’s inequality contributes (2​q/|a|)k(2q/|a|)^{k} to a divided jet.
After multiplication by (17.4), the cancellation is




qM−1−k​(4​|a⁡(q−a)|)k​(2​q/|a|)k=qM−1​(8​|q−a|)k≤qM−1​(10​q)k.q^{M-1-k}\bigl(4|a(q-a)|\bigr)^{k}(2q/|a|)^{k}=q^{M-1}\bigl(8|q-a|\bigr)^{k}\leq q^{M-1}(10q)^{k}.

(17.5)

It follows that the cleared coefficients have rate




α=(1+η)​log⁡q+β+η⁡(log⁡10+Hℓ).\alpha=(1+\eta)\log q+\beta+\eta(\log 10+H_{\ell}).

(17.6)



Each original contact remainder is bounded on |t|=1/2|t|=1/2 by
C​H​(PM)CH(P_{M}) and vanishes to order KK. It is thus bounded on the
local disk by C​H​(PM)​(3​|a|/q)KCH(P_{M})(3|a|/q)^{K}. Multiplication by the fixed
transport, followed by Cauchy’s inequality and
(17.5), gives decay rate




γ=(σ−1−η)​log⁡q−σ​log|a|−β−σ​log⁡3−η⁡(log⁡10+Hℓ).\gamma=(\sigma-1-\eta)\log q-\sigma\log|a|-\beta-\sigma\log 3-\eta(\log 10+H_{\ell}).

(17.7)

The multiplicative constants can depend on rr, but they contribute
only o⁡(M)o(M) after taking logarithms. No bound uniform in a changing
endpoint is hidden in this auxiliary-degree limit.



17.4 The joint polynomial measure


For a nonconstant polynomial
P⁡(X)=c0+∑j=1ncj​Xj∈ℤ⁡[X]P(X)=c_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}X^{j}\in\mathbb{Z}[X], write
H+​(P)=max⁡(1,|c1|,…,|cn|)H_{+}(P)=\max(1,|c_{1}|,\ldots,|c_{n}|); the constant coefficient
is omitted deliberately.


Theorem 17.3 (Joint height and denominator bound).

Fix n,G,η,a,qn,G,\eta,a,q as above and suppose γ>0\gamma>0. Put




x=F⁡(a/q),μ∗=1+α/γ,ν∗=G​μ∗−1.x=F(a/q),\qquad\mu_{*}=1+\alpha/\gamma,\qquad\nu_{*}=G\mu_{*}-1.


For every δ>0\delta>0 there is Cδ>0C_{\delta}>0 such that




|P⁡(x)−pQ|≥Cδ​H+​(P)−ν∗−δ​Q−μ∗−δ\left|P(x)-\frac{p}{Q}\right|\geq C_{\delta}H_{+}(P)^{-\nu_{*}-\delta}Q^{-\mu_{*}-\delta}

(17.8)

for all nonconstant integer polynomials PP of degree at most nn,
all p∈ℤp\in\mathbb{Z}, and all Q≥1Q\geq 1. The constant depends on
the fixed point and displayed parameters, but not on P,p,QP,p,Q.
The linked-slack exponents ν∗+G​ζ\nu_{*}+G\zeta, μ∗+ζ\mu_{*}+\zeta are
also valid for every ζ>0\zeta>0.



Proof.

Choose the integral contact column for every sufficiently large MM
once and for all, independently of the target coefficients. Clear
its jets as above. For c=(c1,…,cn)c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}), the solution of the
dual system with endpoint ac​(r)=ca_{c}(r)=c depends linearly on cc.
The lifted solution with lower coordinate factor
∑j(ac)j​Fj\sum_{j}(a_{c})_{j}F^{j} has endpoint determined by
c⋅(F⁡(r),…,F​(r)n)c\cdot(F(r),\ldots,F(r)^{n}). Its paired remainder is exactly




ℛc​(t)=∑|κ|=Gac​(t)κ​RM,κ​(t).\mathcal{R}_{c}(t)=\sum_{|\kappa|=G}a_{c}(t)^{\kappa}R_{M,\kappa}(t).


It is homogeneous of degree GG in cc. On the fixed local disk,
‖ac​(t)‖≤Cr​H+​(P)\|a_{c}(t)\|\leq C_{r}H_{+}(P), with the same CrC_{r} for every
coefficient vector. The preceding analytic estimates consequently
give the hypotheses of Proposition 15.3 with
rates α,γ\alpha,\gamma. Its small o⁡(M)o(M) terms and its rank cutoff
are independent of cc. Apply the proposition and absorb c0c_{0} by
replacing pp with p−Q​c0p-Qc_{0}. This replacement leaves QQ and
H+​(P)H_{+}(P) unchanged.
∎



When x=hd​(1/b)=F⁡(d/b2)/bx=h_{d}(1/b)=F(d/b^{2})/b with fixed d,bd,b, the same exponents
hold. Indeed P^​(X)=bn​P​(X/b)\widehat{P}(X)=b^{n}P(X/b) is integral and
H+​(P^)≤bn​H+​(P)H_{+}(\widehat{P})\leq b^{n}H_{+}(P); apply
(17.8) to P^\widehat{P} and numerator bn​pb^{n}p.
All factors involving this fixed bb enter the lower-bound constant.


To prove Theorem 17.1, put




B=β+η⁡(log⁡10+Hℓ),C1=B+σ​log⁡3,τ=(1+ε)​(σ−1−η)−(1+η).B=\beta+\eta(\log 10+H_{\ell}),\quad C_{1}=B+\sigma\log 3,\quad\tau=(1+\varepsilon)(\sigma-1-\eta)-(1+\eta).


First choose GG large and then η>0\eta>0 small so that τ>0\tau>0
and




(1+ε)​σ2​τ<A.\frac{(1+\varepsilon)\sigma}{2\tau}<A.


These choices are possible because the fraction tends to
1+1/ε1+1/\varepsilon. One entirely explicit recipe is to put
u=A​ε−(1+ε)>0u=A\varepsilon-(1+\varepsilon)>0, choose




G≥max⁡{1,⌈2​(1+ε)​(2​A−1)​(n−1)u⌉},G\geq\max\left\{1,\left\lceil\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)(2A-1)(n-1)}{u}\right\rceil\right\},


and then set




L∗=2​A​(2+ε)+(1+ε)​(2​A−1)θ,η=min⁡{12​(ω+1),u2​L∗}.L_{*}=2A(2+\varepsilon)+\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(2A-1)}{\theta},\qquad\eta=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2(\omega+1)},\frac{u}{2L_{*}}\right\}.


Writing δ=(n−1)/(G+n−1)\delta=(n-1)/(G+n-1), direct expansion gives




2​A​τ−(1+ε)​σ=2​u−(1+ε)​(2​A−1)​δ−η​L∗≥u>0,2A\tau-(1+\varepsilon)\sigma=2u-(1+\varepsilon)(2A-1)\delta-\eta L_{*}\geq u>0,


so the required margin, including τ>0\tau>0, holds. Now take




C=max⁡{2,exp⁡B+(1+ε)​C12​τ}.C=\max\left\{2,\exp\frac{B+(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}}{2\tau}\right\}.

(17.9)

For a=da=d, q=b2q=b^{2}, the hypothesis b≥C​|d|Ab\geq C|d|^{A} gives
|a|≤q/4|a|\leq q/4 and




τ​log⁡q≥B+(1+ε)​C1+(1+ε)​σ​log⁡|a|.\tau\log q\geq B+(1+\varepsilon)C_{1}+(1+\varepsilon)\sigma\log|a|.


Thus γ>0\gamma>0 and α≤(1+ε)​γ\alpha\leq(1+\varepsilon)\gamma.
The joint theorem, with the target fixed and slack tending to zero,
gives μ⁡(P⁡(hd​(1/b)))≤2+ε\mu(P(h_{d}(1/b)))\leq 2+\varepsilon for integral PP.
Clearing a fixed rational polynomial’s coefficients and using rational
scaling proves the stated rational-polynomial version.


Corollary 17.4 (A concrete tradeoff).

Fix a nonzero integer dd and integers n,G≥1n,G\geq 1. Given ρ>0\rho>0,
for all sufficiently large bb there is c>0c>0 such that




|P⁡(hd​(1/b))−pQ|≥c​H+​(P)−(2​G+n−2+G​ρ)​Q−(2+(n−1)/G+ρ)\left|P(h_{d}(1/b))-\frac{p}{Q}\right|\geq cH_{+}(P)^{-(2G+n-2+G\rho)}Q^{-(2+(n-1)/G+\rho)}


simultaneously for all nonconstant P∈ℤ⁡[X]P\in\mathbb{Z}[X] of degree
at most nn, all integers pp, and all positive integers QQ.



Proof.

For fixed d,n,Gd,n,G, first let bb tend to infinity in the rate
quotient and then let η\eta decrease to zero. The limits are




μ∗⟶2+n−1G,ν∗⟶2​G+n−2.\mu_{*}\longrightarrow 2+\frac{n-1}{G},\qquad\nu_{*}\longrightarrow 2G+n-2.


Choose η\eta first, then bb sufficiently large, and finally the
linked slack in Theorem 17.3 so that the two displayed
exponents fit below those of the corollary.
∎



There are two useful readings of this tradeoff. At G=1G=1 it gives
the classical small-point algebraicity measure: for every
ε>0\varepsilon>0, sufficiently small fixed points satisfy
wn​(x)≤n+εw_{n}(x)\leq n+\varepsilon, where wnw_{n} is the exponent for
0<|P⁡(x)|<H​(P)−w0<|P(x)|<H(P)^{-w} with integer polynomials of degree at most
nn. This height-only phenomenon belongs to the earlier
Chudnovsky–Chudnovsky theory; see [14]. Larger GG
improves the exponent of QQ toward two while increasing the cost in
the target’s coefficient height. We do not claim historical priority
for this particular quantitative tradeoff.


The joint theorem also records coefficient content. If
R⁡(X)=r0+∑j=1nrj​XjR(X)=r_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}r_{j}X^{j} is nonconstant and
g=gcd⁡(|r1|,…,|rn|)>0g=\gcd(|r_{1}|,\ldots,|r_{n}|)>0, ignoring zero entries, then




|R⁡(x)|≥Cδ​H+​(R)−ν∗−δ​g(G−1)​μ∗.|R(x)|\geq C_{\delta}H_{+}(R)^{-\nu_{*}-\delta}g^{(G-1)\mu_{*}}.

(17.10)

Apply (17.8) to
∑j≥1(rj/g)​Xj\sum_{j\geq 1}(r_{j}/g)X^{j}, with p=−r0p=-r_{0}, Q=gQ=g, and multiply
by gg. The two equal slack exponents cancel in the power of gg.


As one explicit example, take n=3n=3, G=9G=9, η=1/101\eta=1/101.
Then θ=55\theta=55, ω=100\omega=100, ℓ=19\ell=19, and
σ=10099/5555\sigma=10099/5555. If




b≥e200000​|d|3,b\geq e^{200000}|d|^{3},


the rates satisfy μ∗≤3\mu_{*}\leq 3 and ν∗≤26\nu_{*}\leq 26. For a direct
check, put T=2​(σ−1−η)−(1+η)T=2(\sigma-1-\eta)-(1+\eta). One has
T>0.606T>0.606, 6​T>2​σ6T>2\sigma, B<80047B<80047, and C1<80050C_{1}<80050;
thus 400000​T>B+2​C1400000T>B+2C_{1}. These inequalities imply
T​log⁡(b2)≥B+2​C1+2​σ​log⁡|d|T\log(b^{2})\geq B+2C_{1}+2\sigma\log|d| and hence
α≤2​γ\alpha\leq 2\gamma. Therefore, for every δ>0\delta>0,




|P⁡(hd​(1/b))−pQ|≥cd,b,δ​H+​(P)−26−δ​Q−3−δ\left|P(h_{d}(1/b))-\frac{p}{Q}\right|\geq c_{d,b,\delta}H_{+}(P)^{-26-\delta}Q^{-3-\delta}


uniformly over all nonconstant integer polynomials of degree at most
three. The threshold is explicit; a numerical value of the final
positive constant is not asserted.




18 A joint measure for two small rational logarithms


For integers N≥8N\geq 8, write
ξN=log⁡(1+1/N)\xi_{N}=\log(1+1/N) and ζN=log⁡(1+2/N)\zeta_{N}=\log(1+2/N).


Theorem 18.1.

For every integer G≥1G\geq 1 and every ρ>0\rho>0, there is an
explicit integer N0​(G,ρ)N_{0}(G,\rho) such that, for every N≥N0N\geq N_{0},
there exists c=c⁡(N,G,ρ)>0c=c(N,G,\rho)>0 satisfying




|u​ξN+v​ζN−pQ|≥c​H−2​G−G​ρ​Q−2−1/G−ρ,H=max⁡(1,|u|,|v|),\left|u\xi_{N}+v\zeta_{N}-\frac{p}{Q}\right|\geq cH^{-2G-G\rho}Q^{-2-1/G-\rho},\qquad H=\max(1,|u|,|v|),


for all (u,v)∈ℤ2∖{(0,0)}(u,v)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\},
p∈ℤp\in\mathbb{Z}, and Q≥1Q\geq 1. In particular




μ⁡(log⁡(1+1/N)log⁡(1+2/N))≤3+ρ(N≥N0​(1,ρ)).\mu\left(\frac{\log(1+1/N)}{\log(1+2/N)}\right)\leq 3+\rho\qquad(N\geq N_{0}(1,\rho)).

(18.1)




Proof.

The positive rational numbers (N+1)/N(N+1)/N and (N+2)/N(N+2)/N are
multiplicatively independent. Any prime divisor of N+1N+1 divides
neither NN nor N+2N+2; its valuation first forces the exponent of
(N+1)/N(N+1)/N in a multiplicative relation to vanish, and then the other
exponent vanishes. Baker’s theorem [5] implies independence
of their logarithms over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}. Its transcendence
statement for nonzero algebraic linear forms in logarithms also
excludes an algebraic nonzero constant. Consequently
1,ξN,ζN1,\xi_{N},\zeta_{N} are independent over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}.


Use f1​(z)=log⁡(1+z)f_{1}(z)=\log(1+z), f2​(z)=log⁡(1+2​z)f_{2}(z)=\log(1+2z) and the two
adjacent grades. Here θ=G+1\theta=G+1, ω=2​G+1\omega=2G+1. Choose
0<η≤1/(ω+1)0<\eta\leq 1/(\omega+1) and set




σ=ω−ηθ,β=ω−ηη​σ​(1+log⁡4).\sigma=\frac{\omega-\eta}{\theta},\qquad\beta=\frac{\omega-\eta}{\eta}\sigma(1+\log 4).


The fixed contact equations are




Rκ=Pκ+Pκ−e1​f1+Pκ−e2​f2=O⁡(zK),K=⌊σ​M⌋.R_{\kappa}=P_{\kappa}+P_{\kappa-e_{1}}f_{1}+P_{\kappa-e_{2}}f_{2}=O(z^{K}),\qquad K=\lfloor\sigma M\rfloor.


Their coefficients are cleared by D1​(K)D_{1}(K); both logarithms have
modulus less than one on |z|=1/4|z|=1/4, so their coefficients are bounded
by 4K4^{K}. The same Siegel argument gives integer contact columns
with log⁡H⁡(PM)≤β​M+o⁡(M)\log H(P_{M})\leq\beta M+o(M), independently of NN.


In this case the dual variables a1,a2a_{1},a_{2} are constant. The lifted
connection has the especially simple form
𝒜=f1′​N1+f2′​N2\mathcal{A}=f^{\prime}_{1}N_{1}+f^{\prime}_{2}N_{2}, with integral matrices satisfying
Ni​Nj=0N_{i}N_{j}=0 for every i,ji,j. Its normalized transport is




I+N1​(f1​(z)−f1​(r))+N2​(f2​(z)−f2​(r)),r=1/N.I+N_{1}(f_{1}(z)-f_{1}(r))+N_{2}(f_{2}(z)-f_{2}(r)),\qquad r=1/N.


Thus only one logarithmic factor appears, independently of GG.
The origin and rr are ordinary points, and
Lemma 15.1 gives full jet rank through
k≤η​M+ON​(1)k\leq\eta M+O_{N}(1).


Set LN=(N+1)​(N+2)L_{N}=(N+1)(N+2). For k<Mk<M an exact clearing integer is




dk=NM−1−k​LNk​D1​(k).d_{k}=N^{M-1-k}L_{N}^{k}D_{1}(k).

(18.2)

Indeed




[tj]​(fi​(r+t)−fi​(r))=(−1)j−1j​(i​NN+i)j.[t^{j}](f_{i}(r+t)-f_{i}(r))=\frac{(-1)^{j-1}}{j}\left(\frac{iN}{N+i}\right)^{j}.


The divided polynomial derivative of order k−jk-j has denominator
dividing NM−1−k+jN^{M-1-k+j}; the numerator NjN^{j} cancels that extra
factor. The remaining logarithmic denominators are cleared by
LNk​D1​(k)L_{N}^{k}D_{1}(k). This proves (18.2) without
any grade-dependent power of the least common multiple.


On |t|=1/8|t|=1/8 around rr, the transport is bounded uniformly for
N≥8N\geq 8. Cauchy’s inequality gives coefficient rate




αN=β+log⁡N+η⁡{log⁡(LN/N)+log⁡8+1}.\alpha_{N}=\beta+\log N+\eta\{\log(L_{N}/N)+\log 8+1\}.


For (u,v)≠(0,0)(u,v)\neq(0,0), contract against the monomials in
c=(u,v)c=(u,v). The scalar remainder
∑|κ|=Gcκ​Rκ\sum_{|\kappa|=G}c^{\kappa}R_{\kappa} has order at least KK
and is bounded on |z−r|=1/(2​N)|z-r|=1/(2N) by
CG​HG​H​(PM)​(6/N)KC_{G}H^{G}H(P_{M})(6/N)^{K}. The derivative factor is (2​N)k(2N)^{k}.
After clearing, the decay rate is




γN=(σ−1)​log⁡N−β−σ​log⁡6−η⁡{log⁡(2​LN)+1}.\gamma_{N}=(\sigma-1)\log N-\beta-\sigma\log 6-\eta\{\log(2L_{N})+1\}.


Both estimates are uniform in u,v,p,Qu,v,p,Q. For γN>0\gamma_{N}>0,
Proposition 15.3 therefore gives linked-slack
exponents




G−1+G​αN/γN+G​δ,1+αN/γN+δ.G-1+G\alpha_{N}/\gamma_{N}+G\delta,\qquad 1+\alpha_{N}/\gamma_{N}+\delta.




For completeness, the following recipe specifies a sufficient
threshold. Put h=ρ/2h=\rho/2, t=1+1/G+ht=1+1/G+h, and choose




η=min⁡{12​(ω+1),h​G2​(G+1)​{t⁡(2+1/(G+1))+1}}.\eta=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2(\omega+1)},\frac{hG}{2(G+1)\{t(2+1/(G+1))+1\}}\right\}.


Define σ,β\sigma,\beta as above and




a0=β+η⁡(1+log⁡16),c0=β+σ​log⁡6+η⁡(1+log⁡4),a_{0}=\beta+\eta(1+\log 16),\qquad c_{0}=\beta+\sigma\log 6+\eta(1+\log 4),






Δ=t⁡(σ−1−2​η)−(1+η)≥h​G2​(G+1)>0.\Delta=t(\sigma-1-2\eta)-(1+\eta)\geq\frac{hG}{2(G+1)}>0.


Since LN≤2​N2L_{N}\leq 2N^{2} for N≥8N\geq 8, take




N0=⌈max⁡{8,exp⁡a0+t​c0Δ}⌉.N_{0}=\left\lceil\max\left\{8,\exp\frac{a_{0}+tc_{0}}{\Delta}\right\}\right\rceil.


Then γN>0\gamma_{N}>0 and αN/γN≤t\alpha_{N}/\gamma_{N}\leq t for all
N≥N0N\geq N_{0}. Taking the linked slack δ≤ρ/2\delta\leq\rho/2 proves
the first assertion.


For the ratio use G=1G=1, Q=1Q=1, p=0p=0, and (u,v)=(b,−a)(u,v)=(b,-a).
If a/ba/b stays in a fixed bounded neighborhood of
ξN/ζN\xi_{N}/\zeta_{N}, its height max⁡(b,|a|)\max(b,|a|) is at most a fixed
multiple of bb. Divide the resulting lower bound for
|b​ξN−a​ζN||b\xi_{N}-a\zeta_{N}| by b​|ζN|b|\zeta_{N}|. Approximants outside
that neighborhood have a constant lower bound, so
(18.1) follows. Multiplicative independence
already proves that the ratio is irrational.
∎



Increasing GG improves the exponent of the rational approximant’s
denominator QQ, but it worsens the coefficient-height exponent.
For a quotient one sets Q=1Q=1 and lets the two integer coefficients
vary, so G=1G=1 is the favorable choice in this construction. It gives
the near-three bound above, not a near-two quotient theorem.


Corollary 18.2 (The lower bound and the range up to three).

Under the hypotheses and endpoint thresholds of
Theorems 16.1 and
17.1, every indicated target yy satisfies




2≤μ⁡(y)≤2+ε.2\leq\mu(y)\leq 2+\varepsilon.


In particular 0<ε≤10<\varepsilon\leq 1 gives 2≤μ⁡(y)≤32\leq\mu(y)\leq 3.



Proof.

A nonzero logarithm of an algebraic number is transcendental by
Hermite–Lindemann. A nonzero real arctangent of a nonzero algebraic
number is also transcendental: its exponential after multiplication by
2​i2\mathrm{i} is the algebraic Cayley quotient, so algebraicity would contradict
the same theorem. The displayed logarithmic and circular expressions for
hd​(1/b)h_{d}(1/b) therefore show that this value is transcendental; multiplying
by a nonzero algebraic normalization does not change that assertion.
For a transcendental zz, every nonconstant P∈ℚ⁡[X]P\in\mathbb{Q}[X] has
transcendental value P⁡(z)P(z). Otherwise zz would satisfy the nonzero
polynomial P⁡(X)−P⁡(z)P(X)-P(z) over ℚ¯\overline{\mathbb{Q}}, and hence be algebraic.
This also covers the positive integer powers in
Theorem 16.1. Dirichlet’s theorem now supplies
the lower bound two, and the cited upper bounds give the conclusion.
∎



These graded applications have checked Lean endpoints relative to the explicit classical inputs recorded in Appendix A; the literal arithmetic recipes are included. The required threshold is chosen after
ε\varepsilon (and the degree and growth parameters), so this corollary
does not infer exponent two by taking ε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0 at a
fixed arbitrary argument.



19 Restricted powers and the meaning of the conclusions


Proposition 19.1 (Fixed power classes).

Let x≠0x\neq 0 be real, let c>0c>0, and let n≥1n\geq 1 be an integer.
Suppose cn∈ℚc^{n}\in\mathbb{Q} and μ⁡(x/c)=2\mu(x/c)=2. The approximation
exponent of xnx^{n} restricted to rational numbers in
cn​ℚnc^{n}\mathbb{Q}^{n} is exactly 2/n2/n, when the height is the reduced
denominator of the approximating rational number.



Proof.

Write cn=u/vc^{n}=u/v in lowest terms with u,v>0u,v>0, and write a member
of the class as (u/v)​(A/B)n(u/v)(A/B)^{n}, with (A,B)=1(A,B)=1, B>0B>0.
Its reduced denominator is




q=v​Bngcd⁡(u​An,v​Bn)≍u,vBn,q=\frac{vB^{n}}{\gcd(uA^{n},vB^{n})}\asymp_{u,v}B^{n},


because the greatest common divisor in this expression divides
u​vuv. For approximants on the branch near x/cx/c, the power map
has nonzero derivative and is locally bi-Lipschitz. If nn is even,
the other branch is handled by the same argument at −x/c-x/c.
Thus an approximation to xnx^{n} of exponent greater than 2/n2/n
would give an approximation to x/cx/c of exponent greater than two.
Conversely, its continued-fraction convergents give infinitely many
errors O⁡(B−2)O(B^{-2}), hence O(q−2/n)O(q^{-2/n}) after taking powers.
Absorb fixed constants by an arbitrarily small exponent slack.
∎



For instance an exponent-two theorem for log⁡α\log\alpha, with
α>0\alpha>0 rational and α≠1\alpha\neq 1, gives restricted exponent
2/n2/n for (log⁡α)n(\log\alpha)^{n} along ℚn\mathbb{Q}^{n}. The normalized
quadratic exponent-two theorem gives restricted exponent one for
(log⁡α)2(\log\alpha)^{2} along the fixed class d​ℚ2d\mathbb{Q}^{2} when dd is
a positive nonsquare integer and α>0\alpha>0 belongs to
ℚ⁡(d)\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d}), has norm 11 or −1-1, and
α≠1\alpha\neq 1 (squaring handles norm −1-1).


These statements concern restricted approximation sets. They do not
give an unrestricted irrationality exponent for a fixed higher power.
Similarly, in the small-point theorems the endpoint depends on the
desired error in the exponent: ∀ε​∃b0\forall\varepsilon\,\exists b_{0}
cannot be interchanged to infer exponent exactly two at one fixed
bb. The joint measures have constants uniform in the target’s
coefficients and rational approximant, but those constants remain
attached to the fixed evaluation point. The restricted-power theorem
and its displayed logarithmic applications are now proved in Lean:
see QuadraticPi/ActualRestrictedPowerExponent.lean and
QuadraticPi/RestrictedLogarithmicPowers.lean.
The preceding graded rank, small-point polynomial, and joint-measure applications now have checked endpoints relative to their explicit classical inputs. Appendix A identifies these boundaries and the exact literal recipes; it does not claim native proofs of the cited foundations.




Appendix A Exact formalization status and source map


A.1 Reviewed statement-level scope, 16 October 2026


Checkpoint136 reviews all 62 numbered theorem, lemma, proposition, and
corollary statements against their current Lean endpoints. The map below
contains 53 direct endpoint entries and nine entries complete as
applications relative to named classical theorem inputs. The latter are
entries 9, 16, 52, 55, 56, and 58–61. There is no remaining concrete
application-adapter gap identified by this review. This is a statement
about these numbered results and their recorded supporting interfaces;
it does not assert that every sentence of the written proofs has a
separate Lean translation, or that the cited classical theorems have
new native Lean proofs.


Earlier recovery ledgers used the label PARTIAL_FORMAL for
these nine classical boundaries even after their applications had been
assembled. This edition uses CITED_FOUNDATION_ENDPOINT so that
a complete conditional application is not confused with an unfinished
adapter. The historical ledgers are preserved. The count is not a
percentage of native proofs. The exact exported theorem types, rather
than a status label, determine the mathematical scope.


All mathematical statement and proof environments from the previous
114-page edition are preserved verbatim. Status prose is updated, and
explicit domain and continuation conventions are added immediately before
the multiplicity and rank statements. The previous source and PDF are
retained unchanged. The one new mathematical source module in this
checkpoint packages the already proved Gaussian Möbius irrationality
and exponent conclusions for the actual Gaussian base field.



A.2 Foundations supplied explicitly as theorem parameters


The following list contains twelve tracked interface names, not twelve
independent classical theorems. Several interfaces specialize the same
classical theory. They are explicit parameters in the application types,
not new global mathematical axioms. A successful #print axioms
report for a conditional theorem checks its proof from its hypotheses;
it does not supply proofs of those hypotheses.


Classical interface 1.


QuadraticPi.GradedHarmonicGrowth.SharpLcmAsymptotic.
The sharp exponential growth of lcm⁡(1,…,n)\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,n), in the standard form equivalent to the prime number theorem. This supplies the sharp arithmetic clearing rate; routes that do not use it are identified separately.



Classical interface 2.


QuadraticPi.GradedAlgebraicValueIndependence.AlgebraicLogTranscendence.
The classical Hermite–Lindemann nonzero algebraic-logarithm transcendence input. Polynomial-value and power irrationality are then derived in the application sources. The input is independent of the new approximation conclusions.



Classical interface 3.


QuadraticPi.GradedCitedSeparationBridge.CitedGenericSeparation.
The cited generic separation statement for the rational differential-system setting used here. Its application is made only after the actual lifted system and its branch and independence data have been supplied. The reference and interface matching are recorded in the saved rank review; see also [20].



Classical interface 4.


QuadraticPi.GradedGenericRankCitation.CitedGenericQuadraticRank.
The cited quadratic-family generic rank input, specialized with the actual graded system. Native adapters provide the ordinary point, independence data, clearing, contact, and uniformity information. The input is not the paper’s final short-jet or approximation conclusion.



Classical interface 5.


QuadraticPi.GradedTwoLogBakerApplication.CitedBakerLogarithmIndependence.
Baker’s linear independence theorem for logarithms of algebraic numbers [5, Chapters 1–2], in the precise two-logarithm application form. It is used in the graded joint measure, after the actual rational exponential arguments have been identified.



Classical interface 6.


QuadraticPi.GradedGeneralNilssonRankCitation.CitedGenericNilssonRank.
The cited general rational-system Nilsson rank result matched to the hypotheses recorded for [20, Theorems 3–4 and Lemma 1]. The actual finite lift and Nilsson-order data are exposed in the rank endpoint; they are not inferred merely from analyticity.



Classical interface 7.


QuadraticPi.GradedOrdinaryAnalyticExistenceCitation.CitedOrdinaryAnalyticFundamental.
The ordinary analytic fundamental-solution theorem on a simply connected pole-free domain. The precise source is [29, Section I.1.2 and Section I.2]. Transport to the original germ and the evaluation point is handled by checked adapters.



Classical interface 8.


QuadraticPi.GradedSimplyConnectedUnionCitation.CitedSimplyConnectedUnion.
The two-open-set simply-connected-union consequence of van Kampen, with nonempty path-connected intersection; see [30, Theorem 1.20 and its two-set discussion, pp.43–44]. This is a topological input, separate from the ODE theorem.



Classical interface 9.


QuadraticPi.SmoothLocalPolynomialCertificate.CitedRegularPointLocalEquations.
The local-equation form of smoothness at a regular complex affine point. The original defining ideal localizes to an ideal generated by the codimension-many equations with independent linear parts. This is the standard local complete-intersection consequence in [26, Tags 00TS and 00T5]; the proof of Tag 00T5 gives the localized generators and cotangent independence.



Classical interface 10.


QuadraticPi.ScalarMatchingInvariantConverse.CitedGelfondSchneider.
The classical Gel’fond–Schneider theorem, used for the converse matching-embedding classification. The invariant-field upper bound and its direct algebraic coordinate criterion do not need this converse input. See the transcendence discussion in [5].



Classical interface 11.


QuadraticPi.AffineGeneralityCitation.CitedRegularPrincipalOpen.
A prime complex affine component has a nonempty principal open of regular points of the expected local dimension. This is the principal-open specialization of dense-open regularity and the closed-point dimension theorem; see [26, Tags 0B8X and 00P0], and [27, Theorems 5.18, 5.23 and Corollary 5.25].



Classical interface 12.


QuadraticPi.AffineGeneralityCitation.CitedComplexPrincipalOpenDensity.
A nonempty principal open in an irreducible complex affine variety is dense in the ordinary complex topology. This follows from equality of ordinary and Zariski closures for constructible sets in [27, Proposition 15.2, p.197], which cites [28, Chapter I, Section 10, Corollary 1, p.60]. Restriction to the actual analytic open set is then proved in Lean.




A.3 Conventions checked at the application boundaries


The real exponent uses reduced rational denominators; the Gaussian
exponent uses the complex modulus of the denominator. They are distinct
approximation problems. The Gaussian Dirichlet lower-exponent proof needs
only its checked constant four. The smaller constant two in the written
auxiliary argument has not been substituted into that proof.


In moving-center interpolation the polynomial source cutoff is weak
(≤H\leq H), whereas the target coefficient cutoff is strict (<H<H).
Successive weight thresholds are chosen first, then a divisibility
modulus independent of the centers, and finally the height cutoff that
may depend on the centers. The bounded-fiber and distinct-center
adapters preserve this order. In two-logarithm determinant arguments,
one selected minor and its ordered columns are used at all embeddings.
The shear comparison does not choose a replacement minor.


In the transverse multiplicity statement, the original analytic domain
meets the component and both original normal bases are general on that
same domain. A canonical nontrivial filter is constructed from relatively
open dense subsets; only finite intersections are required, with no Baire
hypothesis. The geometric classical inputs contain neither the weighted
bound nor a supplied compatible point. The literal factor-two bound holds
for every positive NN, with no equation-dependent lower cutoff.


In the general rank statement the lift, contact and ordinary-point values
refer to a coherent continuation of the original solution germ. The
short-jet offset is chosen before the later contact and slope parameters
and before the polynomial family. In the graded application statements,
the integer endpoint recipes and both signs of the quadratic parameter
are included. For each exponent slack a cutoff is chosen; one cannot
exchange these quantifiers to deduce exponent exactly two at an arbitrary
fixed small point.


The joint polynomial height H+​(P)H_{+}(P) controls only the positive-degree
coefficients. The constant coefficient is absorbed into the rational
target numerator. The positive lower-bound constant is uniform over
P,p,QP,p,Q at the fixed endpoint; it may depend on that endpoint and the
specified parameters. Finally, the restricted power-class exponent
2/n2/n is not an ordinary unrestricted exponent of a higher power.

mathematical result numbers are obtained through the printed cross
references. Public names are fully qualified and are accompanied by their
source-module paths in the machine-readable correspondence file. Supporting
anchors from earlier checkpoints are retained in that file, including
those that are no longer the preferred public endpoint.

Review 52: Lemma 15.1


Checked application on explicit classical inputs.
The literal short-derivative endpoint is completed relative to cited separation, general Nilsson rank, ordinary analytic existence, and the simply-connected-union theorem. Values at rr are on one coherent continuation of the original solution germ. The finite lifted system, contact order, and short-jet budget are identified. The offset is chosen before σ,η,M,P\sigma,\eta,M,P, as required for uniformity; full rank is not a supplied endpoint hypothesis.

A.5 Compiler evidence and reproduction


The fixed compiler is official Lean 4.34.1, commit
5045d0056413266e57c625dcd7c365b10e377c52, with mathlib commit
d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612 and the nine recorded
dependency pins. The compiler, kernel, project trust settings, and bounded
execution configuration are unchanged. The maximum admitted compiler
process count remains two, with four threads per process.


Checkpoint135 built all inherited coverage-anchor modules plus the new
multiplicity modules: 336 targets and 6241 jobs, followed by 2135 printed
declaration types and axiom reports. Checkpoint136 builds the same source
coverage plus the final Gaussian adapter: 337 targets and 6242 jobs.
Its fresh audit checks 2145 declaration types and axiom reports, including
all previous coverage anchors, both new Gaussian declarations, and eight
previously proved supporting declarations used by this review. The only
axioms reported are propext, Classical.choice, and
Quot.sound. No new global mathematical axiom, admitted proof,
compiler modification, or native-decision shortcut is introduced.


The audit source is
anc/lean/validation/LogarithmicPaperStep136Audit_2026-10-16.lean.
The recorded build log, audit output, statement review, selected endpoint
source map, and complete target list are supplied in
anc/lean/validation/checkpoint136/. Readers start with
anc/REPRODUCTION.txt and anc/lean/README.txt.
The accompanying publication source snapshot is labelled
logarithms-v2-2026-10-20-Lean136; its file manifest records the
exact source hashes and the pinned public dependencies.


The mathematical edition and its statement review remain dated
October 16, 2026. The publication copy was prepared on October 19, 2026,
with filename-based font loading and an author-footnote disclosure of
ChatGPT assistance. A literature revision on October 20, 2026 added the
comparison with [23], revised the abstract and
contribution statements, and placed citations beside overlapping results.
The explicit manuscript date is retained and is distinguished from this
revision date and from any arXiv submission date.
All 62 numbered statement environments and all 55
proof environments are preserved verbatim from the reviewed edition.
The source distribution makes the local import closure available without
requiring the research recovery archives; the pinned public dependencies
are obtained separately. Its classical theorem inputs remain explicit.
The retained October 16 compiler evidence is distinguished from the
fresh bounded reproduction completed on October 19. The latter built the
337 original targets and four additional manuscript-source targets, and
checked the original 2145-declaration audit and a separate 51-declaration
audit, using the pinned dependency cache and a source-guarded project
cache. It was not a from-scratch rebuild of every dependency. The results
and logs are supplied in
anc/lean/validation/final-review-2026-10-19/.
The October 20 revision changes no Lean source or mathematical statement;
it verifies the unchanged proof-source bytes and refreshes the article
manifest without claiming another Lean compilation.

D. Mumford, The Red Book of Varieties and Schemes,
second expanded edition, Lecture Notes in Mathematics 1358,
Springer, 1999. Chapter I, Section 10, Corollary 1, p.60;
as cited in Milne’s Proposition 15.2.


      
[29]

D. Miličić, Lectures on Differential Equations in Complex
Domains, Winter 1989. Sections I.1.2 and I.2.
https://www.math.utah.edu/~milicic/Eprints/de.pdf.


      
[30]

A. Hatcher, Algebraic Topology, Cambridge University Press, 2002.
Theorem 1.20 and the two-set discussion, pp.43–44.
https://pi.math.cornell.edu/~hatcher/AT/AT.pdf.

    Instructions for reporting errors
    We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below:
    
      Click the "Report Issue" (
            
          ) button, located in the page header.
    
    Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
    Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all.
    Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.
